MEMORANDUM OF THE PRESS CONFERENCE, THURSDAY, AUGUST 25. 198
At the press conference this morning Acting Secretary
Castle announced the release of a telegram from President Hoover
to the President of Uruguay on the occasion of the anniversary
of tre independence of that country. The Acting Secretary also
said that Mr. McDermott had two or three telegrams from China

and cne from Paraguay wiich the correspondents might see and

Asked if he
Count Uchida's speech before the
894.00
Diet, the Acting Secretary replied in the affirmative. The
correspondent then asked if the Acting Secretary could comment
in any way on the speech. In reply, Mr. Castle said that there
was not much to say about the speech as there was really nothing
new in it. Mr. Castle added that he thought that Count Uchida
evidently addressed the specch to the League of Nations in an
effcrt to give his side of the case. There was no direct
reference to Secretary Stimson's speech of August 8 and Count
Uchida was apparently endeavoring to put the Japanese case
before the world as well as he could.
A correspondent here asked if Count Uchida's contention
SE.8 S
that the right of self-defense extends beyond the frontier
FLLED
was new. In reply, Mr. Castle said that Japan has said that
rignt along, although not so baldly. The correspondent then
observed that the Japanese have claimed that self-defense was
an exception under the Kellogg Pact and that they have con-
tended that self-defense extends beyond the frontier. Replying,
Mr. Cestle said that the question had never been brought up
except by the Japanese. NOT FOR ATTRIBUTION in eny way the
Acting

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Acting Secretary said that he thought it perfectly conceivable
that self-defense might make action beyond the frontier
necessary but that the technicalities of such a situation are
very difficult.
A correspondent then asked if the Acting Secretary con-
sidered Count Uchida's speech as a declaration to the League
of Nations of the intention to recognize Manchukuo, to continue
to use force and to hold what they have taken. Again, NOT FOR
ATTRIBUTION, Mr. Castle said that the Japanese would not admit
that the continued use of force has anything to do with the
present case, because they would claim that they were acting
for the new state of Manchukuo.
A correspondent then asked if the Department of State
takes the position that Japanese recognition of Manchukuo would
be a further violation of the Nine-Power Pact. For background
only and NOT FOR ATTRIBUTION in any way, the Acting Secretary
said that he had not studied the question sufficiently to
enable him to be sure in the matter but that he thought it
would be. The position taken would, of course, depend on the
decision whether Manchukuo came into existence through the
will of the people of Manchuria or whether it was forced into
existence by the Japanese. The correspondent then asked if
we had any doubt about the question. He was informed in reply
that we shall wait to hear what the League of Nations
Commission has to say concerning the case. Observing that the
report of the Commission of the League of Nations is apparently
available in Geneva, a correspondent asked if the Department of
State had received a copy of the report. In reply, Mr. Castle
said that he did not think it possible that the League of
Nations had received a copy of the report because the last we
heard was to the effect that the Commission was preparing
the

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
the report in China. It is doubtful whether the report could
reach Geneva before about the end of September. The corres
pondent then said that possibly the League had only a digest
of the Commission's report but that the opinion seems to be gen-
eral in Geneva that the report is very adverse to Japan.
Commenting thereon, Mr. Castle said that it is possible that
individuals in Geneva have heard from individuals on the
Commission on certain specific points, but that he doubted very
much if the report had been completed.
Another correspondent then voluntecred the information
that he had received a news despatch from Geneva to the effect
that the League of Nations had been notified, presumably by
cable, that the draft had been completed, but that the text
thereof had not been received in Geneva. The Acting Secretary
replied to the effect that he did not believe the report would
be cabled to the League but that it would either be sent by
mail or carried in person over Siberia. Asked if the Department
of State expected to receive a copy of the report before it is
made public, the Acting Secretary said that the Department had
not asked for a copy but that he presumed we would receive one
at the time it is given out in Geneva. asken then if the
Department had received a summary of the report comparable to
the one said to have been cabled to Geneva, the Acting Secre
tary replied in the negative.
A correspondent asked if it is likely that anything will.
be said officially by President Hoover or by Secretary Stimson
in reply to Count Uchida's speech. The Acting Secretary in
reply said that he did not think there was any chance that such
a stetement would be made by either the President or the
Secretary of State.
M. J. McDermott.

ADDRESS OF THE FOREIGN MINISTER, COUNT YASUYE UCHIDA,
BEFORE THE 63RD JAPANESE DIET ON THURSDAY, AUGUST 25, 1952
At the June session of the Diet my predecessor
took occasion to report upon and discuss the outstand-
ing foreign relations of this country. Today, in view
of the importance of the developments in Manchuria that
have taken place since then, I desire to review the
questions regarding Manchuria and China Proper and to
describe in some detail the policy of the Japanese Gov-
ernment.
We are all gratified to note that Mamchukuo has
entered upon a career of sturdy and healthy progress.
The Japanese Government are convinced that recognition
of this new State is the only means of stabilizing con-
ditions in Manchuria and of establishing conditions of
permanent peace in the Far East. And, it is with a
view to the early extension of formal recognition to
Manchukuo that we are making various arrangements,
upon the expected completion of which in the near fu-
ture, our plan will be carried through.
However, there are those in other countries
who do not fully comprehend the attitude of Japan toward
China nor the measures she has taken since the incident
of September 18 last year and who, in default of a cor-
-1

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
rect conception of the advent of Manchukuo, look upon
any recognition of Manchukuo by Japan as an illegitimato
procedure. In view of such misunderstanding as still
persists, I avail myself of this opportunity to clarify
Hapan's position and to expound to you the reasons why
the Goverment consider recognition of Manohukuo to be the
only means of solving the Manchurian problem although in
doing so I may have to repeat what has already been set
forth in successive declarations and statements issued
in the past by the Japanese Government.
It is an indisputable fact that the chaotic con-
dition of China and the so-called revolutionary polioy
carried on by China under the enthralling influence of
extravagant political dogma have been principally re-
sponsible for the unfortunate turn that international
relations have taken of recent years in the Far East.
Although Japan has been the chief vietim of the abnormal
state of affairs in China, other countries have also
suffered intolerable indignities and incalculable ma-
terial loss. At the same time it is admitted by those
conversant with actual conditions in China that no remedy
can be effected by having recourse either to the Covenant
of the League of Nations or to any other organ of what
may be termed "machinery of peace". In fact it has been
the practice of the Powers, as has been demonstrated on
-2

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
innumerable occasions, to repair or prevent injuries to
their important rights and interests in China by direet
application of force without relying upon those instru-
ments of peace.
For over twenty years Japan continued to exercise
the greatest patience and moderation in the hope that
some day China might soberly undertake the task of re
habilitating her fortunes and playing her proper role
in the maintenance of peace in the Far East. China
failed however to show any sincere desire to reciprocate
our good will and kindly sentiments but increased more
than ever in her arrogance and intolerance. Our Govern-
ment took pains time and again to point out to China the
danger she was running in trying too far the patience
of the Japanese people. But China did not heed our
warnings. The incident of September 18 occurred in
Manchuria, the very region regarded as the first bul-
wark of Japan, at the precise moment when the feeling
of our people had been wrought up to the highest pitch
by repeated provocations. We had no alternative other
than to resort to a measure of self defense.
There are those who argue as though the action
of Japan were a violation of the Kellogg-Briand Anti-
War Pact. But such contention has no foundation in fact.
As I have stated, Japan has been foroed to adopt neces-
-3-

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
sary measures for the prevention of wanton attacks upon
important righ ts and interests vital to her national
existence. The Anti-War Pact does not put restraint
upon the exercise of the right of self defense in such
a case.
The pact does not prohibit a signatory power
from taking at its own discretion whatever steps it
finds imperative in order to remove immediate menaces
to its territory and its rights and interests of whatever
kind. And obviously the exercise of the right of self
defense may extend beyond the territory of the Power
which exercises that right. Japan's action is essen-
tially identical with the action that other Powers have
taken elsewhere in similar circumstances.
Following upon this action legitimately taken by
Japan in the face of Chinese attacks, officials of the
regime presided over by Chang Hsuehliang ei ther fled or
resigned as you know with the result of a practical ex-
tinction of that regime. In the meantime among the lead-
ing people of Manchuria who had long resented the
tyranny of the Changs and were opposed to plunging their
land into the civil turmoil of China Proper, a project for
political reform mas already under way. It 1s in every
way natural that these leaders should have taken as they
did advantage of the opportunity afforded by the downfall
of Chang to launch out upon an active movement. " peace
4

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
maintenance committee" was accordingly organized at
Mukden, Harbin and other centres. In view of our own
responsibility in respect of the maintenance of peace
and order in Manchuria we extended necessary coopera-
tion to these committees. It was the Manchurien lead
ers connected with these committees who decided that
the hour had struck for founding the new State. The
establishment of Manchukuo was the outcome of a separat-
ist movement having for its background the geographic
and historical peculiarities of Manchuria as well as
the psychological characteristics of the Manchurian
people.
Those who seek to place upon Japan the re-
sponsibility for the Manchurian revolution by trac-
ing the independence of Manchukuo directly to our mili-
tary operations, simply labour under ignorance of the
facts and their opinions altogether miss the point.
Again, as regards those who fanoy they detect a seoret
connection of some sort on the part of Japan with the
foundation of the new State basing their suspicions
on the fact that there are in fact a number of Japanese
in the employ of the Manchukuo Government, I need only
point to the existence of many precedents for the en-
listment by the young Government or newly founded State
of the services of foreigners. Our own Government since
-5

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.97)
the Meiji restoration have employed many foreigners as
advisers or as regular officials; their number for
instance in the year 1875 or thereabout exceeded five
hundred. Those who misconstrue the presence of Japanese
1n the Manchukuo Government in the fashion alluded to
are placing the responsibility where it does not belong.
Manchukuo has come into being as I have already
remarked as a result of separatist movements within China
herself. Consequently the view expressed in certain quar-
ters that recognition of the new State thus created would
constitute a violation of the stipulations of the Nine
Power Treaty is in my opinion incomprehensible. The
Nine Power Treaty does not forbid all separatist move-
ments in China or debar Chinese in any part of the coun-
少，
try from setting up on their free will an independent
State. Hence should Japan extend recognition to the
existing Government of Manchukuo founded by the will
of the people of Manchuria she would not thereby as
a signatory Power to the Nine Power Treaty violate in
any way the stipulations of that treaty. of course
it would be a different matter on the assumption that
Japan was seeking to annex Mnachuria or otherwise sat-
isfy her thirst for land. Only I hardly need to waste
words in once more disclaiming at this juncture any
territorial designs on our part in Manchuria or any-
-6

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
where else.
Thus far I have elucidated the theses that the
attitude maintained by Japan toward China and especial-
ly the measures we have taken since the incident of
September 18 have been just and proper, that the in-
dependence of Manchukuo has been achieved through the
spontaneous will of Manchurians and should be rogerded
as a consequence of a fissiparous movement in China,
and that recognition by Japan of the new State thus
created can not violate the stipulations of the Nine
Power Treaty. Now let me proceed further and dwell
upon the reasons why the Japanese Government consid-
er the recognition of Manchukuo as the sole effective
means of solving the Manchurian problem.
With regard to the question of finding a solu-
tion for the Manchurian problem, the Japanese Govern-
ment attach the greatest importance to the following
two points: - First, that, in seeking a satisfactory
solution we should a im at the fulfillment of the legi-
timate aspirations of the Manchurian people - at
adequa te guarantees for the ri ghts and interests of
Japan, • at prevention, in order to make Manchuria a
safe place to live in alike for Manchurians and for-
eigners, of any recrudescence of erstwhile anti-foreign
policy and movements and finally at bringing not only
-7

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.99)
stability to Manchuria but permanent peace to the Par
Bast; - Secondly that such solution should be effeeted
by rejecting all sentimental propositions and abstract
theories and arrived at upon the solid basis of realities
of the situation.
In view of the ciroumstances that
led to the outbreak of last September and of the immense
sacrifices our country has been compelled to make in
the past the Japanese Government feel the imperious need
of arriving at a fundamental solution of the Manchurian
problem on the lines of these two propositions and of
eliminating for all time the causes of a secular Sino-
Japanese confliot.
On the other hand, it appears that in certain
quarters a plan is being considered to reach a solution
by patching up matters for the moment by investing China
Proper in one form or another with authority over Man-
churia. That such a plan would only serve to repro-
duce the situation preceding the incident of September
18 is only too plain to us who have been taught by bi tter
experience in the past. The people of Japan can never
consent to a solution of that kind.  Moreover, it is as
clear as day that the investment of China Proper with
power over Manchuria under whatever guise is totally
irreconcilable with the political creed enunciated in
the declaration of independence and other publio state-
-8

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
ments of the Men chukuo Government and therefore that
the scheme in question has no chance of being accepted
by Manchurians. The imposition upon Manchuria of what
is not wanted by Manchurians would be as unjust as ill-
advised in that it would only end by sowing seeds of
future trouble in that land.
The plan to invest China Proper with authority over
Manchuria and all measures of a similar temporizing kind
will never fulfil the object of making Menchuria a safe
and happy land for both natives and foreigners nor do
they accord with our principle of finding a solution
upon the basis of the realities of the situation. Cer-
tainly it is not the way in which to bring stability
to Manchuria or permanent peace to the Far East.
Manchukuo has set out upon an honest programme
that is open and aboveboard. It has declared its inten-
tion to pursue a policy of justice, peace and friend-
ship toward other countries, to take over obligations
of existing treaties according to international law and
usage, to respect vested rights and interests of foreign-
ers and to protect their lives and property, to welcome
all aliens and to a ccord them equal and equitable treat-
ment irrespective of their racial origin, to adhere to the
principle of the open door, to promote international
trade and to contribute to the economio development of
-9-

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
the world; and Machukuo's sincerity of purpose has been
definitely established. It is plain therefore that to
extend to Manchukuo formal recognition and assist its
Government to carry on their sound policy above referred
to will be a notable step toward making Manchuria a happy
and peaceful land for natives and foreigners alike on the
basis of the realities of the situation. And it is
plain, too, that such is the only way to secure a perma-
nent solution of the Manchurian problem.
As long as Manchukuo retains the sterling spirit
in which it was founded and perseveres in its high pur-
pose it is assured of a future full of promise. Here
and there we find persons entertaining exaggerated fear
of the perils of banditry in Manchuria, of making gloomy
forecast regarding the finances of the country. But
we refuse to join the company of these pessimists. Up-
heavals brought about by lawless elements are a univer-
sal phenomena to be observed in newly established states
anywhere in the world. Whereas in most of such cases
it takes some considerable time to put unrest down,
1t 1s only fair to say that in Manchuria the suppres-
sion of banditry is making comparatively rapid strides.
As for the financial status of Manchukuo I have been
told that it is good, really far better than was anti-
cipated at first by the Manchurian authorities. In view
-10

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
of the vast territory, the thriving population and immense
natural resources in its possession, there can be no
doubt that Manchukuo, given a good government, will emerge
as a rich and prosperous country and come to be a great
market for the world. It is my hope that the healthy
development of Manchukuo will not only bring happiness and
prosperity to the thirty million inhabitants of the coun-
try but will serve as a good example for the rehabilita-
tion of China Proper.
As regards the conditions prevailing in China
Proper I regret to say that the growing disturbance in
her domestic administration, coupled with rampant ac-
tivities of communist bandits throughout an extensive
area in the Yangtze Valley and South China, 1s casting
a gloomy shadow on the path of the National Government.
Besides as an anti-foreign, especially an anti-Japanese,
movement still continues unabated, further complications
are likely to arise in the foreign relations of China,
which in turn will make her internal confusion worse
confounded. Truly, our deep sympathy 1s due the Chinese
people, who have to suffer under these troubled condi-
tions. To allow such conditions to persist as they are,
I am firmly convinced, will not only be deplorable for
China herself but will constitute a constant danger of
bringing about a serious menace to the rights and inter-
-11

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
ests of foreign Powers. On the contrary, the whole
world will re joice, should China realize the gravity of
the situation, cast aside her ill-advised foreign policy
and adopt in earnest a constructive programme of devo-
tion to the task of internal readjustment. It goes
without saying that Japan, always conscious of the gen-
eral interests of the Far East, will spare no effort to
afford all possible assistance to China. Indeed it is
my fervent hope that the day is not far distant when
Japan, Manchukuo and China, as three independent Powers
closely linked together by a bond of cultural and racial
affinities, will come to cooperate hand in hand for the
maintena 9 and advancement of the peace and prosperity
of the Far East as well as for the peace of the world
and the civilization of mankind.
-12-

Council on Foreign Relations savors distinctly of a tempest
in a teapot if not of a guilty conscience on the part of the
894.0
Japanese, for we now understand that the speech was merely
an academic discussion of a hypothetical case, while the
Japanese took it as a specific charge of guilt. Unfortun-
1174
ately I was unable to take any steps to mitigate the effect
2
here, because neither the text of the speech nor a resumé of
its substance and intentions have reached me, and by the time
the text arrives from Shanghai the incident will presumably be
closed. However that may be, the Foreign Office has used the
speech deliberately to pour fuel on the temporarily quiescent
flames of public animosity against the United States. I say
deliberately, because the violent Japanese press reaction was
based not on the press despatches from the United States but on
the Foreign Office's inflammatory interpretation of Debuchi's
cabled account, and this interpretation was given to the Japanese
press a day before it was released to the foreign correspondents.

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
This situation reminds me strongly of the efforts of
the German Government, by calumniating foreign nations, to
build up a public war psychology in l9l4, the effort being
repeated whenever some new venture, such as the indiscrim-
inate submarine warfare, was about to be launched.
Here in
Japan the deliberate building up of public animosity against
foreign nations in general and the United States in partioular
has doubtless a similar purpose - to strengthen the hand of
the military in its Manchurian venture in the face of foreign,
and especially American, opposition. I believe that on the
part of the Japanese it is a sign of weakness, not of strength.
The internal economic and financial situation in Japan is ser-
ious and may become desperate.
The plight of the farmers is
very bad, many industries are at low ebb, unemployment is
steadily increasing.
The yen is falling and prices have not
yet risen proportionately.
.Money cannot be obtained from
abroad; I was recently told, aIthough I cannot vouch for the
reliability of the information, that the Government had tried
without success to obtain loans from England, France and Holland
in turn. It will become increasingly difficult to obtain
domestic loans.
This situation is not critical, but it may
become so when the ability of the National Bank of Japan to
absorb domestic bonds comes to an end. Meanwhile millions
of yen are being squandered to support the Manchurian venture,

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3 -
of which the eventual economic advantage is highly proble-
matical, and when the full purport of these expenses becomes
known to the people, in their own serious deprivation, there
is no telling what effect it will create. I believe that a
steadily increasing anxiety exists among the Government and
the thinking men of the country outside of the hot-headed
military clique which refuses to face these facts. It seems
to be primarily this military element - vocalized by such men
as Shiratori - who believe that the best way to obscure these
facts is to work the public into a patriotic and nationalistic
fervor by representing foreign nations, particularly the
United States, as trying to thwart Japan's efforts for alleged
self-preservation.
Such a national temper is always dangerous. The German
military machine, supported by a carefully nurtured publie
war psychology, took the bit in its teeth and overrode all
restraining influences in 19l4. The Japanese military
machine is not dissimilar. It has been built for war, feels
prepared for war and would welcome war. It has never yet
been beaten and possesses unlimited self confidence. I am
not an alarmist but I believe that we should have our eyes
open to all possible future contingencies. The facts of
history would render it criminal to close them.
In this connection the enclosed memorandum prepared by
the Embassy, which will be incorporated in a despatch, may

be found significant.
Respectfully yours,
Nooth. Frew

Memorandum on Japanese Army.

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
MEMORANDUM
The following factors indicate that the Japanese Amy
is making extraordinary preparations for war:
1. Material Preparations:
According to the Military Attaché's office, the
Army Arsenals are working full time (as far as can be
ascertained); the airplane factories are very active -
more active than they have ever been; the Navy Arsenal
at Yokosuka is working day and night; the Army has entire
ly replaced the material taken from the war reserves and
used in the Manchurian and Shanghai campaigns, and war
reserves are believed to be now above normal; the Army
has bought around 500 motor-cars of various types and
makes since last September; extraordinarily large pur-
chases of American machine tools have been made for the
airplane works and arsenals; tanks and armoured cars are
being built as rapidly as possible; a chemical factory
was hurriedly built in Meguro (outside Tokyo) for materials
for chemical warfare end is now under military guard; there
has been a very large increase during the last six months
in the imports of raw materials usable in war, such as
special steels, glycerine, oils, raw cotton, wool, ores,
etc.; the people of the cities are being urged to buy and
man anti-aircraft equipment; anti-aircraft practice goes
on daily in Tokyo; the manoeuvres this year are exception-
al in their number and variety, emphasis being placed on
artillery manoeuvres (the Shanghaf affair demonstrated a
weakness in this line); the War Department is asking for
an additional appropriation of Yen 43,000,000, ostensibly
for unemployment relief but actually for the purpose of
giving contracts for equipment to private concerns and
thus bringing their continuing projects for the mechani-
zation of the Army up to date.
Psychological Preparations:
Numerous articles tending to incite war fever in
the people have been published, but because of the prom-
inence of the writer, excerpts are quoted from an article
written by General Araki, Minister for War, first published
in the KAISHOSHA (an Army officers' magazine) and trans-
lated in the JAPAN ADVERTISKR of July 26 and 27, 1932:
"There is no doubt that much courage is needed
to deal satisfactorily with the present situation.

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
"We must start with the consciousness that
we are Japanese and no other.
"...impress upon the world their (the Japanese)
importance as a nation.
"The Imperial Principle of the Japanese nation
...is...a thing that must be propagated over the
seven seas and extended over the five continents.
Anything that may hinder its progress must be done
away with even by the use of force.
"We of the Japanese nation ....should take
the whole situation (the disturbed condition of
all Asia) into our own hands. We must be active,
even expending the last portion of our national
strength.
"The countries of Eastern Asia are made objects
of oppression by the white peoples. This is an un-
deniable fact and Imperial Japan should no longer
let their impudence go unpunished.
When the Commission of Inquiry of the League of
Nations visited Tokyo in July, 1932, the Japanese press
published concerted and inspired articles affirming Japan's
determination to pursue its "fixed policy". This was
partly for the benefit of the Commission, but also partly
to inspire the people to defy the League if necessary.
The Foreign Office spokesman, Mr. T. Shiratori, released
to the Japanese press on August 9th an entirely unealled-
for, inaccurate and provocative interpretation of the
speech of the Secretary of State before the Council for
Foreign Relations. This was obviously released for the
purpose of arousing nationalistic and/ or anti-American
feeling.
The people throughout Japan (even school children)
are being urged to subscribe to funds for purchasing and
presenting to the Army "patriotism" airplanes, tanks,
passenger motor-cars, armoured motor-cars, and anti-
aircraft equipment. This is partly for the purpose of
conserving Army funds and partly to encourage war-fever.
3. Financial Preparations:
A bill was rushed through the last session of the
Diet, enabling the Bank of Japan to increase its fiduciary

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)
一3-
issue of currency (which is based on Govermment bonds
and certain commercial paper) by Yen 880,000,000.
The bank can now, therefore, take up Government bends
to the additional amount of Yen 880,000,000. This
was done partly to provide funds for the Army's ac-
tivities on the mainland, and partly to provide funds
for agrarian relief. The measure will provide suffi-
cient funds for the present fiscal year, at the present
rate of expenditure.
4。
Political Preparations:
Through the murder of Premier Inukai on May 15,
1932, the political power was taken out of the hands
of the professional politicians and placed under the
control of the military group. At the same time the
Diet was thoroughly cowed.The only remaining opposing
element consists of the advisers to the Throne, Count
Makino, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, and Dr. Ikki,
Minister of the Imperial Household Department, both of
whom are conservatives and inclined to be pacifists.
Count Makino was threatened on May 15th when Inukai was
murdered, and it is believed that further attempts are
now being made to force him and Dr. Ikki out of office.
The above observations all indicate war-like prepara-
tions. On the other hand, when we look for indications of
a peace-seeking or conciliatory nature, we find nothing
tangible. There is, as stated by Shiratori, the Foreign
Office spokesman, in discussing President Hoover's disarma-
ment proposal, no popular sentiment in Japan for disarmament.
There is certainly no articulate movement looking toward
compromise of the Manchurian question. There is no indica-
tion of any tendency to listen to conciliatory advice from
the League or any other quarter.

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
The purpose of the strengthening of the war machine
and the building up of the war psychology is uncertain.
It may be simply to present a united, armed front to the
world in case the decision of the League goes against Japan,
or to be prepared for eventualities should China or Soviet
Russia declare war. On the other hand, it may be that the
aggressive Army group has a definite war in mird. The
Army evidently plans a drive into Jehol, partly to cut
Chang Hsueh Liang's communications with the retel forces
in Manchuria and partly, perhaps, to cut communications
between China and the Chinese Eastern and Trans-Siberian
railway, in order that China may not be supplied from
Soviet Russia in case of a Chinese-Russian combination
against Japan.
Probably, however, the preparations are being made
more for the purpose of being ready for possible eventual-
ities than for a definite war, if the present or enlarged
operations in Manchuria are not considered as a foreign
war.

Tokyo's despatch No. 130 of
September 20, 1932, reports that the
new party (Kokumin Domei) of Adachi,
former Home Minister who caused the
downfall of the Wakatsuki Cabinet last
December, published its platform and
polioy on September 12. Although the
legal steps necessary for formal
official recognition as a party have
not yet been taken, it has been
announoed that they will be taken
before the Diet again convenes in
December. This party was a strong
factor in the recent extraordinary
session of the Diet. The press com-
mented favorably on the quality of the
speeches made by several of its
members. The name of the new party is
officially translated as the "National
League" although the Embassy believes
that "Peoples League" would be a more
satisfactory translation in view of
the party's principles. The platform
and principles need not be read as they
probably have as Nttle signifioance as
the platforms and principles of the
major parties.

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.96)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, September 20, 1932.
No. 130.
SUBJECT:
The Kokumin Domei,
A new Political Organization.
DIV
894.00/439
Copy in 7E
2.0
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
794.0
X
OCT 11 1932
Department of State
OCT 1 812
The Honorable
FILD
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
Reference has been made in several of the Embassy's
recent despatches to a new party formed by Mr. Kenzo Adaghi.
It will be remembered that the chief cause contributing
to the downfall of the Wakatsuki Ministry last December was
the resignation of Mr. Adachi from the Minseito and from
the portfolio of Home Affairs. The Government collapsed and
was succeeded by the ill-fated Inukai Ministry.
During the nine months which have elapsed since his vio-
lent separation from the Minseito, of which he was one of the
ieaders, Mr. Adachi has spent his time in fairly successful
attempts

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
attempts to mould a new party with himself as its chief.
Recently, on September l2th, this organization made a publio
declaration, constructed a platform of three fundamental
planks, and outlined a thirteen-point policy based on the
1/2/3/.
principles of the platform.  There are enclosed translations
of these three documents.
This new organization is called the Kokumin Domei, of-
ficially translated as the "National League", although the
Embassy believes that "Peoples League" would be a more fitting
translation, in view of the party's declared principles.
Although the legal steps necessary to formal official
recognition as a party have not yet been taken, it has been
announced that they will be taken before the Diet again con-
venes in December, so that Lir. Adachi will be at the head of
an actual political party rather than a le ague.
Notwithstanding its unofficial standing, the Kokumin
Domei was a strong factor in the recent extrar dinary session
of the Diet. It claimed the allegiance of thirty members of
the Lower House, mostly recruited from the ranks of the Min-
seito, and took part in interpellations and debates on an equal
footing. The vernacular press in a number of instances com-
mented favorably on the quality of the speeches made by several
of its members, and took occasion to point out that with one
exception it was almost the only organization which had any
really constructive measures to oppose to those presented by
the Government. This of course it could do with impunity, for
it had no part in the coalition Saito Ministry which contains
both Seiyukai and Minseito elements.
It is worthy of note that with the desertion of these
thirty

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
thirty men from the Minseito, the Seiyukai has an absolute
majority in the Lower House, which ill be felt much more
strongly in the next session than in the last extraordinary
session, which was convened to pass relief measures only.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

1. Declaration of the Kokumin Domei
2. Platform of the Kokumin Domei
3. Policy of the Kokumin Domei

From American Embassy
Tokyo.
DECLARATION OF THE KOKUMIN DOMEI
To develop the spirit of the founding of the Japanese
Empire; to prosecute the principle of international justice;
externally, to determine the standard of an honorable and
lasting peace; internally, to consolidate the economic system
under the control of the state and to create a new society
based on the principles of justice and freedom from extortion; - -
all these form the aims and objects of the Kokumin Domei.
The creed of the members of the Kokumin Domei is that the
Empire of Japan does not tolerate any international injustice
or unreasonableness to menace its national expansion and develop-
ment; that the Japanese people shall not have their livelihood
threatened by any social injustice; that the growth of the na-
tional strength and wealth of a state invariably contributes to
the advancement of civilization and the happiness of all the
nations; and that in a country or society enjoying true prosperi-
ty not a single law-abiding citizen should be left in a starving
condition.
The Kokumin Domei has been organized to accomplish the
aforementioned tasks, and we, the members, expect to attain
those objects by all means in our power with the ultimate aim
of realizing the establishment of a true national or popular
government. We appeal to our compatriots throughout the length
and breadth of the land to lend a helping hand in achieving our
avowed object.
(signed by all members)

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)
ioure No.2
patoh No.
130
om American Embassy
Tokyo.
PLATFORM
1.
We s trive to clarify and abide by the spirit of
the founding of this Empire, and expect to reestablish the
principle of international justice.
2.
We expect to realize the principle of the solidifica-
tion and unification of the national economic system under
the control of the state, guaranteeing a living to the
masses.
3.
We expect to root out and remove the accumulating
evils in the political system, and to realize the establish-
ment of a perfect people's government.

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
:ure No.
130
 No.
:m merican Embasy
Tokyo.
PROVISIONAL OUTLINE OF POLICY
1.
Measures aimed at the purification of national politics
based on the reform of the election system, including a reali-
zation of public elections in the true sense of the term and
minimizing the expenses to be borne by the candidates; also
measures making the post of Minister of Justice independent
of the Cabinet,and attaching judicial police officers to publio
procurators' offices in order to exercise rigorous control
over elections and to punish severely corrupt officials who
interfere in elections.
2.
Independence of the Department of Justice. In order to
assure the independence of the judicial right and to maintain
its uprightness the Department of Justice shall be made an
independent executive organ outside the Government, by which
means the evil of the prosecuting organ being controlled by
the Government may be removed.
3.
The control by the State of the major industries and
the monetary circulation institutions. As the central organ
for the State control of industries and money circulation a
central economic control department shall be established. As
a beginning the establishment of a control bank, and the nation-
alizationof insurance shall be carried out.
4.
The accommodation of low interest loans.
5.
The control and regulations of the price of rice, fer-
tilizers, and silk worm eggs, and also of the export of silk
yarn.
6.
The adjustment of monetary liabilities.
7.
The reestablishment of the maritime policy.
8.
The creation of a Japan-Manchukuo economio block. A
thoroughgoing exhaustive investigation into the economio re-
sources, market, labor, industrial possibilities, capital,

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
population, traffic and communication in Manchuria shall be
taken in hand as speedily as possible, on the basis of which
shall be formed a fundamental policy tending to contribute. to
the co-existence and co-prosperity of Manchuria and Japan. The
policy thus formulated to be put into execution as soon as pos-
sible.
9.
A fundamental reform of the educational system including
improvements in educational methods as well as the development
and advancement of learning.
10.
The creation of a central administrative organ as a
department of the Government to be named the Department of
Social Health, its function being to attend to and supervise
all affairs relating to public health, sanitation, the preven-
tion and relief of unemployment, the building of residential
houses by the State and other measures designed to contribute
to an assured livelihood for the people. The Department is
intended to be the instrument for the satisfactory operation
of social policies and the perfection and extension of various
social welfare projects.
11.
A reform of the taxation system.
12.
A crystallization of the policy toward Chosen and Taiwan.
13.
The cooperation of armaments and diplomacy. Armaments
and diplomacy are inseparably related, and expansion or reduction
of armaments cannot be satisfactorily carried out unless conduct-
ed in concert with diplomatic and administrative policies.

Tokyo's despatch No. 165 of
Ootober 21, 1932, reports the growth
of moderate tendencies in Japan.
Until September of this year there
have been constant intimations of an
inartioulate underourrent of opposi-
tion to the military's adventures on
the continent and to the military's
control of the administration at home.
Sinoe September there has been more
definite evidence of the growth of
moderate tendencies, for example, the
failure of the various efforts of the
military to oust Count Makino, Lord
Keeper of the Privy Counoil, from
office, and the decision to return
Mr. Debuchi to Washington instead of
sending a more aggressive type of
diplomat which it is understood the
military desired. The Ambassador has
been repeatedly informed during the
past few weeks by highly-placed Japa-
nese that the moderate elements are
steadily gaining strength and that
they are sure that "everything will
turn out all right", although none of
them is willing to indicate the
probable

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
probable course which the moderate
element would take in the Sino-
Japanese controversy.
The first public evidence of this
growth in strength of more moderate
influences was an editorial in the
JAPAN TIMES of Ootober 8 which struck
a new note in Japanese comment on the
controversy, which is summarized in
the last paragraph on page 3. On
October 18 the TOKYO JIJI reported a
rumor that the Minseito is considering
a plan to displace the Prime Minister
with General Ugaki, Governor General
of Chosen and ranking army officer, to
curb the activities of General Araki,
because "so long as Lieutenant-General
Araki holds office as Minister of War
it will not only be diffioult for
Japan to improve her relations with
other Powers but Japan will be driven
to the verge of bankruptoy". This is
probably a trial balloon but it is an
indioation that there is a body of
opinion in favor of ourbing the
military. The courage required to
publish such a rumor indicates a
possible weakening of military

SECRETARY'S OFFICE
Sir:
Since the outbreak of the anchurian affair in Septem-
ber, 19l, it las een generally known that there exists
in Japan a considerable body of opinion which is opposed
to the drastic actions of the military party. This body
is composed of the more conservative elements of the popula-
tion (retired statesmen, diploats, college professors, et
cetera) who deprecate the alleged violations of Japan's in-
ternational obligations and the impending loss by Japan of
the high position which she has gained in the family ofgations,
FILED
a
K

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and the extreme left group of the proletarians, who are op-
posed to all forms of imperialism. Occasional and faint
protests against the actions of the Japanese Army were heard
from these croups in the early days of the Sino-Japanese
controversy, but they were promptly and effectively suppressed
by the military. Dr. Inazo Mitobe was an exponent of the
first group. In a speech made shortly after the outbreak
of the trouble, he is reported to have said that the mili-
tarists were as great a danger to the steto as the communists.
For this he was hounded and threatened by the ultra-national-
ist groups until he fled the country and went to the United
States, wiere, according to press reports, he is now speak-
ing strongly in favor of Japan's actions in Manchuria -
possibly with a view to returning to the good graces of the
military  arty. An example of the second group consisted of
a small party of proletarians, who, about a month after the
outbreak of the Manchurian affair, began to march around
Tokyo wit! banners protesting against imperialism. They were
promptly arrested.
After a few incidents of this character, criticism of
the Army's actions was effectively stifled, permitting the
Army to proclaim to the world that Japanese opinion was un-
animous in its support of the military program.. The military
clique effectually controlled the press of the entire country.
Despite this assertion, however, the Embassy had received
constant intimations that there was an undercurrent, whose
strength could not be estimated because it was inarticulate,
of opposition to the militar: adventures on the Asiatic con-
tinent and to the military control of the administration at
home.
Since

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Since September, 1932, however, there has been more
definite evidence of the growth of moderate tendencies in
Japan. The continuance in office of Count Makino, Lord
Keeper of t e Privy Seal, despite various efforts of the
military party to oust him, is significant, as is the de-
cision to return r. Debuchi, the Japanese Ambassador to the
United States, to  is post in Washington, instead of sending
a more a ressive type of iiplomat which it is understood
the military party desired.
I have been repeatedly infcrmed, during the past few
Weeks, by Japanese who stand high in the councils of the
nation, that the oderate elements are steadily gaining
strength and that they are sure that "everything will turn
out all right", alth ugh none of them is wi lling to indicate
the probable course which the moderate element would take in
the Sino-Japanese controversy. One retired Japanese diploma-
tic official stated, however, that he believed that there would
be a return to the "Shidehara diplonacy".
The fi st public evidence of the growth in strength of
more moderate influences, however, consisted of an editorial
in the JAPA TIIES AD  AIL of Cctober 8, 1932, which, while
not directly criticizing the military group, certainly struck
a new note in Japanese conment on the controversy. Inter alia,
the editorial declared that "Chief among the failures of many
Japanese has been an inability to appraise correctly the
newer psychology which is being evidenced among the le ading
Western powers. . . . By the newer psychology we mean the
ideas and idealism now abroad in the West in regard to war and
peace and international relations . . . . not totake them in-
to consideration is for Japan to butt her head agai nst the
stone wall of world opinion . . . . Nor can Japan claim that
she

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
she has not had warning.... the nation insisted upon
sticking its head in the sand like the proverbial ostrich".
The TI ES AND MAIL was not suppressed for publishing this
unusually outspoken editorial.
On October 18, 1932, the TOKYO JIJI published a short
item to the effect that rumor had it that the Minseito(the
principal minority p rty) is considering a plan to displace
Premier Viscount Saito and to place General Ugaki, the pre-
sent Governor General of Chosen, in the position of Premier.
This is planned in order that General Ugaki, the ranking
Army officer, may curb the activities of Lieutenant-General
Araki, because the Minseito fears that "so long as Lieutenant-
General Araki holds office as Minister of War it will not
only be diffi cult for Japan to i..prove her relations with
other Powers but Japan will be driven to the verge of bank-
ruptcy",
This rumor is  robably in the nature of a trial
balloon, flown by the Minseito in an attempt to find an issue
upon which the party can return to power, and may or may not
d velop into an actual program. The point is, hovever, that
it is an indication tha: there is a body of opinion in favor
of curbing the military.
Moreover, the mere fact that a
newspaper dared to puplish such a rumor indicates a possible
weakening of military influence and a growth in moderate
tendencies.
As a further indication of the growth of moderate
1/
t ndencies in Japan, I enclose herewith a copy of a memorandum
submitted by Diplomatic Secretary H. M. Benninghoff, con-
cerning a conversation which his father, who has lived in
Japan for meny years, had with one of his former pupils.
The

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
The conversation well illustrates the moderate
tendencies among the more intelligent groups in
the population.
It can be taken for granted that the military
party will not relinquish its present control of the
Government without a struggle, as a political defeat
now would probably mean that the Army would be permanently
relegated to a secondary position of influence in Japan.
The strug le will probably take place in the next Diet
session, over the Army's demands for larger appropriations.
Indeed, the excessive budgetary demands of the Army for
prosecuting the Manchurian campaign and for carrying out
the military defense program may well be the rock upon
which the military party will finally come to grief.
Some observers believe that the military will meet the
crisis by bringing on a war with Soviet Russia, or by
endeavoring to detach North China from the Re ublic of
China, in an attempt to maintain the war-fever in Japan
and thereby assure their continuance in power. At all
events, they must save face, and if they should find
their authority passing , they- the younger and more
fanatical and hotheaded elements of the Army and Navy-
would in my opinion be capable of plunging the country
into any kind of disaster in an effort to maintain their
hold on the direction of national affairs.
The foregoing comnent is necessarily of an indefinite
and somewhat nebulous character. There is certainly
not

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
not sufficient concrete evidence at the present time to
justify an assertion that the military clique is losing
or will eventually lose its grip, or that a return to
the so-called "Shidehara diplomacy" is yet in sight. Even
assuming that the moderate element should gain control of
the Government within the not far distant future, it is
not at present seen in precisely what manner such a develop-
ment could materially affect the situation in Manchuria.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

MEMO RANDUM.
Tokyo.
My father, Rev. H. B. Benninghoff, who is a
Baptist Missionary in Tokyo and who has been connected
with Waseda University for about twenty-five years,
had a conversation the other day with one of his former
students who is now on the staff of the Chuo Koron, a
general monthly magazine of good repute something like
the Atlantic Monthly or Scribners.
This men shoved my father a nunber of articles
attacking in greater or less degree the Manchurian and
Chinese po icies of the Government. He also said that
the magazine, and presumably others as well, was almost
swamped by voluntary contributions of this kind. The
articles, of course, vary in merit, but most of them are
written b: men of prominence and considerable education.
These articles, of course, the magazine cannot print,
chiefly because the police would prohibit the sale of the
i ssue and impose a fine on the management.
Although my father agrees that the military have
absolute control of the Government, he strongly feels that
the conservative and sensible section of the people is much
greater than would appear, and that any sign of weakening
on the part of Araki and Co. would create an avalanche of
disagreement. A revolution, although not at all probable,
is not impossible.
One of the reasons these people are so inarticulate is
that

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.95)
-2 -
that their opinions would be immediately castigated as
treason against the Emperor, which of course would not
be the case except vith the most radical.

The Secretary of State,

Sir:
1/.
There is hereto appended a translation of a curious
docuent which is entitled"CIRCULAR DIST IBUTED BY THE
DAI NANKOKAI (Association of Patriots) OF OSAKA AMONG ARMY
DIVISIONS IN JAPAN". The original document was loaned to
the Embassy by a foreign newspaper correspondent who attaohes
much importance to it, and since it is likely to be published
in the United States, I feel it desirable to make the follow-
ing comment concerning it. The translation hereto appended
yas

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
was made at the Embassy, and because of time limitation,
is somewhat crude.
The writer is obviously a fanatical patriot, probably
a junior army officer, of the type which assassinated former
Premier Inukai. His argument is rather incoherent as a
whole and the language used is highly impassioned, which
unfortunately suffers in translation.
The purpose of this document seems to be to arouse
feeling among the younger officers of the Army against
General Ugaki and against the movement to place him at
the head of the Government. General Ugaki is throughout
the article branded as a traitor. The latter has been
menthoned in recent newspaper articles as a possible
successor to the leadership of the Minseito party, and as
such a candidate for the premiership. The writer's animus
probably grows out of the fact that a few years ago when
Minister of War, General Wgaki permitted army appropriations
to be reduced and effected a reduction in the number of
army divisions. Furthermore, it was General Ugaki who,
as Minister of War last autumn, used the gendarmerie to break
up the plot, referred to as the "October Affair" throughout
this document.
This plot was discussed in the Embassy's confidential
despatch No. 382 of November 7, 1931. It is general 1y believed
to have been engineered by the sampe group that later was
reported to have been implicated in the assassination of
Baron Dan in March of this year. Police investigations at that
time

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
time disclosed the existence of a considerable organi-
zation in which army officers were implicated (See Embassy's
854.00/382c8.
despatch No.561 or April 7, 1932). The "May 15th Affair"
refers of course to the events in which Premier Inukai lost
854.00/4/3
his life (See Embassy's despatch No. 624 of May 20, 1932).
In the course of the tirade against General Ugaki,
several interesting revelations are made. One is that
the writer (or writers) is in thorough sympathy with the
assassins of former Premier Inukai. Throughout the dooument
the word used for killing is "tenchu" which may be translated
as "heavenly punishment" or "death by heaven's will".
The writer apparently had access to confidential informa-
tion in regard to the activities of the reactionary army
group and to the proceedings of the court martials which
followed the May 15th and other disturbances. Of course
it is possible that these mattens are generally known through-
out army circles, although banned from publication and little
known by the public. Incidentally, the account of the events
of May 15th given in this document is the first which has come
to the attention of the Embassy.
Whatever his sources, the writer is weak in history.
He makes the mistake of stating that General Ugaki was
Minister for War at the time of the "Maroh Affair". (Page 5)
General Ugati was at that time Governor General of Chosen
as he is now. A still more glaring mistake is his statement
(page 9) "when Yamagata, Takarabe and Okada ----- came to
hold power in the Navy".....). Yamagata was a marshal in
the Army and is called the "father of the Japanese Army". He
had no partioular connection with the Navy.
The

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.94)
- 4 -
The document is of interest chiefly as further evidence
of the lack of discipline and unrest in military ciroles
which has been noted by the Embassy from time to time. It
serves as evidence that the group of fanatical officers
which has been implicated in various reactionary disturbances
during the past year or more, is still in existence and may
still be regarded as a potential danger.
Although of consierable interest, I do not attach
undue importance or gravity to this document.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Edim I. taulle
Edwin E. Neville

A great national traitor, General Kazunari Ugaki,
in conspiracy with Count Nobuaki Makino, the Keeper of the
Privy Seal, is plot ing to organize a new Ministry under his
Premiership. This scheme is kept strictly secret. It may

on tool, as above stated,such a treacherous scheme should

Dear Mr. Secretary:
794.
Since my last letter to you of September 10 the two
894.00/444-1/
outstanding events in Japan have been the recognition of
793.
Manchukuo and the publication of the Lytton Report.
The
hastiness of the Government's action in recognizing Man-
chukuo had a two-fold purpose: to confront the League of
Nations and the United States with an accomplished fact
before the Lytton Report could be acted upon, and to afford
2
a sop to the Army to prevent their wrecking the present
Saito Government and perhaps setting up a military dictator-
ship.
The net result has been the internal pacification
of the country which is a great deal less restive then
before the step was taken.
The Japanese were apparently surprised when no action
was taken by the United States at the moment of recognition
and that the step caused so comparatively little repercussion
in the world at large.
This feeling may have been one of
The Honorable
FILED
Henry L. Stimson,
Secretary of State,
2
1
Washington, D. C.
19
3

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
relief, but I am inclined to believe that the relief was
tinged with a measure of disappointment, because in the
present temper of the country the last thing the Japanese
really want is to be ignored by the nations.
They prefer
dramatics.
The reaction to the Lytton Report here has been just
what was expected - a general condemnation of the findings
and an outburst of self-righteous indignation, but without
any serious attempt to refute the findings save by blunt
denials of their accuracy.
This public bluster is however
not shared by the saner and more moderate thinkers of whom
one, Baron Hayashi, Master of the Emperor's Household and a
thoroughly outspoken man, recently observed to a group of
friends that he considered the Report an admirable and well-
balanced document, especially Chapter 6 indicating that the
Manchukuo regime had been set up by the Japanese military.
There are plenty of people in Japan who have serious mis-
givings as to where the Manchurian venture is going to lead
the country, but the press does not dare to publish their
views. The military are still completely in control.
In explaining the present psychology of the Japanese,
two recent conversations are significant. Recently Prince
Chichibu invited to dinner Frederick Moore, who has just
come from the United States and is to act as a counselor to
the Japanese Delegation at the forthcoming meeting of the

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Assembly of the League of Nations, and questioned him for
over an hour as to public opinion in America, finally asking
him point blank whether it is true that the United States
is actively preparing for war with Japan. Such a question
from the Emperor's brother is highly significant of the
present nervousness of the country.
The other conversation
was with the Secretary of the America-Japan Society, Mr.
Takeda, whose comments seem to me to size up the situation
as well as anything I have heard, and I therefore append
a brief resumé of them. The kernel of Mr. Takeda's remarks
lies in the statement that the Army undertook the Manchurian
venture because it felt that it would lose all influence if
it did not do something "for the good of the country". What-
ever developments may occur in future, there will therefore
enter into the problem the important element of "saving face",
so essential in oriental countries. Here, I think, is the
most dangerous factor in the whole situation. If the fanat-
ical military clique finds that its program is being impeded
and is likely to fail, whether from internal or external
influences, it is quite capable of plunging the country into
any kind of disaster rather than give in to the saner and
more moderate elements in the country and acknowledging the
defeat of their plans.
The affair of the National City Bank has closed with
the publication in at least two prominent Japanese newspapers
811.20294

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
of the Department's press release conveying the statement
of the Foreign Office holding the Bank blameless and its
motives free from suspicion. Mr. Curtis, the General
Manager of the Bank, expresses himself as fully satisfied
wi th the result of the Embassy's assistance and tells me
that the published statement had an excellent effect, es-
pecially in calming their Japanese employees. The spy
scare continues and instances of innocent American travelers
taking photographs or "counting troops" are frequently
published, but my impression is that the campaign has over-
reached itself and that its absurdity is becoming patent to
a11.
At least one reputable Japanese newspaper has said so.
The anti-Americen press campaign is subsiding, although
the military will probably not permit it to cease altogether.
Several rather friendly articles have recently appeared and
the President's message of sympathy in the apparent loss of
the Hochi flyers will have an excellent effect.
A promi-
nent American journalist told me the other day that he had
spoken to Shiratori of the absurdity of the fear of the
Japanese that the United States was preparing for war with
Japan, to which Shiratori replied: "Of course it's absurd;
do you think we would have dared to attack Mr. Stimson the
way we have done if we thought there was any real danger
of war ?".

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
The new Manchukuo envoy, Pao, is truculently asserting
to all and sundry that Manchukuo proposes to escape from
Japanese tutelage at the earliest possible moment, that
most of the Chinese ministers in Changchun are in the pay
of the Japanese but that he himself is "fairly independent",
etcetera, but I am inclined to doubt his sincerity.
He
has also expressed his opinion that Henry Pu-Yi will soon
become Emperor of a North China State, embracing Manchuria,
with his capital in Peiping. Some of my diplomatic colleag-
ues and foreign press correspondents seem to attach import-
ance to his remarks, but neither Lindley nor I are impressed.
The diminutive Pao, who looks like a child, will probably
continue to "talk big".
As for our own future policy, it seems to me more than
ever wise, while firmly maintaining our position with regard
to the Kellogg Pact, the Nine Power Treaty and the Open Door,
to avoid any unessential steps which might tend to re-enflame
public opinion and give the military further strength.
Friendly gestures such as the comments about Japan in your
recent speech in Philadelphia, the friendly nature of which
was finally acknowledged even by Shiratori, and the Presi-
dent's message of sympathy coneerning the Hochi flyers,
tend to help the hand of the moderate elements in the count-
ry.
This, I gather, is your own feeling. It seems to me

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
that the more the League's action on the Lytton Report
can be tempered with friendly and constructive suggestions,
the more we shall gain rather than lose in the long run.
Our only hope of the eventually enforcement of the peace
treaties in the Far East lies in encouraging the moderate
elements in Japan.
In this connection, a remark made to the Military
Attaché by a Japanese member of the General Staff is sig-
nificant. The officer said: "We are working over-time
nowadays from 8 till 6 because we have to run two separate
departments, the War Ministry and the Foreign Office".
Debuchi came in for an hour's talk with me last evening.
He has been very active making speeches almost daily to
all kinds of people, including the military, but taking
care that they should not be reported in the press. I
gather that he has set forth the position of our Government
clearly and fairly. He talked with the Emperor for over
two hours day before yesterday in the presence of Count
Makino and others; he says that the Emperor understands
our position perfectly and is anxious to stop the anti-
American press campaign and the chauvinistic war talk.
Debuchi said to me that if Chang Hsueh Liang will only
keep quiet, there will be no question of Japanese troops
going to Peiping and that it all depends on Chang's move-
ments. He expressed the hope that after the manoeuvres
of our Atlantio fleet in the Pacific it will return to the

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
Atlantic next winter, because its presence on the west
coast furnishes an excuse for much of the chauvinistic war
talk and military and naval preparations here.
This
thought I hear on all sides. I think Debuchi wanted to
get this to your attention, since he came to me directly
after seeing the Emperor.
He continually repeated to me
that the domestic political situation is now well in hand
and that the more chauvinistio military people are being
compelled to moderate their views. Of the truth of the
latter assertion I have yet to be convinced. I dare say
that Debuchi will be decidedly helpful in painting a true
picture of the United States and the attitude of our Govern-
ment - that we do not possess horns and a tail.
He can
do what I cannot do. This is a time when, on the part
of the American Ambassador, silence on political matters
in public speeches is golden.
Respectfully yours,
Joo6 Jew

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
RESUME OF MR. TAKEDA'S COMMENTS
Mr. Takeda states that, when the Army first started
on its Manchurian adventure, the people of Japan looked
doubtfully at the matter. Later, when the League and the
United States started to condemn the Japanese for their
actions, the people rallied behind the Army, like all
members of a family will stand behind one member who is
being attacked from outside. Just now they will not admit
that the Army was wrong, but, just like a family, eventually
they will admit that maybe the person who was attacked was
in the wrong.
. Any violent move against Japan now will
strengthen this family feeling and keep the Army in power,
but if everyone stays quiet for a while, there will be a
feeling arise against the Army and maybe the Shidehara
diplomacy will return.
Nations are like people; they do
not like to be disliked.
The Army is trying very hard to keep in power, and
they are trying to keep the people aroused. The National
City Bank affair was for the purpose of advertising the
Army.
They are trying to show the people that they are
necessary to the country.
That was one of the reasons
why they started the Manchurian affair. There was a feel-
ing in Japan that the country had to have an economic outlet,
but also the Army felt that it would lose all influence if
it did not do something for the good of the country. If

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
disarmament was successful, the Army would not amount to
anything in the future, so they had to act to save their
position.
The Japanese people are very nervous.
They are like
a boy who has hit another boy and is looking around fear-
fully, expecting someone to punish them. They will be
nervous until after the League meets to discuss the Man-
churian affair.
The Japanese Army's operations are really aimed at
Russia - not at present, but at some time in the future.
Japan is afraid of Bolshevism and feels that it must drive
Bolshevism out of Asia.

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.99)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Paris, November 15, 1932.
Serial No. W. D. 1134
SPECIAL REPORT
By Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant
SUBJECT: A Plea for Closer Collaboration
with Japan
FIGARO published on November 13, 1932 an article
by E. de Gulchen presenting a plea fc a closer colla-
boration with Japan and particularly for a feeling of
more complete confidence in that nation. He remarks
that the more deeply one goes into the Far Eastern
question, the more clearly it appears that one Power
is called upon to play a genuine part there if allowed
to do so but its financial possibilities at the present
moment are greatly diminished. This Power is Japan,
against which foreign powers generally, and the United
States in particular, have closed their frontiers to
prevent immigration although it is necessary that the
Japanese should leave their country in considerable
numbers.
The author goes on to quote various works lauding
Japan and he deolares that Japan has now settled in
Manchuria for the greater good of civilization and
intends

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
intends to remain since the acquisition was made at
the price of the blood of Japanese soldiers and the
work of Japanese engineers and tradesmen. He indeed
considers that the Japanese civilization is illuminat-
ing the entire vast region and he hopes that it will
extend farther south. He goes on to ask:
"Will Americans at least understand that any
moral and even commercial progress made by Japan in
the Celestial Empire can only promote any personal
transactions, even if they were to meet with a
certain competition there?"
My personal comment is that I should be greatly
surprised if this article were not paid propaganda
subsidized by the Japanese Embassy in Paris.
Very respectfully,
wanounglondawron.
Warrington Dawson
Special Assistant
Enclosure:
Extract from FIGARO of November 13, 1932.

December 1, 1932.
The first two attached reports
of Warrington Dawson of November 15
and 19, 1932, give,résumé of an
article in FIGARO by Dr. Legendre
oriticizing the Lytton Report for
being pro-Kuomintang and for
attempting to justify the "false
conception" that Manchuria should
remain Chinese.
The third report of Warrington
Dawson of November 15, 1932, gives
a résumé of an article in FIGARo of
November 13 plead ing support of
Japan by the other Powers and laud-
ing Japan's civilizing effects in
Manchuria.
The fourth report of Warrington
Dawson of November 19, 1932, gives a
summary of an article in LE PETIT
PARISIEN of November 17, 1932, sup-
porting the Japanese.
In these last two despatohes
Mr. Dawson states that the tone of
the artioles leads him to feel that
the writers were paid by the Japanese
Embassy at Paris.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Paris, November 19, 1932.
Serial No. W. D. 1142.
SPECIAL REPORT
By Warrington Dawson
Special Assistant
SUBJECT: The Problem of Japan's Emigration
LE PETIT PARISIEN published on November 17th
a leading article by Andrée Vigllis entitled "Under
the Japanese Mask--The Overflowing Population and
Emigration."
A statistical and historical résumé of the
Japanese emigration question leads this writer up
to a discussion of the policy of the United States
on the subject, accepting Andre Siegfried's book on
the United States as an authority on the conditions
which lead up to "the total eviction of Japanese
immigrants."
The l924 law against Asiatic immigration is
stated to have dealt to Japanese pride a wound which
continues to bleed, causing the Japanese to believe
that only a war causing American blood to flow can
give them satisfaction.
The general

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
The general tone of this article confirms me in my
belief, already suggested in the conclusion of Special
894.00
Report No. W. D. 1134 of November 15, 1932 that the
Japanese Embassy in Paris is probably spending large
sums influencing the French press for purposes of
Japanese propaganda.
Very respectfully,
Cwarongton Dawkon.
Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant

modified even if they eventually should lose control.

DATED NOVE1BER 25,1932
It was reported from Tokyo that there was a growths
difference of opinion between Araki and Koieos, former
Vice Minister of War. Incidentally Koisos' transfer to
Manchuria was largely attributed to this significant
breach. Attempts were also made to diecredit both Koimo
and Minami, Araki's predecessor as War Minister, as
being r garded too moderate. General Hata, Chief of
Gendarmerie, was said to be responsirle for the appearance
of the report in the Kukomin reflecting the actions of
Koiso and  inami who were reoerted to be highly indignant.
The recent resignation of General Okasoto, Co mander of
the Guards Division, was said to be a protest againat the
prevention by the Araki clique of severe punishment to
certain officers of his Division who plotted to petition
The Emperor for the removal of certain high officials and
suspension of the Diet. The rift between the extremists
and the moderates of the Japanese Army, together with the
critigal financial condition; may prove to be a turning
point to bring the moderates into power

Tokyo's despatch NgPAPT97 ofF STATE
November 17, 1932, reports the arrest
on November 5 of 150 persons (about
120 subsequently released) suspected
of implication in the Inukai assassina-
tion plot and in a plot to assassinate
during the November maneuvers General
Ugaki, Count Makino, Dr. Ikki, Prime
Minister Saito, Finance Minister
Takahashi, Baron Go, Ikeda of the
Mitsui Bank, and Baron Hiranuma,
President of the Privy Council. Among
those arresteu were the son of Toyama,
leading reactionary of Japan, and the
man who stole the flag from the
American Embassy compound in 1924. It
is believeu that the arrest of these
reactionaries was ordered by the Prime
Minister, General Araki and Foreign
Minister Uchida, etc., as the police
would hardly have dared to take action
against Toyama. The Embassy is of the
opinion that the military was not
directly conoerned in the most recent
plot

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
plot but that elements in the army
helped to influence the conspirators.
In the final paragraph on page 5 are
given the probable reasons for the
inolusion in the assassination list of
the men mentioned above. The public
reaction to this latest conspiracy
differs from the reaction to previous
conspiracies when there was a very
definite current of sympathy with the
conspirators. This may be due to
ohanged conditions or to the danger
which suoh conspiracies have for the
security and stability of the state.
At any rate, press comment has been
severely critical of this latest
conspiracy and there is good reason
to believe that the public is becoming
disgusted with pseudo-patriots.
