894.00
The political situation here is very quiet
except for one significant factor, which is that
the powers that be, including General Araki him-
self, seem to think that political assassinations
in Japan have gone far enough and have determined
to put ε stop to them by striking at the soul of
the whole movement, namely, old Mitsuru Toyama
himself. His house was recently raided and searched
by the police and his son arrested. Hitherto
Toyama has been immune, but recent rumors of plans
to kill Saito, Makino, Ikki and Takahashi - after
the political murder of four of thc best men in
the country (Hamaguchi, Inukai, Inouye and Dan)
determined the Government that it was time t ta
The Honorable Henry L. Stimson,
Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
①

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
°.
drastic measures to put an end to assassination.
The fact that the Governnent dared to take these
steps - Toyama having been looked upon by a large
section of the public, including the military, as
a sort of super-patriot - is significant of its
growing confidence and authority. This does not
mean that the Saito Cabinet will necessarily last
beyond the next session of the Diet, for it very
likely will not. But it does mean that the
chauvinistic military hotheads and the so-called
patriotic societies, who were directly or indirect-
ly rosponsible for the former political terrorism,
are less firmly intrenched and are, it is hoped,
giving way to a more constructive statesmanship.
The anti-American press campaign has, for
the present, practically ceased. I am inclined to
think that Debuchi had sometiing to do with this
and I dare say that some order to that effect may
have come from the Emperor himself. It is signifi-
cant that the personnel of the press bureau of the
Ministry of War has recently been completely changed.

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
One of the new officers assigned to the bureau had
been transferred from the Japanese Legation in
Mexico, and when our Military Attaché smilingly
expressed the hope that he was going to put a
stop to the anti-American press campaign, he
replied that this was exactly vhat he was going
to do. General Araki has recently been unex-
pectedly cordial in his meetings with our Military
Attaché, as he has with me. Shiratori has lost
much of his influenc
his intimate contacts in
the Army have been trnsferred, and his political
ally, Kaku Mori, the Seiyikai schemer, is ill
and temporarily out of the picture. Shiratori
has been singing on a much lower note in his
press conferences. In my telegram No. 275 of
November 28, 8 p.m., I gave further reasons for
the present quiescence of the anti-American feel-
ing in the country. But of course this feeling
is always present nd would rapidly be fanned into
flame by any untoward incident.
With regard to the forthcoming meeting of
the Assembly in Geneva, after talking with many

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
diplomatic colleagues, foreign press correspon-
dents and American residents who know Japan, I
find no one who believes that Japan will make
substantial concessions, certainly none that would
alter or modify Japan's recognition of "Manchukuo".
On the other hand, I think it possible that Japan
may have something in reserve to bring forward at
the last moment. I base my supposition on tnree
reasons. One is the unlikelihood that Japan will
he so rash as to attempt to face the moral con-
demnation of the whole worlc, in spite of all the
brave talk which is now taking place. It seems
possible that they may have some plan by which
they hope to forestall a vote of censure. The
second is that Japan is arguing its case at Geneva.
If the nation is as sure of its ground as it
claims, why argue? In the third place it is becom-
ing increasingly evident that the recent drastic
decline in the yen exchange and consequent further
inpairment of the country's credit abroad is to be
traced largely to a lack of confidence in Japan's
political relations with the world at large. Until

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Until this feeling is liquidated the danger of
financial and social dislocation through the col-
lapse of the currency is ever-present, while foreign
capital markets cannot be counted on to come to her
aid. It seems to me that the Japanese are trying
to ameliorate the feeling aroused against their
actions by the Lytton Report, and that having put
the best face po: sible on their behavior they may
possibly come forward at the last moment, before
any action is taken against them, with some sug-
gestions looking toward an eventual settlement
some years hence. I do not for a moment believe,
however, that any suggestions which they may make
will contemplate the relinquishment of their
recognition of "Manchukuo" as an "independent
state". The members of my staff and I have been
endeavoring for the past week to obtain some
inkling of what the suggestions, if any, will
consist of, but we have had no success. It is
probably that the discussions in the Assembly of
the League will continue for some time, and if I

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
succeed in obtaining any idea as to Japan's inten-
tions, I shall inform you immediately by cable.
In my cablegram of November 28, 8 p.m., I
suggested that restraint be exercised in handling
the Sino-Japanese dispute, because coercive measures
would undoubtedly result in more firmly welding the
Japanese nation together in opposition to the
League and the United States. Any hint of force,
either military or economic, I believe, would
result in the uniting of the nation behind the
military and would completely overwhelm the more
moderate influences which are vorking beneath the
surface to restore Japan to its former high place
in the councils of nations. Moral pressure, however,
I think can be exerted without this danger and
might tend to widen the rift now beginning to be
noticed between the military and the moderate
elements. Eventually the force of public opinion
throughout the world, coupled with the difficulty
and over-burdening expense of pacifying Manchuria,
might cause Japan to change its attitude toward the

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
problem. Anyway, as I see it, it is our only
chance, as the threat of material pressure would
almost certainly have the opposite effect from
that desired. Thus the time factor is important,
and a gradual rather than an immediate solution
of the problem indicated.
My speech in Osaka appears to have been help-
ful. Its main purpose was to correct the general
belief here that the American people are not behind
you in your active support of the peace treaties.
The contrary was clearly brought out by the words:
"As for the American people, they are
heart and soul behind this (peace) movement
and behind those who have labored to bring
about a situation where warfare between
civilized nations will be as extinct as
human slavery in civilized nations is today.
This peace movement represents a fundamental,
united and unanimous desire of the American
people as a whole. The position of the
United States in this issue is clear-cut and
unambiguous. It has repeatedly been made
clear."
On the other hand, thc speech was eminently friend-
1y in tone and substance, so that the pill was
administered without causing irritation. The
Minister for Foreign Affairs last night expressed

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
to me personally his appreciation of the address.
May I take this occasion to say tuat I think
your instructions to Norman Davis and Hugh Wilson
relating to the Manchurian issue meet the situa-
tion exactly?
Sincerely yours,

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.95)
December 22 1952

American Consulate, Nagasaki, Japan,December 15, 1932.
Strictly Confidential.
The Honorable
Joseph C. Grew,
American Ambassador,
Tokyo.
Sir:
Attached hereto is a statement made by Mr. Glen
Bruner, vice consul at this post, concerning his inter-
view on the evening of Sunday, December 1lth, with a
Japanese claiming to repres nt a direct-action organi-
zation in need of funds.
Needless to say no encouragement has been given this
individual, nor will he receive any should he call again.
I shall direct Mr. Bruner to impress upon him in the
most convincing way possible the utter impossibility
of any American authority's acceding to the plans of
his organization.
It is difficult to guess why the consulate at
Nagasaki should have been selected for this attempt.
Possibly other American consulates in Japan have al-
ready been anproached without success. Perhaps the
fact that the two officers at this post both speak
Japanese may have made an approach through this office
seem easier.
To the average Japanese it seems quite in keeping
to approach a consulate on such a mission as disclosed
by Mr. Bruner's visitor. It is generally believed in
Japan that the chief function of a consul is to spy,
that his more obvious duties are only so much camouflage,
and that large sums of money are regularly spont by
him in under-cover activities of all sorts. To some
extent they are judging others by themselves since
they share with most Orientals a belief in the effi-
cacy of plots and schemes, treachery and sudden strokes
of direct action. To some extent, too, they can point
to historical precedent in Occidental countries.
While I did not see his visitor myself, I am in-
clined to agree with Mr. Bruner in his belief in the
sincerity of the visitor's proposals and in the general
truth of the latter's statements about the organization.
Very respectfully yours,
Henry B. Hitchcock, Consul.
Enclosure:
Statement, as
above described.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
American Consulate, Nagasaki, Japan.
December 12, 1932.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL.
Organization.
Last night at 8:15 my front gate slammed and
a moment later I heard someone violently shaking the front
door of my house. I went downstairs and found that my
wife had started to the door but, because a young man was
peering in at her through the glass at one side of it, she
did not open the door to him. I turned on the porch light
and opened the door, and then talked with him a few moments.
On being convinced that he was neither drunk nor insane,
and at his earnest request, I let him in and invited him to
my study where we talked until shortly after ten o'clock.
I submit the following notes on my visitor and our conver-
sation.
Ippei WADA is a young man about 28 years old, tall for
a Japanese, well built; wears glasses, but has no dis-
tinctive features. He says that he is a middle school
graduate; that he has served in the Japanese army; and that
he is now employed. Last night he was well but quietly
dressed in Japanese clothes. He wore a brown soft hat,
a dark Japanese Inverness, and a brown figured kimono
without hakama. He came into the house with his Inverness
on, and appeared to be perfectly at home as he hung his
wraps on the hall tree.
Once upstairs he incuired if there were any Japanese
in the house, and, on being told that there were, he ex-
pressed the hope that they were not within earshot. He
was assured that they were not.
Mr. Wada did not hesitate to broach at once the
subject of his call. He was manifestly agitated, and it
was evident that he had mustered all his courage in making
his call upon me. But in the course of the more than an
hour and a half of our conversation, he became calm and
self-possessed. When he left he seemed once more wrought
up, but this time it was not because he had been incivilly
received, nor yet because he felt that he had been mis-
understood or that he might be betrayed into the hands
of the police, but because he had received no encouragement
ghat his proposals might be considered.
The

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The RYODAN, according to Mr. WADA, is a "reactionary"
organization of loyal patriots. 1t has 4,000 members, most
of whom are ex-service men . Its leaders number about
fifty, most of whom are about forty years of age, but the
organization is without class distinctions. A leader is
a leader in his duties and responsibilities only.
Although the RYODAN is composed very largely of ex-service
men, it is anti-militarist, that is to say it opposes
the present "militarist-big business" combination which is
using high-handed methods, and is oppressing the common people
both within nd without the Empire. The RYODAN purposes to
overthrow this nefarious combination by violence. It believes
that it is better for a few individuals to die now than for
the common people to suffer eternally from the oppression
of the authorities at present in power. It believes that
the present Minister of War, Lieutenant General Sadao ARAkI,
personally directed the recently attempted coup d'etat in
which Premier INUKAI was assassinated, and that General ARAKI
and his supporters should suffer for their misdeeds. The
RYODAN has members in every division of the Japanese army.
Its members are also found among the workmen and the adminis-
trative employees of every munitions factory, or every
secret workshop, and of the secret arsenals, cmmunition dumps,
and oil reserve stations of every fortified zone in the Empire.
Mr. WADA decries the present coercive methods of the War
Department, is bitter towards the monied interests which
oppress the common people, is sympathetic towards the Chinese,
ridicules the idea that the Manchukuo was established by the
will of the Chinese residing in Manchuria, and denounces
the war propaganda which is being broadcast by the military
clique through military training and by means of moving
pictures.
Mr. WADA declares that he was sent to Nagasaki by the
RYODAN. He represents himself as a secret agent of that
organization. He travels a great deal, for he is one of the
RYODAN'S liaison officers. He organization leaves no written
records. Its representatives carry no credentials or means
of identification. Everything is secret, and all communifations
are made by word of mouth. Telegrams and telephone messages
are always disguised and, should they be investigated, would
always reveal some legitimate business enterprise instead of
their real purport.
Mr. WADA came to Nagasaki about a week ago He had legiti-
mate business. But he had other secret business, and his
principal objective was to get his group in touch with the
American Government authorities through the American Consul
at Nagasaki. He approached his task warily, well knowing
that not only his own life but also the vital interests
of his beloved wause were at stake. He found that the
American consulate is always guarded, and he, not having
any legitimate business at the consulate, dared not approach
it in the daytime for he does not wish hßs face to be noted by
any Japanese employee. He failed to find a method of approach
by

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
by day or by night until he happened to notice that 'the
name and address of th vice consul is shown on the front
gate of the consular office. He located the vice consul's
residence, nd found that it was on a quiet street, where
at night there was almost no traffic. It appeared to be a
place he might approach without being seen. He reported
the situation to his superiors, cnd was ordered to approach
the American Vice Consul in the hope that through him the
American Consul and the American Embassy night be reached.
At last he called at 8:15, Sunday night, December 11, 1932.
Mr. WADA seeks for the RYODAN the aid of the American
Government. The RYODAN understands the reasons for American
opposition to Je pan's present program in Manchuria, and
believes that in the United States it pessibly has a strong
ally, as the objectives of each are the same. Mr. WADA
does not solicit the personal aid of the American Consul or
of the Vice Consul. The RYODAN finds that it cannot succeed
in its purpose unless it has explosives or funds with which
to procure them. It requires not less than Yen 50,000, and
any small donations from American officials as individuals
woulc be of no avail. He asks nothing less than the full
cooperation of the American Government in the supplying
of funds for the work of the RYODAN. He says that he has
been entrusted with the task of getting the RYoDAN in touch
with the American Government through the American Consul
at Nagasuki; that attempts have been made to broach the subject
to the American authorities in Tokyo, but that in every case
these have been found to jeopardize the success of the movement
and were therefore dropped; and that he has been told that he
cannot hope for success the first time he approaches the
American Consul at Nagasaki but will have to appeal to him
time and time again in the hope thet success will come after
repec.ted cffort.
Mr. WADA makes a good impression. He has a good,clean,
alert face. He appears to be mentally sound. He was
thcroughly questioned not only as to his background bust as
to his experience with the RYODAN and his individual ideals.
It was suggested that young Jupanese have been known to use
funds for their own entertainment in the cafes and brothels
of Japan's cities. Mr. WADA swore that he did not touch
liquor, and said that for the sake of an hour's enjoyment
of the pleasures of such resorts he certainly would not sell
his soul, for, should he betray the interests of the cause,
his life would be worthless. His comrades would hunt him
down and destroy him, as they rightly should.
I told Mr. WADA that the American Government would not
be interested in such a movement. He was wrong in his as-
sumption that the American people would be sufficiently in-
terested in his "movement" to invest Yen 50,000 therein.
In any case consuls hao no jurisdiction over such matters.
Mr. WADA said that it appeared to him as only right and
proper that such matters be taken up by the consul through
the

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
the regular channels, and that he hoped that the consul
would approach the Embassy on behalf of the RYODAN.
He was insistent that I take the matter up with the
consul and reguest him to approach the Ambssador. Mr.
WADA is ready to undertake any delicate mission of
investigation on behalf of the American Government and will
be glad to carry out such an invesfigation in order to prove
the genuineness of the RYODAN. He asks that he may be
permitted to demonstrate not only the earnestness of his
group but also their fficiency nd worth.
Mr. WADA himself spoke of the accusation which may
with apparent justice be made of him and his group, that of
disloyalty to the nation and to the Emperor, but he stated that i
is their belief that theoverthrow by violence of the vested
interests is the only solution for the political ills of
the nation, and, in fact, for those of the For East.
He was assured that his "movement" would be laid before
the consul, and was encouraged to leave the house. He was
not invited to return, but he stated that he would be back
for an answer. In response to my warning that the house
might be watched, Mr. WADA warned me, in turn, not to attempt
to trace his movements nor to locate his lodgings in the city
of Nagasaki.
Glen Bruner.

American Consulate, Nagasaki, Japan.
December 16, 1932.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
Subject: A Second Report Concerning the RYODAN, a
Direct-Action Organization.
Reference is made to rcport dated December 19, 1932.
Mr. Ippei Wada called again at my residence at 8:20
o'clock last night. I was alone in the house when the door
was violently shaken, just as it had been on Sunday night.
I went downstairs and admitted him. He came in without ado,
and we went upstairs to my study und talked until after half
past nine, when he left.
On reaching the privucy of my study, after the exchange
of the customary Japan:se greetings, Mr. WADA seated himself
in an easy chair. He was dressed about the same as last Sunday
night. His man er was easy, and the nervousness which he had
exhibited at the time of the first visit was absent. He en-
quired whether I had conveyed his request to the ccnsul, and I
replied th: t I had done so. He showed much interest and at
once questioned me as to the consul's recepti n of the plea
of the RYODAN. I told him that the consul had said that there
was not even a possibility that his request could be granted;
that, as I had inforned him on Docember 1lth, the American
Government is not interested in his "movement"; and that,
although I had complied with his request und had conveyed his
plea to the consul, it would be well to consider the matter
closed.
Mr. WADA did not appeur to be surprised that the RYODAN'S
request had been rejected, but rather expressed his thanks for
my having put the consul in touch vith his mov ment. He stated
that if the United States Government could not be interested
that was all there was to the matter; and that in return for my
having so kindly taken the matter up with the consul he would
give me some "secret information".
In the following resume I set forth the information given
me last night by Mr. WADA, who states that the RYODAN has
gathered the facts as set forth below, and that the RYODAN
knows that its information is reliable.
In 1931, the Japanese military clique, finding that its
power was waning, decided to strengthen its position. The
General

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
General Staff, activated by General Jiro MINAMI, then
Minister of War, laid very careful plans, and, on the night
of September 18, 1931, creted :n "incident" near Mukden.
This "incident" was entirely & fabrication. The military
forces themselves blew u the r ilway, nd then used
this manufactured "incident" for their own purposes.
Premier AKATsUkI at once called a meting of the cabinet
to discuss the "incident". At this meeting it became known
to the members of th cabinet that the "incident" had been
made to order by the military clique. The passions of the
various cabinet members were aroused. Feeling ran high. Baron
Kijuro EHIDEHilhA, Minicter for Foreign Aff irs, was exceedingly
wrathy that & member of the cabinet should have been party
to so dastardly  plot, cnd penly accused the War Minister
of causing th nation cerious embarrassment in its rolations
vith foreign gover ments. The fight waged fiercely, und the
Prenier was on the point of dismissing the Minister of War.
At this point, Kenzo IlACHI, Minister for Home Affairs,
medinted between the quurreling factions, and persuaded the
Premier that it was the duty of the Ministers of State to make
the best of every situation. ADACHI held that for the War
Ministry to be openly discountenanced would be a disgrace that
would fall upon the whole nation. Moreover, it would reflect
even uno. the Imperial House. And it wus therefore the duty of the
members of the cibinet to stand by the"facts" as they he ve since
been procleimed to the world. At ltst the ADACI srguments won out
and the WAKATsUKI Ministry decided to uphold the Wer Minister
in all that he pretended to have discovered, though the know-
ledge that the"incident" was mere fabrication was exce dingly
trying to ths members f the cabinet, and especially so to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
General MINAMI wus thus suved. But he, en out-and-out
militarist, instead of being grateful for having been seved
from te rible disgrace, determined to be revenged upon his
enemies, and to make sure that his policies would prevail in
future councils of St: te. The War Department then, under the
personal direction of the Minister of War, Jiro INAlI, pre-
pared a death list. The orders were that e very person named
therein was to be assassineted. of course the name of
Tenzo ADACHI was omitted. But, beginning with the Premier,
cijiro WAkATgukI, the list included nearly all of the cabinet,
us well a many rominent bankers, incustrialists, und business
men. MINAMI' plan was well worked out, and might have
succeeded had it not been for the personal relations between
: certain major nd CHUZO MITSUCHI, Minister of Communications.
This mejor was indebted to MITsuCHI for the reason that the latter
had aided him to get an education. He interceded for his life.
The General Staff replied that if personal relations were to be
allowec to enter in, not a life could be sacrificed for the good
of the State, and ordered the major himself to take MITSUCHI's life.
The major refu sed. It was then suggested that the only thing
for him to do was to commit suicide. He decided to do so.
Calling

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
Calling at the home of the Minister of Communications,
he paid his respects to his friend, Chuzo MITSUCHI, and drawing
a revolver from his bosom, was about to shoot himself when
MITsUCHI stopped him. He later succeeded in prying from
the mejor's lips the reasons for such behavior. The police
were called in, but at once said that they could not oppose
the military. Premier WAKATSukI was notified. He called
MIMAMI, and confronted nim with unimpeachable evidence that
this plot had been instigated by MINAMI himself. This created
another intolerable situation. But, as it was impossihle
for the Premier to lay charges at the door of the War Department
without involving the Imperial Family in the person of Prince
KiyIN, Con under-in-chief of the Army, the upshot of the whole
affair was that the WAKATgUkI cabinet r sigaed.
According to the RYoDiN, the whole of Japan's political
life is ta inted. oney talks everywhere. The reason why the

IlukAI'S assassination is that the search of Chang Hsueh-

Mr. WADA asted if I had a c mera. I replied that I had a
srll one which I fornerly used in Nagasaki w th the permission
of the Jpanese authorities, but thut that permission being no
longer accorded to foreign residents of this post my camera had
fallen into dis se. He s id that if I would end it to him he woul

As r. WiDA ws about to leave the house last night, he again

he wes sorry there was no chance of the RYoDAn s request being

Tokyo, December 20, 1932.
Henry B. Hitchcock, Escuire,
American Consul,
Nagasaki.
Sir:
I have received and read with interest your letters
of December 15 and December 17 in which you informed me of the
calls made upon the Vice Consul by : man who described himself
as Ippei Wada, a secret agent of the Ryodan. Your attitude
and that of Mr. Bruner have my approval. In case this person
or any other person representing this or any similar organi-
zation should apnroach you, he should be made to understand
that the United State will have nothing to do with any attempts
to interfere with the operation of the Japanese Government.
No agent of the United States can associate himself in any way
with disloyalty or sedition.in this country.
I thank you Br bringing this matter to my attention
and I shall be glad to hear of any further developments
in this case. A copy of each of your letters and of this
reply will be sent to the Department.
Very truly yours,
Joseph C. Grew.
Ambassador.

MEMORANDUM
REOETVED
The Kokumin Domei, a new political
party in Japan.
JAN 11 1033
SECRETARY'S OFFICE
According to press reports, a new Japanese political
F
894.0
G
party, the Kokumin Domei, was inaugurated on Deoember 23,
1932, in the presence of 3500 delegates. The press re-
894.00/462
fers to it as the "National League", whioh is the official
translation, although a more accurate translation would be
"People's Party". Thirty-three Diet members are said to
belong to it, most of them reoruited from the Minseito,
the second largest political party in Japan. The leader
of the new party is Kenzo Adachi who, as Minister for Home
Affairs in the Minseito Cabinet under Premier Wakatsuki,
precipitated in December, 193l, the downfall of that
Cabinet by political intrigue.
The press states that the new party is of Fasoist
tendenoies, the correspondent of the NEW YORK HERALD
TRIBUNE referring to it as "the first Fascist parliamentary
party in Japan". These tendenoies are indicated by the
adoption of a flag with a golden eagle on a light brown
JAN 12
baokground, a uniform of blaok belted jaoket and striped
FILAD
trousers, and by the advocaoy of certain policies, e. g.,
1933
replacement

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
replacement of the Cabinet by a national counoil of
state responsible to the Emperor, control of publio
utilities and shipping, and a Far Eastern Monroe Dootrine.
Some weeks after the Manchurian inoident broke out,
Adaohi began to expound the view that the serious orisis
with whioh Japan was faced demanded the formation of a
National Government. The idea was pleasing to the
bureaucrats and the military, but not to the politioal
parties, as it was believed by many persons that Adaohi's
motives in fomenting the idea of a National Government
were not too remotely conneoted with his personal
interests. The Minseito as a party definitively rejected
the proposition, and Adachi promptly resigned from the
party.
It would seem that undue importanoe should not be
attaohed to the formation of this new party of allegedly
Fasoist character. Its leader, who is 68 years old, is a
skillful politician ambitious to beoome prime minister but
laoking in qualities requisite for a statesman, and in
Japan the strength of a party depends tefy rather on
the personality of its leaders than on policies. It is
soarcely oredible that Adaohi, who has shown himself in
the past to be an opportunist, can be a sinoere advocate
of reaotionism. It is more probable that he is attempting
to

- 3-
to give to a political party of the usual type a reaction-
ary coloring in order to take advantage of the nationalistio
sentiment at present widespread in Japan. Comment by press
oorrespondents indioates that the Japanese press is
skeptioal of the chances of sucoess of the party.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, January 26, 1933.
No. 266.
Subject:
Political Situation in Japan.
1333
F/HS
44
894.00/464
宏
Division of
FAR EASTERU AFFAIRS
FEB 14 1933
epartment of State
MAR 1- 1933
FILED
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
When the present Saito super-party Cabinet was formed
in May, 1932, it was generally understood that it was an
emergency cabinet and that it would relinquish its authority
when the emergencies which called it into being ceased to
exist. It now appears that the time is approaching for
the Saito Cabinet to relinquish the reins of office and to
give place to a cabinet more in keeping with the principles
of

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of parliamentary government. It is not believed, how-
ever, that the change will take place until after the
close of the present Diet session.
The principal opponent of the Saito Cabinet is
naturally the Seiyukai, the political party which controls
an absolute majority in the Lower House of the Diet and
which hopes to be called upon to form a cabinet in suc-
cession to the Saito Cabinet. A group of the younger members
of the Seiyukai is in favor of opposing the Cabinet in the
Diet and forcing the issue. The Cabinet could, however, in
such case dissolve the Diet and call a general election,
which the older members of the Seiyukai do not wish, as an
election might result in the party losing its majority in
the Lower House. Moreover, the public has shown no enthu-
siasm for a return of the Seiyukai to power. It is known
that Premier Viscount Saito and Dr. Suzuki, the President
of the Seiyukai, negotiated during December regarding the
attitude of the Seiyukai during the coming session of the
Diet, and it is now reported that the decision reached was
to the effect that the Seiyukai will not force the issue
during the Diet session(unless something unforeseen occurs),
with the understanding that the Saito Cabinet will resign
as soon thereafter as the emergency conditions permit. The
Seiyukai hopes that Prince Saionji, the sole surviving Genro,
will then recommend to the Throne the formation of a Seiyukai
Cabinet.
The Minseito, the second largest party in the Lower
House, will support the Saito Cabinet, on the grounds that
the present Cabinet was formed for special purposes, such
as relief of the farming class and restoration of economic
confidence

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
confidence, and that it should remain in power until it
has accomplished its task, after which it can dissolve the
Diet, call a general election, and turn over the reins of
the Government to a cabinet formed by the majority party.
The real reason behind its support of the Saito Government,
however, probably is that it sees no possibility at present
of obtaining control of the Government and prefers to work
with a super-party cabinet rather than to be the opposition
to a purely Seiyukai Cabinet.
The Kokumin Domei, Mr. Adachi's new party, is the dark
horse in the political situation. It is openly opposed to
the Saito Cabinet and apparently wishes to force a dissolution
of the Diet in the hope of obtaining a greater number of seats
than it now controls(32) in case of a general election. At
present it is unable to exercise any power unless it can join
forces with the dissatisfied group in the Seiyukai. The
Kokumin Domei is sometimes called the "Fascist Party" of
Japan but it has little in common with Fascism except a
uniform which somewhat resembles that of Mussolini's party.
Mr. Adachi's platform consists of the promotion of the
national spirit and of international justice; the adoption
of a state economy which will redistribute wealth, guarantee
a living to the masses and avoid their exploitation by the
capitalists; and the purification of politics and the estab-
lishment of a strong government by the abolition of the
Cabinet and the substitution therefor of a state council.
He advocates the readjustment of treaties binding Japan
in the Far East and the establishment of a Monroe Doctrine
for Asia. His platform is skilfully designed to appeal to
the

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the Japanese popular taste, but whether or not the Japanese
people will place any confidence in a man of Mr. Adachi's
reputation remains to be seen. It will be remembered that
it was commonly rumored at the time that Mr. Adachi was
bribed by certain financial interests to resign from the
Wakatsuki Cabinet in December, 1931, thus causing its down-
fall and bringing in a Seiyukai Cabinet, which removed
Japan
from the gold standard and made immense profits for the
exchange speculators.
It is rumored that the Saito Cabinet itself feels its
weakness, lacking the wholehearted support of a majority of
the members of the Lower House, and is willing to resign
after the close of the Diet session, in order that a more
homogenous cabinet, better able to cope with the situation,
may come into power. What complexion the new cabinet will
have is as yet a matter of conjecture. As stated before,
Prince Saionji is reported to be in favor of a Seiyukai Cabinet,
but the people have by no means regained confidence in the
political parties and would probably oppose the installation
of a cabinet of politicians at a time of great national stress.
A Fascist Cabinet under Baron Hiranuma is now out of the
question, as the vogue of Fascism has declined rapidly in
Japan in recent months. A group of bureaucrats under Baron
Y. Kubota, a Privy Councillor, is reported to be manoeuvring
for the formation of a new non-partisan cabinet upon the
resignation of the Saito Cabinet.
Whatever cabinet comes into power, however, there is
no reason to believe that the policy of Japan toward the
League of Nations and the Manchurian question will be sub-
stantially altered, as the smoldering revolt against the
control

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.96)
-5 -
control of the Foreign Office by the War Office has not
yet taken any concrete form.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

DIVISION OF
Despatch No. 266.
Date: January 26, 1933.
Subject: Political Situation in Japan.
Summary: From various reports which have reached
the Embassy it appears that under an agreement
between the Saito Cabinet and the Seiyukai( the
majority party) the Seiyukai will not force the
resignation of the Cabinet during the present
session of the Diet but the Cabinet will resign
soon after the close of the Diet session. The
Seiyukai hopes to be asked to form the new Cabinet
but the country does not seem to favor a return to
party government as yet. Another super-party
Cabinet is possible.
The Minseito is supporting the Saito Govern-
ment probably because it prefers to work with a
super-party Cabinet rather than to be the opposi-
tion to a purely Seiyukai Cabinet.
Prinoe Saionji is reported to be in favor of
a Seiyukai Cabinet. A Fasoist Cabinet under Baron
Hiranuma is now out of the question, as the vogue
of Fascism has deolined rapidly in recent months.
Whatever oabinet oomes into power, however,
there is no reason to believe that the polioy of
Japan toward the League of Nations and the Manchuran
FILD
question will be substantially altered, as the smolder.
ing revolt against the control of the Foreign Offige
by the War Office has not yet taken any conorete form.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

February 16, 1933.
Tokyo's despatch No. 271 of January 27,
1933, reports Ashida's interpellations in the
Diet, which were critical of the military and
which were reported in the American press at
the time. Excerpts of the replies of the
Minister of War are given, similar to the press
reports.
No considerable opposition to the military
and naval budgets developed in the open session
of the Diet. A member of the Lower House made
a veiled attack on the military by inquiring
what authority determines the military and
naval strength of the nation. The Prime
Minister replied that it is decided by the
General Staffs with the consent of the Govern-
ment.

EMBASSY OF THE

Tokyo, January 27, 1933.
SUBJECT:
Opposition to the Military in Japan.

Department of State
FEB 2 3 1933
FILED
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
As an indication of a slowly-developing revolt among
the political parties of Japan against the military control
1/.
of the government, I enclose herewith the full text, as
published in the JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, issue of January
23, 1933, of the interpellations in the Diet of Dr. H.
Ashida, a Seiyukai member of the Lower House of the
Diet
F

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Diet and the publisher of the JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL (see
my despatch No. 249 of January 10,1933). The Embassy
is informed that the news agencies cabled brief reports
of these interpellations to newspapers abroad and that
considerable comment was aroused thereby.
The principal points in Dr. Ashida's interpellations
consisted of (1) an assertion that Manchuria is at pre-
sent being controlled and governed almost entirely by
force by the Japanese Army and not in accordance with
any general national Japanese policy looking toward the
welfare of Japan and expressing benevolence toward the
people of Manchuria, and (2) an intimation that the
Japanese Army controls the foreign policy of Japan. Dr.
Ashida requested answers to the questions: (1) "Has the
Minister of War the intention, at least for the time
being, of forsaking the notion that the Army is almighty
and of properly utilizing the power of national unity
which was attained for the sake of the Manchurian ques-
tion?" and (2) "Why, then, may I ask, does the Foreign
Minister not make up his mind and carry out the diplo-
matic policy which he selects as that which Japan should
pursue, even at the cost of his life or in the face of
the obstacles and hindrances which exist in the country?
I regard it as a disgrace to the constitutional govern-
ment of Japan that a situation exists in which not a few
people are persuaded that the Army is responsible for the
diplomatic policy of the country.
In

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
In reply to the first question, the Minister of War
is reported to have said (JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, January
25, 1933), "that he wished clearly to point out that
while there are many who still possess the idea that the
army intends to handle everything in Manchuria, and con-
sequently refrain from investing capital in that terri-
tory, the army does not intend to shoulder the entire bur-
den of leading Manchuria to peace and prosperity. Further-
more, he asserted, it is neither correct for the army to
think it should shoulder the entire burden, nor is it its
duty to do so. The army welcomes any suggestions or advice
with regard to the settlement of the Manchurian question
and will gladly extend whatever cooperation it is within
its power to offer to this end." The Foreign Minister is
also reported to have said (JAPAN ADVERTISER, January 24,
1933), in reply to the same interpellation, "At the time
when Marshal Chang Hsueh-liang's administration collapsed,
the military authorities formulated the policy of Japan
toward Manchuria but after that time there were no such
abuses as are charged by the speaker."
In reply to the second question (regarding the alleged
military control of the Foreign Office), the Minister of
War is reported to have stated (JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, Janu-
ary 25, 1933), "During the thirty-odd years I have been in
the army, there has never been a time when there was greater
cooperation between the War and Foreign Ministries. If the
Army

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
Army is being charged with having gone too far, that
is another question, but even so I do not think we
have gone too far. In any event, there is no truth in
the statement that there is a War Department diplomacy
but not a Foreign Office diplomacy."
The Foreign
Minister is also reported to have stated that "there is
no foundation of truth in the supposition that the Army
is dictating to the Foreign Office."
No considerable opposition developed in the open
session of the Diet to the large military and naval bud-
gets. On January 23, 1933, Mr. Shinya Uchida, member of
the Lower House attacked the military and naval budgets,
pointing out that the Navy budget of Yen 370,000, 000 is
greatly in excess of the estimated budget compiled after
the London Naval Conference, and asking for information
as to the basis of the Army's demand for Yen 145,000,000
for Manchurian expenses. He then made a veiled attack on
the military by demanding a reply as to the authority
which determines the military and naval strength of the
nation. Premier Viscount Saito replied that the strength
is decided by the General Staffs with the consent of the
Government. The Minister of War placed the authority in
the Emperor, with the General Staffs operating through
their right of direct appeal to the Throne. The TOkYO
NICHI

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.93)
-5 -
NICHI-NICHI, English edition of January 24, 1933,
significantly reports that "The House greeted the War
Minister's reply with invectives and laughter."
Respectfully yours,
oL.
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 203 === (confidence 1.00)
Despatch No. 271
Date: January 27, 1933.
Subject: Opposition to Military in Japan.
Summary:
Dr. H. Ashida, Seiyukai member of the
Lower House of the Diet, interpellated
the War and Foreign Ministers regarding the
high-handed policy of the Japanese Army and
the claim that the Foreign Office is domina-
ted by the War Office. Replies quoted. Mr.
S. Uchida interpellated on the subject of the
Army and Navy budgets and the matter of su-
preme authority in the Army and Navy.

In any estimate of the situaticn 1 the Far East the
followin elenents should be givon consideration.
One. By the decisi on of t e Cabinet to secede from
the League of Nations Japan has prepared to burn her most
important bridge with the outside world. This step indi-
cates the complete supremacy of the military and a funda-
mertal dofeat for the moderate olements in the country.
Every important step by the League of Nations has been
forestalled or succeeded by a fait accompli here in order
to demonstrate Japan's independence of and disregard 
Western interference with what se conceives to b her
bliff
authority in her attitude. The military themselves, and
the public through military propaganda, are fully pre-
pared to fight rather than to surrender to moral or other
pressure from the West. The moral obloquy of the rest
of the world at present serves only to strengthen not to
modify

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.98)
MET
2-#45 from Tokio, Feb. 23, 1 p. m.
modify their determination. Any tendency on the part
of the Government to compromiso would almost certainly
result in further assassinations if not in internal
revolution.
Two. In this national temper the following elements
play their part:
4
(a) - The desire of tho military to maintain prestige
and thoir detormination to brook no interforence what-
evor;
(b) - Tho essontial importanco of saving face which
permits no backward step;
(c) - The carefully nurtured belief that Manchuria
is the "life line" of Japan;
(d) - Long strained exasperation with the formor
chaotic conditions in Manchuria and the failure of the
Chinese to fulfill their treaty obligations;
(o) - The total disregard by the military of future
financial difficulties arising out of the huge expenses
of the Manchurian campaign;
(f) - The basic inability of the Japanose to com.
prohond the sanctity of contractual obligations when
opposed to their own Far East interosts.
THREE. With regard to tho drive into Johol, it is belioved
that

 Finally, it may be said that a large section of the
public and the army has been led by military propaganda

The military and naval machines aro in a state of high

Telegram dated February 23, 1933, from the Ameriemm
Ambassador at Tokyo, reads substantially as fellows:
Consideration should be given to certain facters in
estimating the situation in the Far East, namely,
Japan has prepared, by the Cabinet's decision to
leave the League of Nations, to destroy her most importamt
link with other countries, thereby indicating a fundamental
defeat for the moderate elements in Japan and the complete
supremaoy of the military. In order to demonstrate her
independenoe of and disregard for Western interference
with what she conoeives to be her own vital interests,
Japan has forestalled or followed by a fait mocompli
Bul
every important step made by the League of Nations.
Japan's attitude is entirely free of bluff. Rather than
to surrender to moral or other pressure from the West, the
military themselves, and the publie through military
propaganda, are fully prepared to fight. At present their
determination is not modified but only strengthened by the
moral obloquy of the rest of the world. Further
assassinstions, if net intermal revelutiem, would almeet
certainly follew any tendeney on the part of the Govermmemt
to compromise.

metiomal temper:

MAR 18 1933
y dear Mr. Secretary:
894.00/468
I have received, at your direction, the
telegram from our Ambassador at Tokyo, of February 23rd.
89400467
This raises a most serious question.
897
As you are avare, I have all along been
inflexibly opposed to the imposition of any kind of
sanctions except purely pub ic opinion. The imposition
of any kind of sanction, military or economic, would in
the present state of mind of the Japanese people, provoke
the spread of the conflagration already in progress and
might even involve the United States.
MAR 31 1933
FII.ED
As it is not our intention to ever engage in
sanctions other thun that of public opinion, it would seem
to me that some occasion should be taken to make it clear.
It vould certainly relax the tension to some extent. It
would in no way undermine the importance of public opinion
in this controversy for under the non-recognition doctrine
that would be continuous and will ultimately be triumphant.
Confidential File
The whole doctrine of non-recognition is not
alone a method of invoking world opinion but it is equally
important in the phase that it avoids precipitant action
and allows time to vork out proper solutions.
It occurs
to me therefore that we should make it clear somehow. It
vould, I believe, relax a considerablo amount of present
tension.
Yours faithfully,

Dr. Ashida, a member of the House, and of the eiyukai Party, created
considerable excitement both at home and abroad in his speech requesting information
regarding the foreign policy of the Government.I
Dr. Ashida is also the publisher
of the "Japan Times & Mail", and the following quotation is taken from that paper.
894.00/470
hence it is presumed to be correct:
'Although uniformit and coordination of Japan's Manchurian policy
is the main reason for the existence of the National Cabinet, the impression
is imbeded in the public mind that the responsibility for leading Manchuria
is being shouldered excluviwely by the Arry. Starting with officials in the
Japanese Embassy all important posts in Manchuria are Army people. Has the
Minister of War the intention at least for the time being of forsaking the
notion that the Army is almi hty and of properly utilizing the power of
national unity which was attained fof the sake of the Manchurian question?
Let us remember that the Man hurian question at the present time constitutes
Japan's entire diplomacy.
Dr. Ashida's remarks certainly represent the viewpoint of the entire
thinking population. However, contrary to the implication of Dr. Ashida's remark
that foreign policy might better be in the hands of the Foreign Office than the
ar Department, the public at large is content with the results that have been
achieved. Of course, the statement merely confirms what we have been writing for
the past several months.
Mr. Kodama, President of the Yokohama Specie Bank, in discussing publicly
the financial outlook for the coring vear is quoted in the Press as follews:
'It may be possible that the United States would act as in the past to
aggravate the relations betwen itself and Japan, but it must be pointed
out that the so-called Stimson policy would accomplish nothing. The
uneasiness felt in financial circles in the United States towards Japan
FILED
is not due to Japanese policies towards the Asiatic Continent. American
apprehension is largely directed to the financiad status of this country.'

Solely for the Bureau's informat on, I might say that his remarks

follow very closely along the lines of thought held by most of the American resident's
in Japan."

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.98)
March 9, 1933
REPORT FROM TOKYO
General Remarks on Political and Economic Conditions
The 64th Ordinary Session of the Imperial Diet which has been
resumed since January of this year has only two more weeks before its final
adjournment. In this session, various legal measures as well as the annual
budget for the fiscal year of 1933 involving the amount of more than
¥2,230,000,000 were approved. The political situation after the closing of
the present session cannot be definitely predicted at this moment, but irre-
spective of it, our financial circles are bound to have great inflation. With
the approval for the 1933 budget, bond issue to the extent of 1,000,000,000
is authorized. Such a large amount of bond issue is certain to cause great
17
financial inflation and the rise of commodity prices which can be regulated
partly by the market operation of the Bank of Japan, but such an operation will
only retard temporarily, but not check indefinitely the inevitable consequences.
The amount of bank notes outstanding as at the end of February is
¥1,156,Ol3,000. Compared with the amount outstanding as at the end of January,
this is a decrease of 87,000,000. Commodity prices which had been steadily
rising since last July declined by 3% in Februery as compared with the preceding
478
month. These are probably due to the market operation of the Bank of Japan.
Regarding our foreign trade for February, export amounted to
¥118,890,000 and import to ¥182,368,000, giving a total of ¥301,258,000 and
resulting in the adverse trade balance of about 63,000,000. Compared with
the corresponding period of last year, export increased by 48% and import by
42%. These increases are largely due to the slump in the exchange rate of the
yen which remained at approximately twenty one cents throughout the month.
5

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
March 18, 1933
REPORT FROM SCANDINAVIAN DISTRICT
Political and Economic Situation
The Norwegian Government which was formed by the Farmer
Party in May, l93l, resigned at the end of February, and a new liberal
cabinet was formed with Mr. Mowinckel as Prime Minister. It was the
economic questions which prostrated the Government this time. It is
generally expected that the new Goverrment will be more liberal than
the old wi th regard to social undertakings to relieve unemployment.
In connection with the annual report and the accounts of
the Norwegian State Bank for l932, the Director General, Mr. Rygh,
held his usual speech on the economic situation. According to what
he said, the business activity in Norway has been fairly well kept up
as compared with other countries, and in his opinion the position at the
end of 1932 was better than a year ago.
The recent depreciation of the Danish krone was reflected
in the wholesale price index which showed an increase from ll7 in Jan-
uary to l24 in February. The effect on the cost of living is not known
yet.
The recent raise in the German duty on agricultural products
will affect Denmark most seriously and will virtually bring the Danish
export of cattle to Germany to an end. The Danish exthange committee has
announced retaliatory measures in arder to bring about further reductions
in the importation of German goods to Denmark.
Arter a transient decrease some weeks ago, the number of un-
employed in Denmark 1s again increasing, and reached 193,140 people in the
week ending March 3rd.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.97)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 474.
Tokyo, July 14, 1933.
SUBJECT:
Recrudescence of Reactionary
Activities.
794,00
Division of
F/G
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
JUL 23 1933
Depertment of State
PI
894.00/480
COPIES SENT TO
O. N.I. ANDM. I. DA
The Honorable
AUG 2- 1933
LED
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
For the past eight months (since the arrest of
a gang of young reactionaries, including Hidezo To-
yama, on November 5, 1932) little has been heard of
the reactionary movement in Japan, and therefore it
was with some surprise that it was learned on July
1lth that the police authorities had discovered and
frustrated another reactionary plot.
The persons involved in this latest plot were
members of the Dai-Nippon Seisanto (Great Japan
Production

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Production Party), a reactionary organization affi-
liated with the Kokuryu-kai (Black Dragon Society)
and headed by the reactionary stalwarts, Mitsuru
Toyama and Ryohei Uchida. Some 49 members of this
organization, principally from the branches in the
prefectures of Osaka, Hyogo, Nara and Tokyo, organ-
ized themselves into a band called the "Dai Nippon
Shin-heitai", or "Great Japan Godly Soldiers" and
gathered in Tokyo on July 1Oth. The police learned
of the gathering, however, and arrested the men at
the tourists' dormitory attached to the Meiji Shrine,
whither the band had gone to offer prayer before
taking action. On the following day (July 1lth),
the police raided the headquarters in Tokyo of the
Seisanto, and there discovered three Japanese swords,
some canteens filled witn gasoline, and a large quan-
tity of banners and handbills. The banners bore in-
scriptions such as "Ensure the Life of the Imperial
Government"; "Ensure an Economic System based on
the Imperial Way"; "Restore the Imperial Way"; "Down
with Communism"; "Wipe Out the Plutocratic Government";
"Strengthen National Defenses"; and the names of ancient
deities and emperors. The handbills consisted of ap-
peals to the Imperial Army; proposals for the promo-
tion and reform of the Japanese Empire; appeals to
the people; and declarations of the plotters' purposes.
The intention apparently was to distribute the handbills
over Tokyo after a reign of terrorism had been inaugurated.
There

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
There is an unconfirmed rumor that five young
military and/or naval officers have also been ar-
rested in connection with the arrest of the Seisan-
to members. According to the newspapers, the police
have not yet been able to obtain any confessions from
the members of the band, who claim to have come to
Tokyo solely for the purpose of offering prayers at
the Meiji Shrine for the welfare of the nation at
this time of crisis and for the enhancement of na-
tional defense. The police, however, apparently have
other information which makes them believe that a
serious incident might occur, as they posted extra
guards on the llth around the Premier's official
residence, those of other Cabinet Ministers, and
Embassies. RENGO states that the plan was to assas-
sinate the Premier, the Minister for Home Affairs,
the War Minister, Count Makino, Baron Wakatsuki
(President of the Minseito) and Mr. Suzuki (the
President of the Seiyukai). The possession by the
plotters of a detailed floor plan of the Metropoli-
tan Police headquarters also led the police to believe
that the scheme included a raid on police headquarters.
The alleged plan to assassinate Saito, Yamamoto,
Makino, Wakatsuki and Suzuki is in line with previous
plans of ultra-reactionaries to abolish political par-
ties and the parliamentary form of government, but the
inclusion of the name of General Araki in the list of
those marked for assassination injects a new element
into the situation. It appears that there has been
considerable

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
considerable dissatisfaction among the younger,
fire-eating element in the army and navy with
General Araki. It is known that he has been act-
ing as a brake on the more violent elements in the
Army, much to their discomfiture. He has come out
openly in support of the Saito Cabinet, implying
his approval of the present form of government, which
some elements among the militarists and reactionaries
would like to change to one more in keeping with the
vague principle of the "Imperial Way". Moreover,
he has given his tacit approval, at least, to the pur-
chase of the Chinese Eastern Railway by Japan or "Man-
chukuo", while the more violent elements in the Army
take the stand that Japan already controls the rail-
way and that there is therefore no reason why Japan
or "Manchukuo" should pay for it. It is presumed by
some observers that these constitute the reasons for
marking General Araki down for assassination.
Certain factors indicate that this latest demon-
stration by the reactionaries was not intended to be
serious and was in the nature of a warning. In the
first place, those involved acted quite openly, with
the apparent intention of attracting the notice of
the police. In the second place, the expedition to
Tokyo of the reactionaries was not well planned or
equipped. The band apparently had no fire-arms,
hand-grenades or explosives, and possessed only three
Japanese swords for forty-nine men, with a small amount
of

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
of gasoline with which to start fires. It does not
seem possible that the band could have inaugurated
a reign of terror with this meagre equipment. Cer-
tain observant Japanese with whom the writer has
talked believe that the incident is simply a no-
toriety-seeking scheme on the part of the participants.
The trial of the reactionaries involved in the inci-
dent of May 15, 1932 (in which Premier Inukai was
killed) is now taking place and has fired the imagi-
nations of the young dare-to-dies among the Japanese.
Other observers attach a deeper significance to the
scheme and believe it to be in the nature of a warn-
ing to the courts not to deal too harshly with those
involved in the affair of May 15, 1932.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.98)
Embarry,
Jokyo.
MTH:
Despatch No. 474
Aimmary
Date: July 14, 1933.
Subject: Recrudescence of Reactionary Activities.
Summary:
On the night of July 10-11, 1933, the police
in Tokyo arrested 49 members of the Dai-Nippon
Seisanto, a violent reactionary organization,
who had formed a band called the "Shin-Heitai".
The police evicently believed that this band
intended to create a reign of terror, as extra
guards were immediately posted around th e
principal public buildings and the foreign
embassies and legations. A search of the
headquarters of the band disclosed only three
Japanese swords and some gasoline. It is ru-
mored that the band intended to assassinate
Premier Saito, Home Minister Yamamoto, War
Min ster Araki, and the heads of the two prin-
cipal political parties. The plan to assassi-
nate the politicians is not new, but the plan
to assassinate the War Minister is new. This
is probably due to dissatisfaction among the
more violent element in the Army and Navy with
the braking influence of Araki. The apparent
intention of the band to attract the attention
of the police and their poor organization and
meagre eauipment cause observers to believe
that this was only a notoriety-seeking scheme
on the part of young reactionaries who have
been inflamed by the trial of the reactiona-
ries involved in the affair of May 15, 1932.
Others believe it to be in the nature of a
warning to the courts not to deal too harshly
with those accused of the May 15th affair.

L'Officiel publie une loi contre la propagation
des maladies héréditaires. Elle prévoil la stérili-
sation des personnes. hommes ou femmes, attein-
tes de ces maladies. Il s'agit notamment des idiots,
des épileptiques, des fous, des aveugles et des
sourds-muets, ainsi que des alcooliques chroni-
ques. Seuls, les cas graves sont envisagés.
On procédera à cette stérilisation sur la de-
mande de l'intéressé, de son tuteur, d'une per-
sonne préposée à ses soins, d'un médecin qualifié
ou d'un directeur d'établissement (asile d'allé-
més, eto.). La décision sera prise par un tribunal
composé d'un juriste et de deux médecins, dont
un spécialiste des maladies héréditaires; le méde-
ein entendu comme expert sera tenu de déposer
et dispensé du secret professionnel Un appel d?
ce jugement pourra étre fait devant un tribunal
spécial, dont la sentence sera définitive. Elle
pourra etre imposée à celui qui en est l'objet. Les
frais de l'opération seront supportés par l'assu-
vanee-maladte. Il s'agit de stérilisation et non pas
de castration, opération qui sera réservée à cer-
tains criminels.
On rappelle que des lois analogues existent dans
vingt-quatre Elats américains sur quarante-huit,
u Danemark et dans un canton suisse. On.
constaté, en Allemagne, que les familles saines
n'ont plus qu'une moyenne de deux enfants, alors
que les idiots et simples d'esprit ont généralement
des familles nombreuses. sf l'on ne portalt re-
mède à cette situation, il en résulterail, aprbe
quelques générations, une dégénérescence com-
plète de la race.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 2, 1933.
Tokyo's despatch No. 497 of August 17,
1933, discusses the position of the Jap-
anese Cabinet with respect to the political
parties. It is opportune, interesting
and well written.
You may perhaps feel that it is
somewhat too eguivocal with regard to
the future and somewhat lacking in in-
cisiveness. These possible defects are
inseparable from any honest attempt to
forecast political developments in Japan.
Politics in Japan revolve to a large
extent around the personal relationships
between politicians and without the con-
clusive knowledge of these relationships
it is almost impossible to know what is
actually going on beneath the surface.
I hesitate to express any opinion
with regard to the several prophecies
which the Embassy ventures to make, as
I have not been following politicai
events in Japan sufficiently closely. I
believe, however, that the Embassy is on
sound ground when it expresses the belief
that the present cabinet will continue for
some time to come without any material
ohgng

The Embassy has not, for some time, renort-

tion. This has partly been due to the lack of any

because of the impossibility of making sense out of

government" and the evasive attitude of Japanese

Peiping's despatch No. 2265, dated

1933
0CT 5
tion, regarding the future plans of the Japanese.
He informed me that officials of the Kailan Min-
ing Administration, with whom he had talked recently
at Chinwangtao, seemed to believe that the Japanese
military would probably start a fresh invasion of
North China before the end of the year, for the purpose

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
While Colonel Drysdale and I agree that from a
military point of view there is nothing to prevent
such a move, I personally doubt whether the Japanese
Government has any such intention. If it had been
contemplated to obtain China's immediate recognition
of "Manchukuo" by military means, the Japanese would
doubtless have taken advantage of the opportunity
which presented itself three months ago, when they
were within a few miles of Peiping.

156, October 11, noon.
CONFIDENTIAL. Reports of serips disagreement in the
894-002
Cabinet continue. On what appears to be reliable authority
the new Minister for Foreicn Affairs has aligned himself with
the Finance Minister in opposition to the demands of the army
and navy for increased appropriations. Hirota is reported to
be using his influence with the reactionary and superpatriotic
societies to undermine the position of the Chauvinistic
military elements. As Hirota is friendly with this croup,
due to his early associations, they are stated to be willing
0
to listen to him and to accept his views as to what is goodi
FILED
for the nation in its relations with other powers.
This turn of affairs is said to be causing much anxioty
to the militant faction in the army which is pushing the
Minister of War to insist on his demands for excessive
military appropriations. This group cares little for foreign
opinion and is ready to go to almost any lengths in what they
consider to be the country's needs.
The view

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.98)
REP
2-#156, From Tokyo, Oot. 11, noon.
The view of the Minister for Foreign Affairs is that
the nation needs peace and friendly relations with foreign
nations, that the country is in no danger at the moment, and
that greatly increased military expenditures at this time
will earn an ill-will abroad which will cost Japan more than
the increased margin of safety is worth. If Hirota and
Talahashi can carry their point, many qualified observers
are of the opinion that there will be danger of a coup d'etat
or an attempt at one on the part of the dissatisfied military
elements who see their power waning. In such an event much
would depend on the attitude of the Ministry of the Interior
which controls the police.
The diot is duo to meet in December and the budgot must
bo ready by that time. Thoreis a measure of opinion that
Viscount Saito may find himself unablo to meot tho Diet with
his presont Cabinet and that he will either resign or give
a deoision which will discomfit one or the other of the groups
in the Cabinet.
GREW

Latest indications are that the Army and Navy will
secure most of their monetary demands in the next budget,
but that the Minister for Foreign Affairs will obtain
general acceptance of his contention that the country
is in no danger of attackat this time; and that the
increased expenditure for the Army and Navy, especielly
the former, will be based largely on their technical and
reasonable need of much new equipment because of the gen-
eral advance in military and naval armament in recent
years of which Japan must keep abreast.
Outwardly this does not look like a victory for the
C
moderate elements; as a matter,of fact it is a recognis
FTLED
tion of the position of the Minister for Foreign'Affatts
3
to an extent which seemed unlikely a few months ago. ton-
sequently we may expect less bluster and truculence on
the part of the military in matters affecting foreign
affairs; they will have most of the money they want, but
will

will admit that the Minister for Foreign Affairs should
not be embarrassed in the conduct of his office by pro-

his efforts towards improving Japan's international rela-
tions.
In view of the foregoing situation, which amounts to
a comromise between the discordant elements in the Cabi-

met appears more favorable than when my 15d was sent.

FILD
entitled "The Trials of the Reactionaries Involved in the
2
1933
Incidents of 1932" which has been prepared in this Embassy.
The trials have not yet been completed but there are trans-
mitted those sections having to do with the uncompleted
trials of the "Blood Brotherhood", who planned the assassi-
nations of former Finance Minister Inouye and Baron Dan, and
with the courtmartial of the military cadets who helped in
carrying out the terroristic acts of May 15 when Premier
Inukai

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Inukai was assassinated. It is believed that the court-
martial of the naval cadets who planned the May 15th Inci-
dent will be completed in time to permit the forwarding of
another section by the next pouch.
As will be seen from reading the report, the several
incidents were the outcome of conditions which have existed
for many years.
. The dissatisfaction with the political
parties, which was one of the prime causes, is of long
standing. Large elements of the population have looked
askance, not only at the corruption in high places to which
considerable publicity has been given from time to time, but
also at the petty squabbles which have created the impression
that the party leaders were interested in little more than
their own advancement. Not only the army and navy but also
the civilians, especially those in the agricultural districts,
consider that they have suffered from these conditions. Re-
actionary societies have long preached the necessity of a
radical reform and during the last two or three years groups
have been planning to take "direct action" to "reform the
country".
The army is recruited mostly from the country districts
and the rank and file have long heard the expressions of dis-
satisfaction mentioned above. In addition to that, after
entering the service they are subjected to a system of train-
ing which aims to instill in them an ardent patriotism. The
traditions of feudal days are taught
them thoroughly and,
as that age is comparatively recent in Japan, these teachings
possibly mean more to them than they would to soldiers of
some other countries. It is difficult to say how different
the

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
the fundamental traits of the Japanese are from those of
other peoples and how much these traits have influenced
their action in this case. However, the fact is that along
with the rigid discipline there exists a kind of patriotism
which in the present case prompted army cadets to commit
crimes which in any other country would be considered with-
out question the most serious which a soldier could commit.
It is significant that the incident of May 15, 1932
was planned by naval officers. This indicates very serious
conditions of discipline in that body. The immediate cause
of thc affair was undoubtedly dissatisfaction over the London
Naval Treaty. It will be remembered that at the time of the
ratification of the treaty the question of the "Supreme Com-
mand" caused a serious rift among Japanese naval officers.
This difference of view appears to have been reflected among
the rank and file of the navy. It led the roup involved
to take "direct
in the incident under discussion to decide
action" before the next naval conference in order to remove
all who might cause it to be a duplication of the London
Conference as far as Japan is concerned.
After the several assassinations and other terroristic
acts were committed, a press ban was placed upon the publi-
cation of any information concerning them. This held force
during the investigations. After more than a year had elapsed
it was announced that public trials would be held and, as
will be seen from the enclosed report, they have been stretch-
ed out during many months and are still continuing. They
were started at the beginning of the summer months when there
is usually little news for the papers to publish and they
received

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
received the most sensational publicity possible.
It was obvious that the trials could have taken place
with less fanfare and that the government intentionally
stage-managed them. Much speculation has taken place re-
garding the motives of the government for indulging in this
procedure. The opinion among the most experienced observers
is that one im ortant intention of the government was to
discredit those who take part in such affairs. It was hoped
that by letting the defendants speak their minds they could
be made to look ridiculous. The government wanted to give
them enough rope to heng not only themselves but also, figu-
ratively speaking, the entire reactionary movement.
To what
extent this effect was produced it is very difficult to say.
Certainly the government's success was not complete. The
nation in general appears to have sympathized with the cul-
prits. Instead of being considered ridiculous they are
looked upon as idealistic and patriotic, but impractical and
lacking in political sense. They undoubtedly did convince
the public of their ignorance of affairs and of their being
simple-minded in a sense, but beyond this the government's
plan, if the above was its plan, was something of a failure.
If the government's prime purpose in giving such publi-
city to the trials was not accomplished, there were produced
other effects, possibly of a far-reaching nature and satis-
fying to the accused. The first was the complete discredit-
ing of the political parties. During the first half of this
year the machinations of the Seiyukai and Minseito, especial-
ly the former, were attracting the attention of the press
and the public. The Seiyukai was credited with the desire
to

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
to weaken the Saito Government and bring about an early re-
turn to party government. It is true that the public was
not impressed with these moves and the Seiyukai did not on-
hance its prestige. However it worried the government and
the military. Whether it was intended or not, when the
public heard the speeches of the reactionaries during the
trials and their denunciations of the politicians, all
hope of an early return to party government vanished. Some
people say that the development of parliamentary government
in Japan was retarded a decade, but this is a statement
hard to prove regarding a complicated society influenced by
so many factors.
It should be remembered that parliamentary institutions
are a relatively recent innovation in Japan; that the pub-
lic has never been accustomed to have public business settled
by discussion; and that consequently opinions such as those
held by the defendants in the trials have undoubtedly been
held by large numbers of Japanese people for a long time.
Furthermore, their views concerning "politicians" may be
shared by thousands, or even millions, in other countries.
However, it seldom happens that such views are given the
publicity accorded them in Japan in the summer of 1933.
Cases of corruption in higher circles have received considera-
ble attention in the press of Japan in recent years, as
they would in any country, and it is only human that many
should reason that much corruption must exist which is never
given the light of publicity. Such opinions are often heard
but seldom shouted from a public tribunal. The effects of
the latter process are now shown in Japan. The public has
only too readily sympathized with such sentiments expressed

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
by men who were willing to sacrifice their lives for their
convictions. The result is that political parties have
suffered a setback from which it will be very difficult for
them to recover.
The discrediting of the parties had the momentary ef-
fect of strengthening the Saito Cabinet. What the more far-
reaching effects will be is difficult to tell. Whereas, six
months ago the talk in political circles was of the return
of party government, now there is a revival of discussions
of fascisms and dictatorships. At the moment of writing the
political parties are discussing a merger among themselves
to combat the influence of the military.
A second most important result of the trials has been
to make the London Naval Treaty more unpopular. If this was
the purpose of the naval officers who planned the Incident
of May 15th they have been successful to a great extent. As
the naval courtmartial has not been completed at the time
of writing this despatch and the section of the Report on
the Trials covering the navy has not been transmitted to the
Department, more complete discussion of this point will be
forwarded later. However, the publicity given to the views
of the defendants in the naval courtmartial has undoubtedly
acquainted the public with the arguments against naval limi-
tation in a manner not otherwise possible.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.
