=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
THE TRIALS OF THE REACTIONARIES
INVOLVED IN THE INCIDENTS OF 1932.
Introduction.
Tokyo has recently been the scene of a series of pub-
lic trials which are of signal importance to students of
Japanese psychology and morals. Nominally, the subjects
of the trials are the murderers of former Finance Minister
Inouye and Baron Takuma Dan and those who committed the
series of terroristic acts on May 15, 1932 which included
the assassination of Premier Inukai. Actually, the Japanese
body politic is passing through a serious crisis and these
trials in a sense enable contemporary historians to study
the fundamental conflicts and trends to better advantage
than they are generally privileged to do.
One outstanding feature of the trials is the conflict
revealed between the letter of the law and a certain kind
of patriotism which is talked of a great deal in Japan.
The accused - civilians, soldiers, and sailors - committed
the most serious offenses known to the nation's laws and
the military and naval codes. Yet they insist that they
were impelled by a type of patriotism and loyalty to the
Throne which the Japanese consider distinctively their own
and one of their prize possessions. If similar acts had
been committed in earlier days the perpetrators, according
to the usual interpretation of Japanese history, would have
felt impelled to take their own lives. However, western
ideas have been absorbed to such an extent that the plot-
ters have refrained from committing the usual "harakiri".
On the other hand nearly all of the defendants have blamed
the

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the conditions which they hoped to remedy largely on the
penetration of foreign ideas into Japan.
The defendants undoubtedly enjoy the sympathy of the
mass of Japanese people, especially the rank and file of
the army and navy. Although no evidence has been produced
to indicate that high officers of the armed services were
implicated in any of the assassination plots, yet there is
no doubt that most of them share the beliefs and ideals of
the accused. During the trials they have been torn between
their sense of military discipline and the patriotic ideals
which they have learnt as a part of their military educa-
A valuable contribution toward an understanding of
this spirit which animates the Japanese army is provided
in a recently published book written by the War Minister,
General Araki. According to a condensed translation appear-
ing the JAPAN ADVERTISER, the author, in discussing the
training of the Japanese soldier, makes the following state-
ment:
"...military education in other countries attaches
primary importance to training in the art of
fighting and in order to reinforce it they add to
it spiritual training based on the psychology of
warfare. Their education may be summed up as an
intensive disciplinary training to fit men for
fighting. Military education in Japan, however,
.....places more stress on the moral side of the
training.....making morally superior soldiers.
Further on he states:
11
We hear soldiers criticized for their foolhardi-
ness or 'naivety'. That does not matter for it is
the good effect of military education. That the
soldiers are too simple or easily imposed upon
shows that the world outside is worse than the mili-
tary in their moral standards."
Throughout the trials the "immorality" of the politicians is
referred

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
referred to. The members of the armed forces who took
part in the assassinations and attempted assassinations
appear to have thought that they were helping to rid the
Emperor of "immoral" advisers. Their ideas on these mat-
ters were taught them by their superior officers and the
latter therefore cannot escape a certain responsibility
for the consequences, a fact which they undoubtedly real-
ize.

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
THE CRIMES
Before going further in discussing this matter a brief
resume of certain facts will be given. On February 9, 1932
Mr. Junnosuke Inouye, twice Finance Minister of Japan and
outstanding Minseito leader, was shot dead as he was arriv-
ing to speak at a political meeting. On March 5, before
the public had recovered from this shock it learnt that
Baron Takuma Dan had been shot by a native of the same vil-
lage from which come the assassin of Mr. Inouye. Baron Dan
was senior managing director of the Mitsui Gomei Kaisha and
the outstanding leader of Japanese industry and finance.
Within a few days the police discovered the existence of an
organized socicty called the "Blood Brotherhood", the mem-
bers of whicn had sworn to kill many of the most prominent
leaders in Japanese politics and industry. The plotters
were immediately arrested and the press was prohibited from
publishing anything about them until the public trials of
fourteen of them began on June 28, 1933.
In the meantime, it appears that other groups planned
to carry out the aims of those who had been arrested. The
leaders and organizers were a group of junior naval officers.
They secured the cooperation of a group of civilians and
tried to interest a group of army officers. Failing to win
over the latter, they finally obtained the aid of some mili-
tary cadets. May 15, 1932 was the day set for a series of
terroristic acts which the perpetrators hoped would throw
the city into confusion, cause the proclamation of martial
law, and thereby bring a military regime into power which
would destroy the influence of the "privileged classes".
About

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
About five o'clock on the afternoon of the appointed day
two groups composed of naval officers and military cadets
entered the official residence of Premier Inukai. After
wandering about the house and frightening away all those
detailed to guard him, they fired several shots at him
from the effects of which he died a few hours later. A
policeman also died from wounds received in defending the
house. During the attack upon the Premier's residence
another group threw a hand grenade at the entrance of the
home of Count Makino, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, doing
little damage. A group of uniformed men also entered the
offices of the Metropolitan Police Board destroyed a cer-
tain amount of furniture and other property and wounded a
policeman. Simultaneously, civilian groups attacked certain
electric power stations but failed to cause any serious
damage.
The Japanese public was naturally stunned by these acts
but the effect which the perpetrators desired was not pro-
duced.
Finance Minister Takahashi immediately became Pre-
mier pro tem. The only surviving genro, Prince Saionji,
was summoned ano it was finally decided to appoint Admiral
Viscount Saito as Premier of a new national government. The
various offenders either gave themselves up to the police or
were soon arrested. After several days a press ban was
placed upon the publication of news concerning the police
investigations. Little was heard for a year, until the be-
ginning of the public trials of the participants - those of
ten naval officers on July 24, those of eleven military
cadets on July 25, and those of twenty civilians on Septem-
ber 26, 1933.

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
The Trials of the "Blood Brotherhood"
The public trials of fourteen members of the Blood
Brotherhood began on June 28 and continued until July 28
when they were interrupted by a dramatic challenge of the
presiding judge made by Nissho Inouye, the leader of the
accused. They lasted, however, long enough to produce
very interesting evidence regarding the mentality of the
membersof the Blood Brotherhood and the motives for their
crime. The st tements of the young men on trial are rend-
ered more significant by the sympathy which is expressed for
them by large sections of the Japanese people. Persons who
criticize their actions laud their motives. In the country
districts it is understood that the masses go further than
feeling mere sympathy for them. Large sections of the Em-
peror's subjects actually share the political and social
beliefs of the accused, which the more sophisticated people
of the cities consider to be based largely on ignorance.
Nearly all the men on trial are in their early twenties.
One exception is the alleged leader, Teru Inouye, also known
as Nissho Inouye, who is 47 years of age. For many years
prior to 192l he was an army secret agent stationed princi-
pally in Manchuria. He returned to Japan in that year and
soon became interested in the doctrines of the Nichiren sect
of Budihism. For one year (1928-9) he secluded himself in
the mountains studying th Nichiren prayer.
According to the statements made in court by one of
the other defendants, the Nichiren sect places more emphasis
on practice than on prayer. It believes in a Spartan manner
of living. The members are a serious set of people and sub-
1ect

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
ject themselves to many hardships of living in order to
prove their "sincerity". According to the evidence submitted
at the trial the "prophet" Nichiren taught his followers that
they should fight with the sword to save the nation, that
not a single unhappy person should exist, and that one can-
not save one's self alone but must save the whole nation.
"The height of Budohism is sensitiveness to nature..... The
compassion of the samurai is the natural and spontaneous
sentiment of the human being. It is a sentiment quite dif-
ferent from vhat is implied in 'Love your enemy'." - such are
some of the statements of the beliefs of the Nichiren sect
as stated during the trial of Adashi Konuma, aged 23, the
actual assassin of former Finance Minister Inouye.
Nearly all the accused stated that Nissho Inouye was
their inspiration and guide. He himself assumed the blame
for the plans to assassinate not only Finance Minister Inouye
and Baron Dan but also nearly a dozen other prominent leaders
for the purpose of doing away with the present system of
government and restoring Japan to the ancient "Imperial Way".
The reasons for their dissatisfaction with the present sys-
tem may be summarized as (1) the corruption of thepolitical
parties, (2) the oppression of the privileged classes,and
(3) the tendency among the general public to seek pleasure.
During the trial the accused constantly referred to the manner
in which the rulers allowed Japan to suffer the loss of her
dignity at the London Naval Conference. It appears that Japan's
acceptance of a naval limitation below that of the United
States and Great Britain was considered by all of them a form
of national humiliation and was for many of them the immediate
cause

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
cause for turning against the Government.
The leader, Nissho Inouye stated as follows:
" I am not thinking much about the building of the
state. However, party politics in Japan disregard
the politics of the country; it is specially privi-
leged politics. Politics of the right wing in Ger-
many is politics of the god. In order to overcomè
the present deadlocked condition of Japan, tnere is
a necessity to reform and correct party politics
which disregard the constitution of the nation. The
genro system should be abolished. The Emperor should
give the final word. Matters should be left to the
Emperor's decision. Political parties are to be blamed
for the high-handed manner in which matters are carried
out by the privileged class. Another important thing
is to borrow the influence of religion."
Kenji Mori, 22, intended slayer of Premier Inukai, when
giving his evidence, shouted his denunciation of "the ex-
ploitation of the masses by the capitalists and the degrada-
tion of politicians". He stated that "destruction must pre-
cede construction". Takeshi Hoshiko, 25, a law graduate
at the Kyoto Imperial University, said "in order to live
one must save others first". He explained that he wanted
to save the working class and then proceed to the enforce-
ment of a strict code of social measures.
Most of the accused stressed their loyalty to the Em-
peror. Kishiro Suda, 26, who was assigned to kill Prince
Iyesato Tokugawa, at that time President of the House of
Peers and descendant of the ancient Shoguns, stated that the
abdication of the Shogun had not erased his "deed of dis-
loyalty" to the Emperor. Suda's desire was to assassinate
Prince Tokugawa because of the centuries during which his
ancestors deprived the Emperor of power.
On July 28, just as Judge Sakamaki began questioning
one of the defendants, Nissho Inouye leaped to his feet and
challenged him, shouting "Does not the judge recognize the
necessity

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
necessity of no more questions for the time being so that
he may hear the true feelings we had?.... I challenge the
presiding judge.... he has not been thorough in preparation
for this trial and has not watched the proceedings carefully
.... The judge seems to think these fellows are simple....
concentrates only on legal interpretations and has no proper
understanding of the defendants as human beings." He then
turned on the assistant judge an challenged him also. A
canvass of the prisoners was hel and all except three ex-
pressed themselves as in agreement with Inouye. The judge
then "rejected" the challenge but stated that it could be
carried to the Court of Appeal.
This amazing incident was followed by one which as-
tounded foreign observers even more. The press reported
that the Judge had visited Inouye in his cell to pray him
to withdraw his challenge. This being refused the challenge
was carried to a higher court and it was only on September
7 that the latter handed down a decision rejecting the ap-
peal. It is unerstood that the intricacies of Japanese
court procedure will prevent the public trial being resumed
for some time.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
-10 -
The Courtmartial of the Military Cadets.
The public courtmartial of the eleven military
cadets involved in the May 15th Incident, which included
the assasination of Premier Inukai and the attack on
various builcings in the city, began on July 25. They
were all accused of rebellion. During the trials all of
them showed that they were imbued with an intense patriot-
ism but their speeches lacked the philosophic touch charac-
teristic of the testimony given by the members of the
Blood Brotherhood.
They were all very young men, between
22 and 25 years of age, and had received a different kind
of training from that of the civilians. Furthermore their
part in the affair had been only to follow the directions
given them by their naval colleagues. Some modern psycho-
logists might say that they were motivated partly by a de-
sire to obtain glory comparable to that accorded the 47
ronin famous in Japanese history; but to the layman their
motive seemed to be a sincerely patriotic desire to reform
the government. During the trials each declared that he
continued to believe in the righteousness of his acts and
each asked that the death penalty be inflicted upon him.
The first cadet to be questioned, Akinori Goto, said
"Let the attempt be carried on by one after another through
seven gener:tions".
He then expressed regret that his ac-
tion had caused apprehension to the Emperor and said that
he expected to receive the death sentence in punishment for
this. He asserted that the group wished by their acts to
arouse potential leaders to save the nation in its crisis.
He said that they were impressed by the suffering among
the agricultural population in their native district and
they

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
they believed that the national crisis had its roots in
the "degradation of the privileged classes"; that the
politicians had done nothing to relieve the distress in
the agricultural districts, from which most of the army
comes.
Ichinosuke Shinohara commenced his testimony with
the same assertions of not having changed his mind during
the period since may 15, 1932 and of being willing to ac-
cept the death penalty on account of the anxiety which his
acts caused the Emperor. He professed himself to be a
close student of the Constitution and of Chinese-Japanese
relations. He blamed the Government for obstructing the
establishment of Greater Asia by aggravating the relations
between China and Japan. He criticized the attitude of the
United States toward Japan. He attacked the London Naval
Treaty and the Japanese delegation which negotiated it.
Haruo Yagi, after reiterating the beliefs already ex-
pressed by his confederates, said that he had been interest-
ed in the Korean question and he blamed the Japanese admini-
stration for the anti-Japanese and communistic thought of
the Korean masses. Another defendant attacked the student
classes of Japan, accusing them of idling away their time
and ignoring national affairs. Another stated that in
order to establish a continental policy in the Far East the
evil influence of the United States, the Soviet Union, and
Great Britain in China must be eliminated.
Kenichi Sakamoto told the court that the conspirators
were divided into two groups, one to raid the house of Pre-
mier

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
- 12 -
mier Inukai and the other to attack the residence of Count
Makino, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal. It was planned that
after the assaults were carried out the two groups were to
unite
and call at the residence of Fleet Admiral Count
Heihachiro Togo and ask him to wait on the Throne with a
plea for a declaration of martial law. They planned then to
summon Seikyo Gondo, a contemporary scholar noted for his
imperialistic doctrines, to come to the official residence
of the War Minister to direct the work of reconstructing
the state machinery. It appears th't the latter part of
the program was not carried out because the first part did
not throw the city into as much confusion as had been anti-
cipated.
The motive most often mentioned by the military cadets
in explaining their actions on May 15 was the corruption
among the politicians and "privileged classes".
The army
is recruited largely from the country districts where pover-
ty reigns. The statements of the cadets are generally held
to represent the opinions of the class from which they come.
They resent the growing wealth of the industrial classes
and blame their own condition on the corruption among the
politicians of which they read. This explains the general
sympathy which their case has aroused in the country districts
of Japan. The War Minister has been deluged with petitions
for clemency for them.
The estimates of the total number
are all over 50,000.
On August 19, standing near a glass jar containing
the severed little fingers of nine sacrificial sympathizers
with the cause of the defendants, the Procurator asked the
Court to impose a punishment of eight years imprisonment on
the eleven cadets. He ackncwledged their patriotism and, as
further

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
- 13 -
further justification for a light punishment, he conceded
that they had not participated in planning or directing the
affair but had been led by naval officers. However, he
maintained that they ha committed the worse crime known
to the Army Code, that of rebellion, and were subject to
terms of servitude. In addition, he pointed out that their
acts had caused great anxiety to His Majesty the Emperor
and this could never be excused.
For sevoral days after the delivery of the Procurator's
speech the lawyers for the defense pleaded for the acguit-
tal of the cadets. They used every conceivable argument
from references to the famous 47 ronin to assertions that
assumption of power by the present national cabinet just
after the incident occurred was a move toward the kind of
government desired by the defendants.
They denounced the
politicians and the "privilegec classes" and praised the
high-mindedness of the accused. The attorneys were follow-
ed by several former superior officers of the accused who
pleaded on their behalf.
They stated that the threat of
war hangs over Japan and that the cadets were inspired by
the highest and purest patriotism. One of them stated that
the defendants resorted to direct action with the sole object
of "rehabilitating the country in the face of the threaten-
ing situation abroad".
The feelings of the people were given time to become
more quiet before sentence was imposed. A month elapsed
during which little was heard of the army trials and finally
on September 19 the Court imposed sentences of four years
imprisonment

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.98)
- 14 -
imprisonment on the eleven cadets. In delivering sen-
tence the Court stressed the self-sacrificing spirit of
the defendants and the fact that they were not the leaders
in the plot. The sentence was very favorably received by
the friends of the defendants and by the public in general.
It was generally recognized that some sort of punishment
would have to be imposed but that, for political reasons,
it could not be severe. Sympathizers in the agricultural
districts undoubtedly thought that the cadets should be
acquitted but the trials dragged or so long that their
enthusiasm geve out before sentence was passed.

Despatch No. 565.
Date: 0ctober 31, 1933.
Subject: Report on the Reactionary Trials.
Summary:
There is transmitted with this despatch part
of a report on the trials of the Reactionaries who
committed a series of terroristic acts during the first
half of 1932. On February 9 of that year former Fi-
nance Minister Inouye was assassinated. On March 5,
Baron Dan of the Mitsui organization met the same fate
at the hands of a native of the same village from which
came the assassin of Mr. Inouye. It was found by the
police that they were only two out of a number marked
for the same fate by a reactionary society called the
"Blood Brotherhood". Finally on May 15 a series of
terroristic acts were carried out under the leadership
of a group of young naval officers who were assisted
by some military cadets and some civilians, resulting
in the assassination of Premier Inukai.
The principal causes of these affairs were dis-
satisfaction with the political parties on the part of
the people in the agricultural districts as well as
those in the armed services and dissatisfaction with
the London Naval Treaty on the part of the naval of-
ficers.
The public trials of those involved, which took
place more than a year after the crimes were committed,
were stretched out over many months and were given
tremendous publicity. It is believed that the Govern-
ment hoped that by letting the defendants air their
beliefs they would be made to look ridiculous. This
purpose was not accomplished to any great extent as a
large part of the public appear to sympathize with the
accused. The trials have had the effect of discredit-
ing the political parties and strenthening the opposi-
tion to the London Naval Treaty and future projects for
naval limitation.
The report transmitted with this despatch covers √
the uncompleted trials of themembers of the Blood Bro-
therhood and the trials of the military cadets. The
report on the trials of the naval officers will proba-
bly be transmitted in the next pouch.

Sir:
FILED
4921
With reference to my despatch No. 565/of October
31, 1933, transmitting part of a report entitled The
Trials of the Reactionaries Involved in the Incidents
1/.
of 1932", I have the honor to enclose the section of
the report describing the courtmartial of ten naval
sub-lieutenants involved in the terroristic acts of
May 15, 1932 which included the assassination of Pre-
mier Inukai.
The trials have only recently been completed, the
sentences

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
sentences having been announced on November 9. Two of
the defendants were sentenced to fifteen years imprisonment
without labor, one to thirteen years, three to ten years,
three to two years, and one to one year imprisonment. The
four men included in the last two groups were granted stays
of execution which permit their enjoying their freedom on
good behavior.
As thenaval officers instigated the plans for the
terroristic acts of May 15, 1932, and were the leaders in
the affair special interest was attached to their trials,
which commenced at the end of July. During the entire month
of August and the first half of September the newspapers of
Japan were full of the sensational statements made by them
before the court. As news was very scarce it may be said
that these trials furnished the public with its principal
reading matter during that period.
The main topic discussed by the naval officers was the
London Naval Treaty. They bitterly denounced the treaty
and the Japanese officials who negotiated, signed, and rati-
fied it. In fact, the statements of the defendants in the
courtmartial started the recent nationwide discussion of
the London treaty and the next naval conference which has
been described in other despatches of this Embassy. One of
the defendants stated that he and his associates were deter-
mined to take "direct action" in order to"bring the nation
to its senses" before the next conference. The "outbursts"
of the defendants fully prepared the public for the "warn-
ings" of the jingoists and constituted military preparedness
propaganda of the first order. The Army was provided with
strong

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
strong support for its theory of an approaching crisis
1n 1935-36.
As a result of the trials the general public links
the present naval limitations with corruption in politics
and other evils denounced by the defendants. In gauging
Japanese opposition to continuance of the present naval
ratios careful consideration must be given to this fact.
It may prove possible for the representatives of western
nations to win over some Japanese officials to their point
of view but the latter are not apt to forget what happened
to some of those responsible for the London Treaty. There
are reactionary leaders who are ready to sacrifice their
lives for what they consider to be the honor of Japan and
they are supported by a public which has now been thoroughly
imbued with their ideas.
On the other hand it must be recognized that the same
apparent fearlessness exists in some of the Japanese liberals.
There is a story now going the rounds of Tokyo that Baron
Wakatsuki, the chief Japanese delegate to the London conference,
when leaving his home recently to deliver a speech in de-
fense of the naval treaty, summoned all the members of his
family and bade them farewell, saying that he would probably
not return alive. The story is probably true, though it
cannot be verified. It has taken considerable courage for the
former liberal premier to make several of his recent speeches,
especially the one made at Nagoya where he tried to counter-
act some of the influence of the defendants in the court-
martial.
There are people in Tokyo who believe that the cause
of the militarists will suffer a set back as a result of
the

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the sentences offifteen years imprisonment which were
the maximum imposed upon the army and navy men. The man
who in 1930 fired the shots at Premier Hamaguchi which
were adjudged to have caused his death six months later
was recently sentenced to death. The fact that the slayers
of Premier Inukai received punishment of so much milder a
nature may convince the people that the military have gone
too far in creating for themselves a privileged position.
Japanese liberals, who are incensed at the verdict, hope
that this will be the case. It is reported that several
civilian members of the Cabinet have privately expressed
their dissatisfaction with the light penalties inflicted
upon the military and naval men and that it will strengthen
their opposition to the budgetary demands of the armed ser-
vices.
It is yet too soon to state whether the penalty im-
posed by the civil court will be generally considered by
the public to be too severe or the sentences of the court-
martials too lenient. However, it is certain that in re-
ducing the death penalties demanded by the naval procura-
tor to fifteen years imprisonment the courtmartial averted
serious trouble within the navy itself. The procurator's
demand evidenced an attempt to impose a more severe penalty
but the outcry from the young naval officers was too strong.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
The Courtmartial of the Naval Officers.
While the trials of the members of the "Blood Bro-
therhood" held the attention of the public during July,
the courtmartial of the ten naval officers who planned
and led the May 15th Incident when Fremier Inukai was
assassinated provided the sensational news of August.
The courtmartial of the Army cadets, which has been des-
cribed, was of great intereet to people in the country
districts whose mentality the cadets typified. However,
the naval court proceedings were of greater general in-
terest, partly because the defendants were probably of
a slightly higher type of mentality and partly because
the naval officers were the leaders in organizing and
directing the affair.
Five of the naval officers were accused of rebellion
and five of attempted rebellion. With the exception of
one, a lieutenant of 35 years of age, all were sub-Lieu-
tenants of ages ranging from 25 to 29. According to the
Procurator's opening speech and other evidence produced
during the trial, the original leader in planning the ter-
roristic program was the late Lieutenant Commander Fujii
who was killed in the Shanghai fighting early in 1932. He
preached the theory of Greater Asia, with Japan leading
the other Asiatic peoples in throwing off the domination
of the white race. He and two of the accused, Kiyoshi
Koga and Kakushi Murayama, worked hard to interest other
persons in their ideas. They believed that in modern
Japan, the party statesmen, the wealthy, and the "privileged
classes"

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
classes" were all corrupt and had no patriotic sentiments.
As long ago as 1930 they were planning to take "direct
action" for a national reform. Fujii was killed on Febru-
ary 5, 1932, but the others carried his plans forward.
The plan as finally formulated was to form four groups.
On the appointed day one would attack the Premier's offi-
cial residence; a second would attack the official residence
of the Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, Count Makino; a
third would attack the Seiyukai Party headquarters; and a
fourth would attack the Mitsubishi Bank. Following these
moves the first three groups would together attack the
Metropolitan Police Board and the fourth would attack
several power stations. By carrying out this plan they
hoped to assassinate the Premier and Count Makino; to fight
a decisive battle with the police, destroying its power
and revealing its powerlessness; to throw the capital into
darkness; to arouse the people and bring about the procla-
mation of martial law; and, by these acts, to show the peo-
ple that law and order could only be maintained by the mili-
tary. They all stated during the trials that they had ex-
pected to be killed during the affair and had written fare-
well letters to their families.
As related elsewhere in this report the naval officers
in cooperation with a group of army cadets and a group of
civilians succeeded in killing the Premier, but their several
attacks upon public and semi-public buildings and institutions
did not create as much confusion as was intended and failed
to bring about the effects desired by the conspirators.
The first defendant to testify was Kiyoshi Koga. He
stated

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
stated that destruction, not the destruction of nihilism,
but destruction with full confidence of reconstruction and
the realization of better conditions was the ideal and
driving power of the rebellious group in taking a hand in
the nationalist conspiracy. The institutions which were
attacked were, he said, symbols of corrupt policies and
their destruction was necessary as the first step toward
reform and purification. Koga did not explain very satis-
factorily why he, as leader of the group which threw hand
grenades at Count Makino's residence, changed the plans
at the last moment and did not try to kill the Lord Keeper
of the Privy Seal. He denied the rumors that he had been
bribed to spare the life of Count Makino and asserted that
the reason for his decision was that the party had arrived
there late and he thought it more important to get on to
the Metropolitan Police Board where the great fight was
expected to take place.
The second defendant to be questioned was Yoshio Naka-
mura. He stated that the present party system of government
was more tyrannous than the old Shogunate and "has brought
extreme disgrace on Japan as a country ruled by the Emperor".
He said that he had been aroused especially by the great
number of scandals among the politicians who had been hold-
ing office. The plotters wished to get rid of Count Makino
because they considered him an undesirable influence near
the Throne. Nakamura stated that they thought that their
action would bring into power a coalition Cabinet with
General Araki and Baron Hiranuma as key members, without any
simulation of the western system but established purely
on

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
on the guiding spirit of "Yamato Damashii", the essence
of Japanese patriotism.
Taku Mikami, the leader of the group which attacked
the Premier's residence, bitterly criticized certain naval
authorities and particularly those responsible for the
negotiation of the London Naval Treaty. He attacked Baron
Wakatsuki, the leader of the Japanese delegation at London,
and Admiral Takarabe. He stated that he had planned to
kill the latter upon his return from London but had re-
frained, thinking that a better effect would be produced
if a group did it. He said that in 1928 and 1929 as a re-
sult of reading many books on the Meiji Restoration he
became convinced of the necessity of a national reconstruc-
tion and that he had especially never liked the spirit domi-
nating the chiefs of the navy. At the time of his first
cruise he thought that the relations between the officers
and men were too formal for effective cooperation at a
time of emergency. He believed that most of the higher of-
ficials of the navy were corrupted by their contacts with
politicians and plutocrats. He stated that the aim of their
revolution was to bring about direct Imperial rule and har-
mony between the ruling and the ruled; that the plutocrats
inconsistent with their aims were to be overthrown; that
"our conviction is that the meaning of the National Constitu-
tion must be clarified on a basis of the fundamental policy
of the Empire"; that those opposed to this must be removed;
and that insofar as the conspirators desired to establish
direct "Imperial Rule" they were neither Right nor Left.
Mikami said that he did not commit suicide after the May 15th
Incident because he feared that after his death the true
intention

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
- 19 -
intention of the conspirators would not be understood.
As the trials progressed it became more and more ap-
parent that the London Naval Treaty was the immediate
cause of most of the plotting among the naval officers.
Some defendants confessed that they had planned the as-
sassination of Mr. Hamaguchi who was Premier at that time
and who was shot by a man outside the group on November
30, 1930, subsequently dying from his wounds. As time
progressed themost radical of the discontented officers
in the navy got together under the leadership of Lieute-
nant Commander Fujii and the plan to commit wholesale as-
sassinations was the result. They all knew Nissho Inouye,
the leader of the Blood Brotherhood. They were inspired
by his plans and the beginning made by his organiza-
tion in the killing of Finance Minister Inouye and Baron
Dan and they we e determined to do what the members of the
Blood Brotherhood had failed to do, - i.e. to bring about
a national reform by means of "direct action".
Isamu Kuroiwa, the one who fired the first shot at
Premier Inukai, introduced the subject of the London Naval
early
Treaty/in the course of the trials. He said that Japan's
ratio was forced upon her by "a certain country" as a means
of promoting its anti-Japanese policy. He stated further,
"When Admiral Takarabe and the other delegates left
to attend the Conference, they resolved not to re-
turn alive if they could not induce the Conference
to accept the Japanese claims ....... They were like
the warriors of the Heiki group who took to flight,
mistaking the noise of sea birds as the sound of an
enemy. .... The worst was to follow. The Hamaguchi
Cabinet endorsed the action of the delegates at Lon-
don.... brought pressure to bear on the Naval General
Staff, thus violating the Supreme Command.... Finan-
cial circles approved the Treaty in view of Japan's
trade relations with the United States.... The Genro
and

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
and the Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal were in
unholy league with the political parties.....
The parties allowed themselves to be influenced
by financial circles.... The signing of the treaty
was the result of a league between political par-
ties itching for power and the Takarabe faction
of the Navy, which also was itching for political
power.n
Hiroshi Yamagishi, who gave the order to fire on the
Premier, contributed toward the explanation of the connec-
tion between the naval officers and the civilians. He de-
nied that Nissho Inouye was the leading figure in the plots.
He said that the members of the Blood Brotherhood were fel-
low thinkers with the naval officers, and nothing more. He
said that the theories of Mr. Seikyo Gondo, a nationalis-
tic writer, inspired him and his associates but that Mr.
Gondo was not considered a member of their group. He reiter-
ated that the London Naval Treaty was the immediate reason
for the plot and said that the plotters struck when they
did because they thought that somet ing had to be done be-
fore the treaty should expire.
Kakushi Murayama told the court that abolition of pluto-
cracy in Japan, restriction of the system of private owner-
ship, and penetration of Manchuria were among the "Reforms"
desired by the plotters. He said that, during the year of
his imprisonment since the incident, he had prayed daily
for Premier Inukai, Finance Minister Inouye, and Baron Dan,
hoping that their deaths would contribute toward the recon-
struction of the country. He bitterly assailed the "privi-
leged class, the political parties, and the plutocracy",
emphasizing especially the "necessity of clearing the Imperial
Court of corrupt elements".
Of interest to Americans is the fact that the conspira-
tors at one time planned to kill Mr. Charlie Chaplin in
the

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
the hopes that his death would start a war between Japan
and the United States, which would strengthen the position
of the military in Japan. According to the testimony of
one of them, their final plans provided for the assassina-
tion of the American Ambassador and the American Consul
General. The witness stated that this would have taken
place during a second series of assassinations planned for
later in the night of May 15. The testimony on this sub-
ject is a little vague as only one witness mentioned the
plans for this "second wave" of assassinations. Evidently
the plans for the later phases of the affair were slightly
vague. Furthermore, it happens that neither the American
Ambassador nor the Consul General were in Japan on May 15,
1932. The reference to them is one ofseveral statements
made by the defendants which indicate that the group was
not in close touch with anyone familiar with happenings in
official circles in Tokyo.
On September 11 the Procurator demanded that the Court
impose the following sentences: the death sentence for Mikami,
Kuroiwa, and Koga, who were the three principals in the af-
fair; life imprisonment for three; six years imprisonment
for three; and three years imprisonment for one, who was in-
cidentally the only defendant over thirty years of age. The
Procurator's address to the Court was well received by the
press and the general public. He expressed great sympathy
for the aims and ideals of the accused but insisted
that as they had violated the law they must suffer the pe-
nalties provided for their respective offenses. He admitted
that their motives were the highest but condemned the view
put

=== Page 336 === (confidence 1.00)
-22-
put forward by the defendants' lawyers that an act of
violence is justifiable if the motive is right. The press
voiced the sympathy of the nation for the defendants but
refrained from criticizing the Government's Procurator.
In fact, the latter's speech was in general lauded as a
model of clear thinking and most editorials stated that
heavy penalties would have to be inflicted in order to
uphold law and order.
The Procurator in his speech made references to a
"certain incident" which influenced the plotters. One of
the accused later expressed regret that the Government did
not see fit to publish the details of "the certain i cident".
More light was thrown on the matter by the ASAHI whie
stated in an editorial of September 12:
"The general belief is that the participation of
Sub-Lieutenant Koga in the 'certain' affair
prompted him to hope to rely on certain powerful
forces to adjust the situation after the institu-
tion of martial law. To this 'certain affair' is
also ascribed the trust which the accused naval
officer came to repose in the power of Dr. Shumei
Okawa and his group, now on trial for outrages com-
mitted early last year. It seems that the plot of
Dr. Okawa and his associates to bring about a reform-
ation of the country through resort to violence
provided the main inspiration to the perpetrators
of the May 15 outrages." ( Dr. Okawa is one of the
civilians under arrest and being held for trial for
complicity in the May 15 affair).
Nothing more is known about this mysterious affair. During
the first half of 1932 Tokyo was agog with rumors of plots
and some of the stories, discredited at the time, may have
been true; or,"the certain incident" may have taken place
several years ago. It is assumed by many that the Govern-
ment's reason for suppressing information concerning "the
certain incident" is that one or more members of the Im-
perial Family were implicated. This would explain Sub-
Lieutenant

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
Lieutenant Koga's belief that the situation on May 15
would be adjusted after martial law had been proclaimed.
The only apparent connection between "the certain incident"
and the May 15th Incident is that, in the former, one or
more influential persons were involved on whom the parti-
pants in the latter thought they could rely during the
later stages of their affair.
Although the press expressed its accord with the de-
mands of the Procurator, it soon became apparent that every-
thing was not right within the navy itself. It was reported
that at least one officer had attempted to commit harakiri
in protest. Within less than a week after the demand was
made the Navy Minister found it necessary to send out a
circular note requesting that all members of the navy "use
the utmost prudence in their speech and conduct". It was
publicly admitted that a furore of protest had been caused
in the Fleet. The Minister had to secure the assistance
of Fleet Admiral Heihachiro Togo who emerged from retire-
ment to the extent of issuing a statement to the navy re-
questing it to remain calm. The Minister assured the navy
that "the sacred courtmartial would never fail to pass a
fair judgment".
The naval courtmartial did nothing for more than a
month. During this time public agitation over the trials
began to subside. The trials of the civilians involved in
the May 15th Incident proceeded but appeared to arouse lit-
tle general interest. Then the Civil Supreme Court, which
had been considering the appeal of Sagoya, the assassin of
former Premier Hamaguchi, refused to reverse the death sen-
tence

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
- 24 -
tence imposed by a lower court and many considered this
to mean that a severe penalty would be imposed by the
naval courtmartial on the leaders in the assassination of
Premier Inukai.
The decision was announced on November 9. The maxi-
mum penalty was fifteen years imprisonment without labor
and was imposed upon only two of the defendants, Mikami
and Koga. Kuroiwa, the third, for whom the death penalty
was demanded by the Procurator, was sentenced to thirteen
years imprisonment. The three for whom sentences of life
imprisonment were asked were sentenced to ten years im-
prisonment. Of the other four defendants, three were
sentenced to two years imprisonment with five years stay
of execution and one to one year imprisonment with two
years stay of execution. The last four were released a
few days later and will be allowed their freedom as long as
they behave themselves during the periods of five and two
years respectively.
The sentences constituted a victory for the reaction-
aries. Japanese liberals are in general disgusted. Ap-
parent to all is the discrepancy between the penalty im-
posed by the civil court on the assassin of Premier Hama-
guchi, whc did not die for several months after he had been
shot, and the sentences given the assassins of Premier
Inukai, who unquestionably died from the effects of the
bullets. Of course the naval courtmartial was influenced
by conditions in the navy. It is not doubted that serious
trouble would have arisen if the three naval officers had
been sentenced to death. In passing sentence the naval
courtmartial considered themotive of the crime while the
civil court tried to uphold law and order by stating that
"the

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.98)
-25 -
"the fixing of the penalty should not be based upon
motive".
The first reaction of the press was to express the
warning against too much emphasis being placed upon the
purity of the motives of the accused as it might encourage
others to commit the same crimes. Japanese newspapermen
are personally very critical of the sentences but they are
not yet bold enough to express their "anti-military" views
in writing. Many of them predict privately that the mild-
ness of the sentences will react against the armed services.
However, all recognize that tranquility has been restored
in the navy.

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.98)
Despatch No. 581.
Date: November 15, 1933.
Subject:
Transmitting a portion of a Report on the
Trials of the Reactionaries involved in the
"May 15" Incident.
Summary:
This despatch, which is a supplement to Des-
patch No. 565 of October 31, transmits another
section of the report entitled "The Trials of
the Reactionaries Involved in the Incidents
of 1932". The present section covers the trials
C
of the young naval officers who were involved
3
2
in the May 15th Incident. As the terroristic
acts which involved the assassination of Pre-
AN
C
mier Inukai originated with some of the naval
L.
officers special interest was attached to their
trials.
Another reason for the tremendous popular in-
terest which was shown in the naval courtmar-
tial was that the London Naval Treaty was the
main topic discussed by the defendants. They
denounced the treaty and all the Japanese of-
ficials who had anything to do with it. They
furthermore asserted that the principal cause
of their participation in the conspiracy was
the "humiliation" which Japan suffered at Lon-
don and their desire to remove the "corrupt"
politicians from office before the next naval
conference.
The wide publicity which the statements of the
defendants received has served to impress their
ideas upon the Japanese public and has there-
fore constituted excellent military prepared-
ness propaganda. In spite of the demand of
the naval procurator that the death penalty
be inflicted upon the three leading members
of the group, the maximum sentence was fifteen
years imprisonment for two of them. As the
civilian assassin of former Premier Hamaguchi
was recently sentenced to death, Japanese li-
berals hope that the comparative mildness of the
courtmartial sentences will cause a reaction
against the armed services. However, it is
too soon to state that their wish will become
a fact.

SPECLAL REPORT,
By Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant.
SUBJECT: Articles by Robert Poulaine
Dealing With the Far East
Forwarded herewith will be found further
articles addressed from the Far East to LE TEMPS
by Robert Poufaine; one, which appeared on
October 25, 1933, concluding the series on Japan,
and the other, published on November 29, 1933,
dealing with Manchuria.
The former leads up to the following conclu-
sion:
"Even though budget burdens (in Japan) may
be inoreased, it should not be concluded that the
general situation will be compromised. If a threat
were to arise from abroad against Japanese econ-
omics or the national currency, the extraordinary
discipline of this people would assert itself, and
the

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
the Emperor would need only to appeal to the dewotion
of mis subjects, for each one to control his high
temper and impose upon himself severe restrictions."
The article on Manchuria ends with the following
words:
"Not very long ago, prophets were announcing an
inevitable war in the Pacific. I do not wish to yield
to the easy fad of vacillation, but I make bold to
write with a knowledge of facts that in the near
future Manchuria may well become once again a battle-
field."
The originals of these articles are enclosed.
Very respectfully,
wasnunglan Dawnon.
Warrington Dewson,
Special Assistant.

Tokyo's despatch No. 609 of Decem-
ber 14, 1933, contains some very inter-
esting suggestions. The despatch reports
rather briefly on the resignation of
Matsuoka from the Seiyukai and on his
call for the submergence of party strife
to fece the "momentous time". It then
discusses the question of the readiness
of the Japanese for government by parlia-
mentary methods. The despatch points out
that it is the Japanese custom, both in
politics and business, to rely on the
advice and guidance of elders; and the
belief is expressed that the Japanese
Government requires something in the
nature of a balance, "such as a Shogun or
a group analogous to the Genro". The sug-
gestion is nade that perhaps Matsuoka had
this idea in mind in calling for the dis-
solution of political parties.
Saionji, who is referred to in the
report, was never one of the real Elder
Statesmen. He has no great force and has
never attempted to give leadership. He
is, however, extremely astute and he is
well

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
well-informed. He has, therefore, made
it his practice, when acting as an oracle,
to act only as a mirror of public opinion
and to give such advice to the Emperor
which he believes to be the concensus of
opinion. For example, it is understood
on good authority that Saionji advised the
Emperor to sanction Japan's withdrawal
from the League, largely because of the
popular demand for withdrawal, although
he personally did not approve.
The Japanese Government functioned
fairly satisfactorily for ten years or
more without a real Elder Statesman, and
while the need for a "balance-wheel" can
be understood in the present circumstances,
it is not believed that if and when normal
conditions are restored a body of this
sort would be tolerated.
The principal obstruction to a well-
ordered parliamentary system in Japan is,
of course, the lack of consciousness of
the rights of the individual and of popular
initiative, due to the persistence of the
feudal idea that government is bestowed
on the people from ahove.

No. 609.
SUBJECT:
The Resignation of Mr. Matsuoka from

the Seiyukai Party and the Unpopularity
of Party Government in Japan.

JAN 9-1934
Sir:
FILED
The prestige of the Japanese political parties
and the hopes for an early return to party government
have received a setback in the resignation of Mr. Yo-
suke Matsuoka from the Seiyukai Party and from the Im-
perial Diet. The severance of Mr. Matsuoka's ties with
his party was emphasized by a statement which he simul-
taneously issued to the press, in which he proclaimed
anti-parliamentary and reactionary views such as are
popular with a large section of the Japanese public.
Mr.

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Mr. Matsuoka became a wellknown figure abroad as a
result of his vigorous defence of the Japanese Manchurian
policy befo e the Assembly of the League of Nations. At
the same time his popularity at home reached great heights.
His fellow-countrymen looked upon him as a modern Horatius
defending his people against the onslaughts of the world.
His arrival home was the occasion for a public patriotic
demonstration of a size seldom seen in Japan.
After the welcoming ceremonies were over, little was
seen or heard of Mr. Matsuoka for many months. It looked
as if his star was on the wane. However, as he is generally
credited with being ambitious for his own advancement, as
well as intelligent, it is more probable that he was waiting
for what he considered to be the proper moment to appear
before the public again. Having been a reputed
11bera1
for many years in the past, but more recently the staunch
defender of the militaristic Manchurian policy, it is be-
lieved by some people that he has been uncertain which
course would be the more advantageous for him to follow in
the future. Apparently he has now decided to make the most
of his prestige which he gained with the reactionary ele-
ments while he was at Geneva. His resignation from the Sei-
yukai is taken to mean that he does not expect an early re-
turn of party government; that he expects a more reactionary
cabinet to follow the present one and hopes to become a
member of it. Some say that he is too late; that he has
been quiet too long.
At any rate his resignation from his party and from his
geat

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
seat as a Seiyukai member of the Diet made headline news
on December 10. He simultaneously issued a statement in
which he denounced government by political parties and
advocated a "superparty" government. The more important
portions of the statement are as follows:
" At this momentous time when the nation stands
at a crossroad, and will either rise or fall, it
is essential above all things to bring together
in unity all the abilities of the people, there-
by enabling the nation to act as one man.... that
the national crisis may be surmounted. I believe
it necessary that all political controversies and
class strife should be eliminated.... parties im-
pair the harmony of the nation and destroy the ac-
cord of the people.
n
Accordingly it is my earnest hope that the
seasoned and skilled men will free themselves
from the fixed restraints an return to a purely
superparty position. From this viewpoint I urge
that the parties be dissolved.....Western style
party government does not conform to the condi-
tions of our country nor the character of our
people.....To wait for the political parties to
improve is like waiting for pigs to fly......
Dissolution of the political parties does not
mean the end of constitutional government in all
its phases. On the contrary I believe that con-
stitutional government adaptable to our country
can be effected only through the unification of
the nation as one man, without any opposition
between the parties or among factions.
n
The time has come when the Japanese race must
carry out its important mission in the interests
of peace..... As a first step I advocate the dis-
solution of parties."
There have been extensive press comments on the above
statement. The papers have expressed sympathy with Mr.
Matsuoka but have demanded further explanation of his ob-
jective. His denunciation of the parties and his advo-
cacy of constitutional government are termed inconsistent.
Several papers have seized the occasion to defend the
much maligned Imperial Diet. They have argued that in a
nation of sixty million people there must be differences
of

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
of opinion; that it is impossible to congeive that the peo-
ple shoul: be united as one man in expressing their opinions;
and that the Diet with its party alignments does reflect
public opinion. They point out that the existence of the
parties does not prevent cooperation on a non-partizan
basis, as is shown by the present cabinet.
The general unpopularity of party government in Japan,
of which one has heard so much during the last two years, is
due partly to the corruption of so many of the politicians.
The views of the military on this matter have received much
publicity. The best that can be said for the Army as a
governing force is that the mass of its leaders appear so
far to be honest and sincere. This makes a strong appeal to
the mass of Japanese. However, there are signs that a con-
stantly increasing group of army officers are quite satis-
fied with their position in the present government. They
have great influence over every department but little res-
ponsibility, except for the War Department. Therefore their
efforts at the present time are aimed at preserving the ad-
vantage they have gained.
On the other hand, the younger element of the Army and
many of the reactionary groups continue to preach theories
of government which are naive and immature. They advocate
a return to the "Imperial Way". This is a term difficult
to define but it appears that they envisage a return to di-
rect rule by the Emperor. They fail to recognize the fact
that the Emperor would under their proposed system be brought
into all the political squabbles that would certainly develop,
and

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
and eventually lose his prestige in the eyes of the people.
Many students of contemporary history, both Japanese
and foreign, believe that the basic trouble is that Japan is
not yet ready for Parliamentary government. It is question-
ed whether the Japanese are psychologically and temperamen-
tally suited for it. For instance, the members of the Diet
appear to be incapable of debating public questions as in-
telligently and calmly as is done in English-speaking
countries. Moreover, they find it difficult to be dignified
in defeat. Those who beaten in the voting "lose face", which
is hard on any oriental, even a Japanese. Every organiza-
tion in Japan, commercial or otherwise, has its advisers,
who "arrange" matters of great importance for the ort: 1-
zation. Their wisdom is recognized by the more active mem-
bers and their advice is heeded. In the realm of government,
a similar situation existed in the past. From the termina-
tion of the military dictatorship under the rule of the Sho-
guns, until recent years, the Japanese had the Genro, or
Elder Statesmen. These men had the confidence of the Emperor
and of the Cabinet and guided the nation in its development.
However, Prince Saionji is the only one left and he does not
now exercise the power wielded formerly by his colleagues.
Many of those who are familiar with Japanese history
believe that the Government of Japan cannot be carried on
effectiyely without some balance-wheel, such as a Shogun or
a group analogous to the Genro. They believe that one of
these institutions or something which is a mixture of the
two must sooner or later once more find a place in the
Government

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.96)
Government. It is possible that Mr. Matsuoka had some
such idea in mind when he made the comments quoted above.
Respectfully yours,
.
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
Despatch No. 609.
Date: December 14, 1933.
COM.
Subject:
The Resignation of Mn Masguoka from the
Seiyukai Party and the Unpopularity of
Party Government in Japan.
Summary:
Mr. Yosuke Matsuoka, who defended Japan's
Manchurian policy before the League of
Nations, has recently gained considerable
publicity by resigning from the Seiyukai
Party and from his seat in the Diet, and,
simultaneously, issuing a statement de-
nouncing party government for Japan.
Party Government is at present very unpo-
pular in Japan, due seemingly to the cor-
ruption and insincerity of the politicians.
The younger element in the Army and the re-
actionaries advocate a return to the "Im-
perial Way", or direct rule by the Emperor.
Many students of Japanese history believe
that the Japanese are incapable of carrying
on the parliamentary form of government suc-
cessfully and that what the country needs
is a system analogous either to the old
Shogunate or to the Genro. Possibly some-
thing new combining characteristics of them
both will develop.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 11, 1934.
The American Ambassador at Tokyo reports
in his despatoh No. 608 of December 12, 1933,
certain interesting statements made to him by
the Netherlands Minister at Tokyo on certain
foreign policies of the Japanese Government.
The Netherlands Minister, who has served
in Japan in various capacities for a period of
about twenty years, attached great importance
to the so-called Pan-Asiatic movement, and he
is inclined to believe that the plan to
organize the Asiatic countries into a group
under the leadership of Japan has considerable
support in Japan. He was of the opinion that
the Japanese Foreign Minister is a staunch
supporter of the movement and that he is in
sympathy with Japan's expansionist ambitions.
Reports from American consular officers in
the Dutoh East Indies recently received by the
Department indicate that considerable apprehen-
sion is being felt by the Dutch authorities in
the Netherlands East Indies over the growing
popularity of the Japanese among the native
population. A prominent Dutoh official ex-
pressed in confidence to an American consular
officer

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
officer the belief that his Government would
not be able to rely upon the loyalty of the
natives in the event of serious difficulties
which the Netherlands Government might have
with the Japanese Government.
The Netherlands Minister was principally
preoccupied with the "crisis" which the
Japanese believe will confront their country
in 1935. The Minister pointed out to Mr. Grew
that although the Japanese Navy had been
relatively quiet during the critical period
of the Sino-Japanese conflict, it is now very
much to the front, as indicated by the large
appropriation allotted to it in the budget for
the coming fiscal year. As, in the opinion of
the Minister, there was little chanoe of Japan
securing naval parity with the United States
and Great Britain, he thought that there was
great risk of the Japanese seizing Guam and
the Philippine Islands. He did not believe
that the economic consequences of a war with
the United States would aot as a deterrent on
the Japanese Navy.
Commenting on the observations above
outlined of his Dutch colleague, Mr. Grew
stated that he was not inclined to dismiss the
views of an intelligent and conservative
observer. Nevertheless Mr. Grew does not
believe

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.74)
18
I thinh
the Pundat
shoed e
ti.

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
believe that the present Japanese Foreign
Minister is a chauvinist, and he credits the
Foreign Minister with seeking to improve
relations with the United States and Great
Britain as well as with other foreign countrie
However, in Mr. Grew's opinion the Foreign
Minister may be regarded as an ardent
nationalist, and he points out that Mr.
Hirota could not remain in office unless he
were sympathetic with Japan's expansion in
Asia.
Mr. Grew concludes his despatoh by
expressing the need of approaching the coming
year with particular caution.

Confidential for the Secretary and Under Secretary.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir :
It is naturally my habit in Tokyo, as it has been
in other former posts, to keep in close touch with those
Confidential File
of my diplomatic colleagues whom I find by experience to
be well informed and to use their minds constructively
in diagnosing political situations and in trying to fore-
see future developments. Boiled down, the number of
such colleagues is painfully restricted, and lately the
arrival of several new Ambassadors and the absenceon
6
1ea
1934

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
leave of other chiefs of mission, has still further
limited the intelligence market where views, information
and ideas can profitably be exchanged.
Among the co1-
leagues with whom I maintain frequent contact and whose
information and opinion I find to be generally accurate
and intelligent is the Netherlands Minister, General
Pabst.
A former officer in the Dutch Army, he has been
Minister in Tokyo since 1923 and has therefore had longer
experience in Japan than any other chief of mission save
only the Belgian Ambassador, Dean of the Diplomatic Corps,
who came in 1921.
General Pabst possesses the phlegmatic
hard-headedness and common sense typical of the Dutch and
is not at all of the type prone to find sensation where
none exists.
Furthermore, as a former army officer and
Dutch Military Attaché here, he has intimate contacts with
the Japanese Army and Navy not generally enjoyed by the
diplomats in Tokyo. I mention these facts merely as
background in connection with various comments which he
has recently made to me in the course of our talks, as
set forth below.
General Pabst attaches much importance to the Pan-
Asiatic movement in Japan and to the steps being taken to
revise and develop the Great Asiatic Association which
790.94/35
were initiated last March (Embassy's despatches Nos. 3l1
790.9447
of March 7, 1933, and 468 of July 13, 1933). He believes
that Mr. Hirota, the present Minister for Foreign Affairs,
in spite of his policy of improving relations with foreign
countries, is a staunch supporter of the movement, in the
initiation of which he participated, and that he is in
General
sympathy with Japan's expansionist ambitions.
Pabst

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Pabst acknowledges, however, that he has no precise
evidence to support this theory. The idea, as he
understands it, is to develop an Asiatic League of
Nations composed of Japan, as the leader, and "Manchukuo",
China and Siam, in other words, a bloc of the yellow races
against the white. He said that he had recently been
looking at a new text-book just issued to the Japanese
primary schools in which is included a large map of the
.Far East, comprising Japan, "Manchukuo", Siberia, China,
French Cochin China, Siam, the Straits Settlements, the
Philippines and the Dutch East Indies, and that on this
map there are printed three flags, those of Japan, China
and "Manchukuo".
The American, French, British, Russian
and Dutch flags are conspicuous by their absence.
The
Minister said that he had observed a significant thing at
the funeral of Princess Asaka on November 12, namely that
in the row of funeral wreaths the place of honor was given
to the wreath from President Pu Yi, the second place of
honor was accorded to that of General Tinge, the Minister
of "Manchukuo" to Japan, while the wreath from the entire
Diplomatic Corps was relegated to third place.
His in-
terpreter had read the Japanese inscriptions.
The Minister's chief concern, however, is in connec-
tion with the "crisis" which the Japanese, especially the
Army and Navy leaders and the press, are continually harp-
ing upon as certain to occur in 1935. As regards the
factor of Soviet Russia in this impending crisis, he
entirely concurs in the views set forth in my strictly
confidential letter to the Under Secretary of State of
October 6, 1933. But what gives the Minister most concern
is the

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4-
is the possible attitude and action of the Japanese
Navy if and when it becomes evident in the exploratory
conversations preliminary to the Naval Conference in
1935 that Japan presumably cannot hope both for an
agreement according to Japan neval parity with the
United States and Creat Britain and at the same time
for a continuance of the restrictions against e forti-
fied naval base for the United States in the Philippines.
In the General's opinion there will be grave risk at that
time that the Japanese Navy will suddenly descend upon
and occupy Guam.
"You have noticed," General Pabst
seid in effect, "that the Navy is no longer content to
take a back seat as it was more or less obliged to do
during the campaign of the Army in Manchuria.
The Navy
is now forging to the front as is clearly evidenced by
the immense appropriations accorded it in the present
budget, partly at the expense of the Army, and in the
great naval maneuvres scheduled for next year, the first
time that such maneuvres have occurred in two consecutive
years.
If, at a given moment, the Navy feels that its
prestige is at stake and that the future safety, welfare
and ambitions of the country lie in its hands, it will
no more be subject to restraint by the civil Government
than was the Army in Manchuria in 193l." On my referring
to the utter stupidity and probable inconclusiveness of
an American-Japanese war to which such action would in-
evitably lead, and the tremendous loss to Japanese trade
which would result from such a war, the Minister said,
in effect:
"Don't for a moment think that questions of
economic or financial loss to the country would carry
any

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
一 5 -
any weight whatsoever with the Navy; the present budget
is already likely to bring the country perilously near
to financial disester in future, but the Army and Navy
go cheerfully ahead with their plans as if the country's
financial status is a matter with which they are not
concerned and cannot afford to be bothered, and that
economically Japan could get along without America some-
how or other if she had to do so. Furthermore from the
military and naval point of view, an American offensive
would be a negligible factor, because they hold that no
American fleet could effectively operate in Japanese
waters so far from its Hawaiian base and with its lines
of communication liable to be intercepted.
Once in
possession of the Philippines, with unrestricted naval
building in view and having eliminated the dreaded threat
of an American naval base in eastern waters, Japan believes
that she could disregard the United States and proceed with
her Asiatic ambitions.
The next step would be the approp-
riation of New Guinea which offers a considerable extent
of territory for colonization and would, to a certain
extent, solve the immediate problem of Japanese emigration."
The foregoing is not a precise quotation of General
Pabst's remarks but represents closely the general trend
of his ideas as he expressed them in our last conversation.
There is no doubt that he has the future safety of the
Dutch East Indies constantly in mind, of which the future
safety of the Philippines is a corollary. He alluded with
emphasis to the fact that the Japanese had recently in-
vited a delegation of natives from Java to visit Japan,
with a view to broadening the Japanese market in cheap
cotton

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
cotton and other goods in the East Indies, and that
this delegation is now here.
After a stay of but eighteen months in Japan I
should be loath to characterize as fantastic the con-
sidered opinions of an intelligent and conservative ob-
server with ten years' experience of this country and
its people.
We have already seen the sort of action
of which an unrestrained and domineering army is capable.
We have had ample evidence that in this country the un-
expected can always happen.
In previous despatches I
have pointed out the uneasiness with which certain of the
Japanese regard the year 1935-1936, and the determination
to alter the present naval ratios.
The prestige of the
Japanese Navy has for a time been eclipsed by the spot-
light in which the Army has moved during the past two
years.
Prestige is a powerful factor in the Orient.
So far as the present Foreign Minister is concerned,
it is safe to say that he is not a chauvinist, that he
believes it in the best interests of Japan to be on
cordial terms with all foreign countries, especially the
United States, China and Great Britain, and that he does
not believe in provoking war in any quarter.
.He is
therefore steadily working to develop better relations
abroad because he believes that Japan can carry through
her program far more readily and effectively in an at-
mosphere of friendship than in one of hostility with other
nations.
At the same time, he is what might be called an
ardent nationalist.
His views, from all that I can gather,
are that politically, economically and culturally, Japan
can

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
can prove herself the leading nation in Asia.
The
recent growth of Japanese trade throughout the Far East
naturally gives added prestige to the country.
.Mr.
Hirota is undoubtedly sympathetic with Japanese develop-
ment, and will give what encouragement and help he can
to any movements which tend to enhance the country's
position. He could not hold office on any other terms.
I am unable to state to what extent he approves of the
recent adventures in Manchuria. Here again he must, if
he is to remain in office, accept the si tuation as he
finds it, a fait accompli which he is estopped from
criticizing adversely.
To sum up, the Netherlands Minister, an experienced
and conservative observer, believes that the year 1935 is
likely to produce in fact the crisis which is now being
freely predicted in theory, and that the Japanese Navy
at that time is fully capable of taking radical action
such as the Army has already taken in Manchuria.
Having in mind the various points which I have
mentioned in this despatch, it behooves us to approach
the year 1935 in all wakefulness.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 23, 1934.
Mr. Grew's telegram No. 15, January 23,
5 p. m., obviously reflects great care in
estimating the situation as objectively as
possible, but apparently the possibilities
with regard to future developments arising
from the change in the War Office are as
obsoure to him, in Tokyo, as they are to us,
in Washington.
It may be observed by way of comment on
the telegram:
(a) With reference to paragraph one: Any
expeotation of seeing "an important reorienta-
tion of policy" in the near future is not
warranted by what is known of the domestic
Japanese situation! Popular dissatisfaction
against the military is becoming perceptible,
and the withdrawal of General Araki would make
possible minor changes of military policy
without necessarily involving major changes of
polioy.
(b) With reference to paragraph two: The
retirement of older generals and their replace-
ment by younger officers would tend to weaken
the "moderate" element within the Army.
(c) with reference to paragraph four: The
significance of the change will depend largely
upon

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
upon the political views of General Hayashi,
with regard to which we have practically no
information. If the change were designed as a
measure of preparation for war the logical
place for an outstanding military leader would
be in the General Staff and not in the War
Offioe.
(d) In conclusion: The circumstances
which support the belief that the change would
make for a turn towards moderation would seem
to be superior both in point of number and of
weight to the ciroumstances which would support
the belief that the change indicates a
strengthening of the reactionary element.

15, January 23, 5 pm.
Several radically different hypotheses are advanced
in various quarters to account for the resignation yes-
terday of General Araki as Minister of Var, none of which
can yet be confirmed as accurate. His present illness
has furnished the ostensible reason, and it is quite
possible that this is also the actual reason because his
illness deprives the army of a representative in the com-
ing debates in the Diet and there are doubts as to his
physical ability to carry on his work for some time to
come. The following factors and conjectures are, however,
FILED
reing advanced in various quarters.
1
One. There are those who hold that the saner elements
in the Government, realizing the dangerous position into
which Japan is drifting, have taken advantage of Araki's
illness to insist on his removal as the symbol of a
policy of military aggression and that as a result we
may expect to see an important reoriontation of policy.
These

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.99)
Page two No. 15, from Tokio.
These elements believe that Araki was not popular with
the Emperor and that with Hirota's growing strength
Araki's position in the Cabinet had become more or less
isolated. There has also been increasingly wide spread
indignation at the size of the military budget. It is
said that Wakatsuki and Suzuki recently threatened em-
barrassing interpellations in the Diet which would have
placed the army in an unfavorable light before the public
and that they agreed to refrain only on the basis of
Araki's retirement.
Two. There has been dissatisfaction among the young-
er officers in the army at being hindered from promotion
owing to Araki's tendency to retain the older generals.
General Hayashi, the new Minister of War, is a leader
of troops rather than a staff officer and is more likely
to retire the older generals and advance the younger men
with whom he is popular.
Three. Hayashi is said to be of a silent disposition
and is reported to have stated that army officers should
abstain from politics. It is therefore hold that he is
not (repeat not) likely to indulge in Araki's inflamatory
writings and speeches. This gives rise to a beliof that
his appointment representa a victory for the liberal
elements

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.98)
Page throc No. 15 from Tokio.
elcments in the Governmont and a rising influence of
the political parties and that the change of ministers
will have a stabilizing effect in the country.
Four. On the other hand Hayashi is said to bo
sclf-willcd, impetuous and capable of rapid decisions
as when he moved the troops from Korea to Manchuria
on his own responsibility in l93l, in spite of Baron
Shidchara's protests, ic is not (repoat not) concilia-
tory and is less likely than Araki to compromisc.
Thore are, thorofore, those who believe that political
unrest will incrcaso and who sec in his appointment a
stop to prepare for possiblc war.
My present belief is that paragraphs numbered onc,
two and three probably have bearing upon thc actual
situation. The coming debates in the Diet may affora
enlightenment.
Repeated to Peiping.
