=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Paris, January 22, 1954.
Serial No. W. D. 1563.
SPECIAL REPORT,
By Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant.
SUBJECT: Correspondence of LE TEMPS
From The Far East
Continuing its series of letters addressed from
the Far Kast by its special correspondents Robert
Poulaine and F. Chevallier, LE TEMPS published on
Jannary 6th and January 12th, respectively, two further
letters from the latter of these two correspondents.
In the first of these articles, Chevallier
declared that if Japan's policy appeared as an un-
expected series of movements, it should not be con-
cluded that accident or impulsiveness was responsible
for

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
for this. It should be realized that Japan was
now in the grip of what might be called either
evolution or revolution, since opinions differed
on this point, but the fact remained that the
entire nation including civilians as well as
military men and industrialists as well as peasants
acknowledged that changes must be made. For ten
years, Japan had been sound asleep in happiness
and prosperity, caring as little for the immediate
future as for the past. Today, the country has
awekened in the grip of a nightmare composed of
economic, finencial, and political factors whose
reality is too evident for illusions to be possible.
Chevallier considers that the present crisis
is rather of a sociel nature and that its solution
mey be near at hand.
In his second article, Chevallier discusses the
various political parties, promising in the neer
future to examine their respective reections put face
to face with the radicsl changes noted in national
conditions.
He adds that he will approech the question of
the solutions under consideretion only after ex-
amining these preliminary conditions.

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.90)
Very respectfully,
ernglon Dawnoy.
Warrington Dewson,
Special Assistant.

ORIENTATION POLITIQUE ACTUELLE

ORIENTATION POLITIQUE ACTUELLE

On passing through Tokyo today en route from his
post at Seoul to Vancouver, Consul General Davis told
me that in his farewell interview with Lieutenant
3
General Kawashima, in command of the troops in Croseny
2
the General spoke with enthusiasm of the great progres
FEB 2 4 1934
and development in the arts of civilization which had
FILHD
taken place in Japan and added his hope that the op-
pressed and suffering people of Manchuria, China and
Siberia would soon be enabled to share these Heaven-sent
gifts

-2-
gifts. The foregoing is not a precise quotation but
conveys the sense of the General's remarks as repeated
to me by Mr. Davis. They represent, perhaps, the thoughts
of a certain type of Japanese military mind.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

AR 5 1934
March 30,1934.
DIPARINT VT OF STATE
Tokyo's despatch No. 683 of February 21,
1934, reports on the position of the Saito
Government.
It would appear from the despatch that
the recent difficulties of the Saito Govern-
ment are due largely to dissensions in the
Seyukai. If you will omit reading pages 3
4 and the first two paragraphs of page 5, I
think you will find the despatch somewhat
less confusing. It is obvious that the public
in Japan realizes that attacks are being made
on the stability of the Saito cabinet but that
it does not know very much of what is going on
behind the scenes.
The principal point to be emphasized,
however, is that in the opinion of the Embassy
the Government will be able to withstand the
attempts to undermine it.

Sir :
I have already reported the recent attempts in the
Diet to discredit the Saito Cabinet* and the resignation
of Baron Nakajima, Minister of Commerce and Industry**.
FILED
1934
of Education, who is alleged to have accepted a sum .of
¥50,000 from the managing director of the Saghalien
Industrial Company in connection with the sale of certain
farest
*Embassy's despatches Nos. 673, February 19, 1934, and No.
664, February 6, 1934 (Monthly Political Report for Jamuary,
pp. 26, 27, 28, 38-39).
**Embassy's telegram No. 24, February 9, 1934.

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
forest lands in Saghalien, and there are charges that
other members of the present Cabinet were involved in
questionable dealings in transactions of shares held by
the Bank of Formosa. Mr. Hatoyama is said to have been
cleared of any criminal action, but his resignation is
freely predicted, and in certain quarters the early fall
of the entire Saito Cabinet is considered possible.
In examining this situation, the following factors
should be given consideration:
For nearly two years, or ever since the assassination
of Premier Inukai, the political parties have been cowed.
The present session of the Diet as I have previously pointed
out, is the first one since that time in which there has
been any real criticism of the Government.
Intimidation
of the speakers, or the threat of it, has been absent, and
the more timid members have been able to express their views
without fear of reprisels even if their utterances were
not "patriotic" enough to suit the terrorists. Still,
the political parties are out of favor with the public,
which regards them with suspicion as hotheds of corruption,
perhaps not without reason.
Finding that their attacks on the Budget, although
productive of no concrete results, were well received,
some of the members of the Diet have launched attacks on
Ministers of State. There is a certain measure of popu-
larity attaching to onslaughts on members of the Cabinet,
and as this sport has been closed for two seasons, con-
siderable zest has been displayed in its pursuit this
year.
.Besides, there has been some foundation for the
charges levelled.
In

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3-
In l927 there was a series of business failures
which ultimately involved some of the largest banks in
Japan.
The Bank of Taiwan was particularly affected, as
it had advanced money to a number of trading and industrial
enterprises which were unable to pay.
The Government,
through the Bank of Japan, came to the assistance of the
Bank of Taiwan, and in return forced a reorganization and
a drastic writing down of the Bank's assets.
The Bank
retained the stocks and bonds which had been hypothecated
to it, carrying them as assets at decidedly reduced figures.
During the past few years, as the Department is aware,
Japanese business in all lines has been experiencing some-
thing of a boom.
Industrial concerns that were considered
practically worthless have been able to meet their obliga-
tions and show a profit.
. Consequently much of the old
collateral held by the Bank of Taiwan began to have a market
value.
To some extent this was aided by the high values
placed upon some of the privately owned properties which
were to be included in the proposed steel merger.
The
Bank decided to dispose of these holdings, it being stated
that the amounts by which the sale prices exceeded the
values of the bonds and stocks as carried on the Bank's
books were to be added to the surplus.
The sales were
made, not on the open market, or at auction, but by private
arrangement with individual purchasers.
The Bank officials
claimed that the sales were made in this manner to avoid
disturbing the market by dumping large blocks of securities
- a course which Bank officials claimed would have defeated
its own purpose by depressing prices.
This action by the
Bank of Taiwan, added to the high values placed on the
private steel plants for merger purposes (See Monthly
Political

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.98)
一4-
Political Report for January, page 38), caused much un-
favorable comment, as there was a measure of opinion that
the public's interest had not been properly protected by
the Government, particularly by the Department of Commerce
and Industry.
This situation naturally had repercussions in the
Diet. Baron Nakajima, the Minister for Commerce and
Industry, has resigned, and Mr. Hatoyama, as stated above,
is now under fire. It is reported that other members of
the Cabinet will be attacked and that perhaps the whole
Saito Ministry will be compelled to retire.
It is interesting to note, however, that the charge
brought against Baron Nakajima was not corruption or lax-
ness in the discharge of his duties, but something akin
to disrespect for the Imperial House or a disregard of
the teachings of Japanese official history. It seems
that some years ago Baron Nakajima, who at that time had
no political ambitions, wrote a pamphlet on one Ashigaga
Takauji, a prominent figure in Japanese Medieval history,
who has been the subject of much controversy..
matter is of about as much immediate importance as a
dissertation on Benedict Arnold would be in the United
States, but Baron Nakajima took a position which laid
him open to the charge of having publicly supported the
reputation of one who had been branded a traitor to the
Imperial House.
Baron Nakajima, having resigned under a charge of
this absurd character, certain disaffected elements of
the Seiyukai turned their guns upon Mr. Hatoyama, accuaing
him of having accepted a present of some  50,000.
As a
matter

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
matter of fact, it is well known that Mr. Hatoyama has
collected various sums for the Seiyukai at different
times.
The particular transaction complained of took
place some years ago when he was out of office, and it
would appear to have been no more unethical than the
collection of contributions to a political campaign fund
in the United States, since he could not have done much
for the donors in any case.
Nevertheless a select com-
mittee of the House of Representatives will look into his
conduct.
There is a large measure of opinion that the only
purpose of these moves is to embarrass the Government.
It is thought that the Prime Minister will perhaps wait
until the tempest has quieted a little and will then ask
the Seiyukai to name a person to take Mr. Hatoyama's place,
in case his party is dissatisfied with him, but as the
Seiyukai at the moment is split into three factions, it
is believed that such a request would confuse the party
still further, especially as its chief whip, one Mr.
Mochizuki, has resigned, apparently in disgust.
In all
probability, Viscount Saito counts on this situation as
an asset in his favor.
The basic fact seems to be that
certain politicians, seeing that the Diet is powerless to
control policies or legislation, are determined to cause
the Government as much trouble as possible, taking advantage
of certain transactions with which there is public dis-
satisfaction.
The foregoing is cited, not for its intrinsic im-
portance, but to show how difficult it is in many cases
to deduce from their passionate remarks in the Diet the
reasons

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6-
reasons which frequently motivate the behavior of the
members of that body.
Probably the Government would
not have permitted a motion of non-confidence to be made
in regard to Baron Nakajima or Mr. Hatoyama, or it is
possible that many members would have refused to take
part in a motion of that kind for reasons which they would
not discuss.
Such reasons might well be that a consider-
able part of the funds alleged to have changed hands found
their way into the party coffers, and that too much specific
publicity could embarrass the party no less than the in-
dividuals concerned.
Consequently the causes of acti on
have to be guessed, a statement which applies to Japanese
as well as to foreign observers.
Well informed observers
believe, however, that Viscount Saito will not be forced
out of office because of the action of the Diet in regard
to Nakajima and Hatoyama.
There is a feeling that the
political parties have no one who has sufficient prestig
to take his place; that the Army has no one who measures
up to the position, since not even Araki commands its un-
divided confidence, while the Navy's recent large and
truculent demands for money has alienated public sentiment
to an extent that would make the selection from that source
extremely difficult.
These facts would seem to discredit
one rumor commonly heard about town to the effect that the
present attacks on the Cabinet in the Diet have been care-
fully instigated, aided and abetted by the Military for
their own purposes.
Having in mind the foregoing factors, the position of
the Government is still too nebulous to justify a definite
prediction by the Embassy as to the outcome of the attadts
in the Diet.
My present belief is that the present Cabi-
net will weather the storm, although further individual
resignations

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
resignations may occur. Viscount Saito enjoys immense
personal prestige throughout the country and he has safely
withstood the various political efforts to dislodge him
during the past two years, so that there is good reason
to discredit the recurrent rumors of his impending down-
fall. It is doubtful if any other individual could ∞mmand
such general respect or could successfully control the
various opposing political factions. Developments may
occur behind the scenes, however, which may always upset
the most careful calculations. Few Japanese, even in
high positions, are fully aware of what is going on.
In this connection I have the honor to enclose a
memorandum of a recent conversation between Mr. Neville
and a prominent member of the Foreign Office who is trust-
worthy and whose relations with this Embassy are such that
he speaks his mind with complete frankness.
Respectfully yours
No,
Joseph C. Grew.

REPORT OF CONVERSATION, MR. NEVILLE AND MR. KURUSU,
CHIEF OF THE COMMERCIAL BUREAU OF THE FOREIGN OFFICE.
FEBRUARY 18, 1934.
I asked him about the political situation in
general. He said that he could not understand it.
He did not see what the politicians thought they could
accomplish by causing trouble for Hatoyama. The Sei-
yukai had no one, so far as he could see, to replace
him as Minister; if they forced him out, they would
be asked to name some one else, and that would cause
a tremendous row in the party, which is split into
three factions. He said that there seemed to be no
one who could take Saito's place as Prime Minister,
which meant that it would ail have to be settled by
the Prime Linister in the end, and might result in
Saito's picking a completely non-party man to be Minis-
ter of Education. I asked him if the military and the
reactionaries generally might not rise up and scare the
politicians again as they had done in 1932. He said
that he thought not; that the day for successful terror-
ism was over.  A year ago, perhaps, a Government with
Hiranuma or Araki as Prime Minister might have been formed,
but he did not see the possibility at the present time.
He said that for the time being there was nothing to do
but leave it all to Viscount Saito. He then said that
it was difficult for non-Japanese to understand the work-
ings of Japanese politics, which was not strange, as a
good many Japanese did not understand it either. He
said that all the talk about Nakajima and Hatoyama which
appeared in the papers reelly did not mean much. He
said that no one really cared about historical essays
in politicel life, nor was any one interested in Hatoyama's
receipt of money some years before he became a Minister
of State. Every one knew Hatoyama got money for the
Seiyukai's campaign fund. There was some feeling, however,
that the recent steel mergers had placed too high a valua-
tion on some of the private plants; there was much dis-
satisfaction too because the Government had failed to for-
bid the Bank of Taiwan to sell the shares which it had
held in the steel companies end the rayon company. It
seems that some years ago when Suzuki and Company, a large
trading firm, and a number of other concerns, including
some banks, had failed, the Bank of Taiwan had come to
their rescue and had been supported by the Bank of Japan
and the Treasury in avoiding a panic. The Bank of Taiwan
had taken over a lot of bonds and shares in various con-
cerns as collateral. The collateral was not worth much
at the time - in fact, it was really a face saving device.
Vith the recent boom in rayon, and the general business
activity of the past year and a half, these securities
began to have a value. The bank, accordingly, began to
sel1

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
sell these old obligations and stocks and had built up
its reserve fund. The contention was that these bonds
and shares ought to have been sold on the open market or
by public auction.The bank did not do this, the os-
tensible and perhaps the real reason being that to begin
selling on a large scale would have had the effect of
demoralizing the market. Anyway, there had been no
general distribution of what looked like melons, and the
politicians were angry. Accordingly there was trouble.
Still, he said, it was extremely improbable that the
political parties would have control of the Government
again, because the public did not trust them.

Both transmitted portions of a report on the trials of
the reactionaries involved in the terroristic incidents
of the first half of 1932. The first-mentioned despatch
transmitted the Introduction, a short account of the
Crimes which were committed, an account of the Uncompleted
Trials of the "Blood Brotherhood" which succeeded in
murdering former Finance Minister and Baron Dan, and an
account

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
account of the court martial of the Military Cadets
involved in the May 15 Incident. The second despatch
referred to transmitted that portion of the report
deseribing the Court Martial of the Naval Officers
who were involved in the May 15 Incident. I am now
1/
transmitting with this despatch another section of the
report, which describes the trials of the civilians
who were involved in the Incident.
The twenty civilians who have just been sentenced
played a relatively unimportant part in the events of
May 15, 1932. Their leader, Tachibana, had actually
left Japan for Manchuria a few days before. His con-
federates did no more than try to bomb a half dozen
or more electric transformer stations for the purpose of
throwing the city into darkness and confusion and thereby
assisting the work of the military and naval men. In
spite of supposedly detailed and careful preparation,
their attemmpts appear to have been weak and their success
was almost nil.
Their trials began in September just as the sensational
court martial of the naval officers was drawing to a close.
The civilian trials proved to be an anti-climax from a
theatrical point of view. Although Tachibana, their
leader, was the kind of man who might have been expected
to provide the public with sensational statements, his
testimony attracted little attention. The public was
either wearied of the reactionary outbursts of the con-
spirators or else the newspapers provided other more
interesting

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
interesting reading matter.
The only phase of the trials in the civilian
court which attracted attention wus the delivery of
the judgments. The sentences of life imprisonment
for the leader and terms of fifteen years for others
were very much out of proportion to the sentences
of four and fifteen years awarded the military and naval
men by their court martials. This discrepancy started
discussions in the newspapers and the matter has been
commented upon in the Imperial Diet.
The general amnesty granted on February 11 in cele-
bration of the birth of the Crown Prince benefited to a
certain extent the conspirators of the May 15 Incident
and consoled that part of the public which sympathized
most deeply with them.

Joseph C. Grew.

The Trials of the Civilians Involved in the May 15 Incident.
The trials of the 20 civilians involved in the
incident of May 15, 1932 began on September 26, 1933.
Their part in the affair which resulted in the assassina-
tion of Premier Inukai was to attempt to throw the city
into darkness and confusion by bombing the electric
transformer stations which supply current to Tokyo.
They were active on the outskirts of the city, while
the military and naval men were doing their killing
and bombing within the city.
However, the damage done
by the civilians was unimnort nt and the city was not
throvn into the state of confusion desired by them.
A half-dozen transformer stations were attacked.
Several hand grenades never exploded and in other
cases damage was done only to minor apparatus. When
they realized that their attempts had failed deveral
of the participants tried to escape but they were
eventually arrested.
The charges against the twenty civilians varied
between murder, attempted murder, abetment of murder,
and violation of the explosives law.
The leader of the civilians was Kozaburo Tachibana,
40, the head of the Aikyojuku School in the Ibaragi Pre-
fecture. He was of rural origin and his formal schooling
went no further than high school. However, he appears
to have been interested in social problems and for many
years was a professed socialist. He took up farming
in his native prefecture and became intensely interested
in bettering the condition of the fanmers. He gradually
came

=== Page 423 === (confidence 1.00)
-27-
came to the belief that the farmer would have to help
himself and could not depend upon the politicians and
he therefore established a school for the propagation
of his ideas. The Aikyojuku School gradually became
the center for the development of reactionary ideas.
Tachibana became a friend of Nissho Inouye, the leader
of the "Blood Brotherhood", whose trial is described
in a previous section of this report. They decided
that their ideas could be achieved only through dras-
tic and violent means. No evidence was produced to show
that Tachibana was one of the plotters in the "Blood
Brotherhood" affair, which has already been described,
but he was in accord with the ideas of those plotters.
When they failed and he was approached by the young naval
officers who were planning to carry forward the campaign,
he did not hesitate to cooperate. The prosecutor
charged Tachibana with (1) receiving money from Lt. Koga
of the Navy as a campaign fund,(2) receiving hand grenades
from another Lieutenant, (3) influencing his pupils to
attack the power stations, and (4) planning the attempted
murder of Zei Nishida, one of the group's accomplices
who was suspected of treachery to them. He did not
actually take part in the activities of May 15 but fled
to Manchuria a few days before. There he escaped arrest
for some time while waiting for his comrades to organize
another outbreak in Manchuria. When he found that they
had been arrested in Japan he gave himself up to the
police in Harbin. He was formally charged with murder,
attempted murder, and violation of the explosives law.
The

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
-28-
The next most important personage among the group
of civilians was Dr. Shumei Okawa, an economist and the
organizer of two nationalistic societies. His associates
were the source of some of the funds of the conspirators.
He was accused of supplying them with money and arms.
Another defendant well known in Japan was Hidezo
Toyama, the son of the famous Mitsuru Toyama. Young
Toyama, who is associated with the Pan-Asiatic movement,
was also accused of supplying arms and ammunition. The
formal charges against Okawa and Toyama were abetment
of murder and violation of the explosives law.
Others were Nagamitsu Kawasaki, a farmer devoted
to the Nichiren sect of Buddhism and a great friend of
Nissho Inouye; Kenichiro Honma, 43, principal of the
Shizanjuku School at Tsuchiura; Kunihiko Goto,31, a teacher
in the Aikyojuku School; Masayo Hayashi, 40, a brother-in-
law of Tachibana; and Takeshi Ikematsu, 23, who was ex-
pelled from the Army Cadet School because of his beliefs
and later joined the Aikyojuku School.
The rest were
students in the last-mentioned school.
During the trials Tachibana was the principal spokes-
man of the group. He denounced the political parties, gave
a sensational account of the plight of the farmers, and
stated that the only hope for them was to restore by drastic
means the direct rule of the Emperor. He denounced the
government officials who are entrusted with agricultural
affairs stating that they are graduates of technical
schools and aertcultural colleges where there is no con-
ception of the farmers' plight. He termed Japanese
Universities "just so many employment agencies" where
the

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
the teachers think they have fulfilled their duty
when they present the students with their diplomas.
He said that he had established his school with the
assistance of Shozo Hayashi and Kunihiko Goto, two of
the defendants, for the cultivation of the "philosophy
of the soil". The students in Tachibana's school did
not pay tuition and the teachers were paid meagre salaries,
the funds being donated.
Tachibana denounced fascism, as
it is generally known, because of its non-recognition of
the power of the Emperor. He is an advocate of"The Kingly
Way". He said that the government should be based on
the agricultural families of Japan and "Manchukuo", with
the Emperor as the direct ruler. He said that the present
parliamentary system of Japan is but a poor imitation of
the British; that the members of the Diet are not repre-
sentative of the farmers of the nation.
Most of his testimony was concerned with describing
the condition of the farmers. He contended that the
agricultural system of Japan is on the verge of collapse;
that taxes and the cost of fertilizer are too high. He
said that the farmers are constantly victimized by persons
who take advantage of their ignorance. Their economic
position is weakened by elaborate and expensive marriage
ceremonies which, he stated, are due to their lack of
recreation and their desire to get drunk and forget their
troubles.
Tachibana stated that the most serious blow suffered
by the farmers in recent years was the ban placed on the
exportation of gold after January 11, 1930. In conse-
quence of the ban, he contended that all farm products
fell to prices which made it cheaper for the farmers to
throw them away than to market them. He pointed out that
the

=== Page 426 === (confidence 1.00)
-30-
the man responsible for the ban was former Finance
Minister Inouye who was assassinated by the "Blood
Brotherhood".
Several of the civilian defendants stated that they
took part in the conspiracy because they did not wish
to leave everything to the military and naval men.
They stated that they were not in favor of a military
dictatorship. However, they did not make it clear
how they hoped to defeat the plan of their military
and naval acoomplices, who testified in their respective
trials that they wished to throw the city into confusion
so that martial law and a military dictatorship would be
established.
Throughout all the trials of the partici-
pants in the May 15 Incident the defendants have been in-
definite about the actual steps which were to be taken after
the city was thrown into confusion by the assassinations
and the destruction of the transformer stations.
The United States was brought into the testimony when
her statesmen were accused of thwarting Japan in its
ambitions on several occasions,- e.g. after the Sino-
Japanese War and the Russo-Japanese War, and during the
Siberian expedition.
The civilian conspirators, like their accomplices
in the armed services, denounced Marxism and stated that it
is not suited to Japan. One of them stated that the
unbroken Imperial line at the head of Japan makes the
nation superior to all others.
The testimony of Dr. Shumei Okawa,LL.D., economist
and

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.99)
-31-
nationalistic
and  writer, was given mostly during a private session
cf the Court. He is stated to have been connected
with a "certain case" of several years ago, about which
great secrecy has been preserved. He admitted in public
to having supplied the plotters with arms and ammuni-
tion as well as money. He stated that he "thought a
resort to violence was the inevitable means to which
high thinkers might resort in times of extraordinary
tenseness".
One day was devoted to the question as to whether
the hand grenades used by the conspirators were made at
the time of the Russo-Japanese war. The matter was never
settled but the defense attorneys tried to prove this
to be true and that therefore their age would have pre-
vented their being effective.
The defendant who tried to murder Zei Nishida, one
of his accomplices, testified that he went to Nishida's
house and after conversing several hours drew his re-
volver and tried to shoot his intended victim. However,
the latter dodged the bullets and the assailant fled,
thinking that he had done his duty in at least making
the attempt.
On November 30 the Public Procurator made his demands
for sentences. He asked that Tachibana, the leader, and
Nagamitsu Kawasaki, who had acted as liaison between his
group and the "Blood Brotherhood", be sentenced to life
imprisonment; that In. Okawa, the economist and national-
istic writer, Ikematsu, the former Army Cadet, Kunihiko
Goto.

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
Goto, the teacher, and Hideo Okuda, a student, be
sentenced to fifteen years imprisonment; and that
the others receive terms ranging from seven to twelve
years imprisonment. In contrast to the principle on
which the military and naval men were sentenced, the
Procurator at the trial of the civilians contended
that the motives of the men, no matter how patriotic,
were of minor importance.
The trials of the civilians did not attract as much
attention in Japan as those of the army and navy men
involved in the May 15 Incident. However, the severity
of the sentences demanded by the Procurator caused wide-
spread comment. The nation's sympathy for the conspira-
tors, which had been dormant, was again aroused. The
demands were compared with the sentences of fifteen
years imprisonment given to the two naval officers
who were the actual leaders in the plot.
To the surprise of everyone the Court on February 3
handed down sentences in most cases as severe as those
demanded by the Procurator. Tachibana was sentenced to
life imprisonment. Dr. Okawa, Ikematsu, and Goto were
given 15 years' imprisonment. Kawasaki, for whom the life
penalty was asked was given only 12 years imprisonment, as
were two others. The rest of the sentences ranged from
five years to eight years.
The awarding of the sentences brought forth another
outburst of sympathy for the defendants, but it was
tempered by the knowledge that the court had hurried its
decision in order to permit the sentenced men to take
advantage

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.98)
1
-33-
advantage of the ammesty which was to be proclaimed in
celebration of the birth of the Crown Prince. However,
reactionary circles were incensed over the severity of the
civilian sentences as compared with those given to the
military and naval men. The first action of the defendants
was to appeal to a higher court. Then they realized that
this move would prevent their benefiting from the ammesty
to be proclaimed on February 1l. The day before the ammesty
was granted all, except three, withdrew their appealsand
were therefore prepared to receive a reduction in their
terms of imprisonment.
The Amnesty of February 11, 1934.
On February 1l the Emperor of Japan granted an amnesty
in celebration of the birth of H.I.H. The Crown Prince of
Japan. During the weeks preceding the amnesty the Japanese
law courts were busy delivering sentences so that as many as
possible would benefit. The last stages in the trial of
the civilians involved in the Incident of May 15 were hurried
for this reason.
By the amnesty all death sentences were commuted to
life sentences; all sentences of life imprisonment were
commuted to 20 years imprisonment, except for persons over
70 years or under 16 years of age in which cases their
terms were reduced to 15 years imprisonment; all those
sentenced to terms of imprisonment other than for life
had their sentences reduced by one-fourth if they had
not commenced to serve their terms, and by one-half of
the remainder, if they had served one-half of their terms.
Persons

=== Page 430 === (confidence 1.00)
-34-
Persons condemned for certain crimes,-such as treason,
lese majeste, murder of blood relatives, murder for robbery,
cruel murder, repeated robberies, and breach of military
discipline,- did not have the benefit of the ammesty.
All of those condemned for the part they took
in the Incident of May 15 benefited from the ammesty.
The eleven army cadets who were sentenced to four years
of imprisonment had their sentences reduced to three
years. The naval officers who were sentenced to 15 years
imprisonment had their terms reduced by nearly four years
and the others proportionately. Tachibana, sentenced to
life imprisonment, will serve 20 years, and his confederates,
except the three who appealed, had their terms reduced
by one-fourth.
Although the amnesty benefited 35,000 prisoners, accor-
ding to the newspapers, the attention of the public was
drawn principally to its effect upon the conspirators in the
May 15 Incident and to the fact that it saved the life of
Tomeo Sagoya, sentenced to death for shooting Premier Hamaguchi.
Sagoya and four others who were awaiting execution had their
sentences reduced to life imprisonment.

SUBJECT:
The Reason for General Araki's

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
S i r :
Mr. Obata, who was formerly my colleague in
Constantinople and later Ambassador in Berlin, now
retired from the diplomatic service, called on me
today and in the course of conversation said that it
might interest me to know the precise reason for
General Araki's resignation, which he had learned from
reliable sources. He said that General Araki had
practically been forced out of office for having issued
too many unredeemed checks in the political sense. In
other

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
other words he had made too many promises to satisfy
the young chauvinists in the army with regard to future
military action which he had not been able to carry out
and he had accordingly lost the confidence of this group.
Realizing this situation he had made use of his illness
as an ostensible reason for stepping aside.
The question of his successor immediately arose
and it was generally believed that the choice would fall
on General Mazaki who had long desired to succeed Araki
and whose ultra-chauvinistic leanings endeared him to the
rank and file of the army. However, Prince Kanin, the
Chief of Staff, absolutely refused to hear of General
Mazaki's appointment and it was he who insisted on
General Hayashi who took a good deal of persuading before
he consented to accept the post. Mr. Obata said that
General Hayashi came from his own part of the country
and that he knew him well and that while he was outward-
ly identified with the most chauvinistic element in the
army, he maintained this attitude merely in order to be
able to influence and control the younger and more hot-
headed officers. In thought, however, he is not chauvin-
istic and Mr. Obtata feels that his appointment means a
great step forward for the liberal school of thought in
Japan as opposed to the die-hard element.
Mr. Obata then observed that this liberal school
of thought had gathered much strength in the last several
months. The freedom of speech and criticism of the army
in the present session of the Diet represented a marked
change

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
change from the last session of the Diet where none of
the representatives dared to make themselves heard. In
other words, the chauvinistic military influence was
decidedly on the wane. Also in the Foreign Office there
had been a great improvement. At one time there was a
prominent element in the Foreign Office which was closely
allied to the military and in fact the Gaimusho was
broken up into two camps. Obata said he had spoken to
Count Uchida about the importance of getting rid of
these chauvinists but Count Uchida was much too timid
to take action. Shiratori's transfer was the first step
and now everything had changed. He thought that this
change would be progressive throughout the country and
that Japan's relations with the world will be much better
as a result of it.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

The reaction gainst the military was first sensed
a year ago following & speech in the Diet by Count Uchida
in which he stated in effect that Japan would rather be
reduced to ashes before abanuoning her position in Man-
churia. This extraorainary statement, with its dire im-
plications, occasioned some sober thinking in this country,
and has contributed a new phase to the language: "shodo
gaiko" or "desperate dinlomacy".
This phrase was freely
discussed at the time of Uchida's resignation, and strong
hints ere made that Ji pan had no stomach for any such policy
of desneration in dealing with foreign εffairs.
Count Uchida w: s known to be hand-in-glove vith the
milit ry uthorities, nd his resignation was undoubtedly
a blow to the milit ry influence. 'he new Foreign Minister,
Mr. Hirota, has so unmistakbly uenonstrated his eagerness
to cultivote better r lations abroao that mere mention of the
changed Japanese diplomacy is suff cient in this discussion.
The Department will recall that shortly before Hirota assumed
office, Mr. Shiratori, notorious chief of the publicity section
of the Foreign Office, nd r. Toni, Chief of the Asiatic
Bureau, both closely in touch vith the War Office and highly
influential officials unaer the Uchida regime, were transferred
away from the Foreign Office. These arch leaders of the chau-
vinistic element had split the Gaimusho into two bitterly
hostile camps. Their passing healed this split and left the
more moderate camp under the new Foreign minister definitely
in control.
No discussion of military influence in Japan during
the past two years would be complete without mention of that
extraordinary high-priest of the military cult, half-mystic
and half-Spartan, - General Araki. His great personal influence
both

both in military and civilian circles made him a unique,

he decided for a war with Russie or the United States, there
is little doubt as to what would heve hapoened.

Gener:l Hayashi, the present Minister of War, is

in the Diet have shown him to be an entirely different
type from his predecessor. His appointment may be con-

thought in Japan, as opposed to the extreme chauvinist
element.

Embassy's despatch No. 566, October 31, 1933.

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.99)

I have mentioned the reaction against Count
Uchida's declared policy of "desperate diplomacy", and
have digressed to touch on the personalities of his
associates and successors, who have influenced the conduct
of foreign af airs. An outline of other factors which
have changed public atitude tovard the military should
follow.
In Japan, as in all countries, the pendulum of public
feeling tends to resume the norm. The public may be
aroused to a high pitch of feeling over a given situa-
tion, but sooner or lcter the tautness slackens. This,
in my oninion, is what is hanpening in Japan. Under the
calm leadership of Mr. Hirota, the country has come
to accept a more reasoned view of foreign relations,
and to discount the cries of "emergency" and "crisis" from
the military die-hards. Utterances in the Diet would
indicate that the public now begins to feel that it has
peen tricked into an unnecessary state of agitation, and
is inclined to blame the military for the false alarm.
Outspoken discussions of the budget in the Diet also
reveal dismay over the size of the military demands and
the suspicion that the military have deliberately stirred
up the people with the talk of a crisis in order to get
the money for their own plans. The staggering cost.
of the "desperate diplomacy" is being brought home to
the Japanese in earnest. The Army and Navy budget for
the 1934-35 year is approximately Yen 940,000,000 out of
a total budget of Yen 2,112,000,000. But more sobering
is the fact that the military expenditure exceeds the
total

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
total revenue from taxation by about Yen 150,000,000.
As one speaker stated in the Diet,: "We will have no
country to defend if we spend all our resources on defense".
Curiously enough, the most heated criticism of the
military in recent months, both in the Diet and the press,
has been of military meddling in politics and of military
indiscipline. The notorious "May 15th Affair" in which
Premier Inukai was assassinated in a plot involving Army
and Navy officers, caused a tremenaous shock to the nation.
At the trials the military cuthorities seemed to try to
exploit the affair as a demonstration against the corrup-
tion of party government.
More recently the public has
seemed to suspect that the whole parliamentary structure
was being undermined in favor of a
movement toward fascism
of a Japanese brand, leo by elements in the armed forces .
Japanese may feel disgust at the corruption of the politi-
cians, but they obviously dread a fascist regime. Cri-
ticism of military interference with affairs of state out-
side of their proper ken occupied more attention in the
Diet than any other topic, barring the budget. I have
1!
annended to this despatch translations of selected state-
ments made in the Diet in this connection. They bear
out practically all conclusions drawn in this despatch.
The influential capitalistic classes have long fretted
under the exactions of the military and the Army's re-
strictions on the capitalistic exploitation of Manchuria.
No extension of military or fascist authority would be
pleasing to these classes.
Finally, due consideration of the effect of military
appropriations on the farming classes must be given in
assigning causes for the decline of military favor. The
Army

=== Page 446 === (confidence 0.93)
-8-
1C

rom the farms, where during the past few years con-

abroad as to the attitude of this country. The year
1936 may prove to be critical, but it is premature to con-
clude that thecrisis may not be voided. While defense
measures are neces ary, the oroper diplomatic measures
urt not be neglected.
Eizo Ishizuka(Imperial Nominee-House of Peers)
February 7.
"I understand that in certain military circles
there exists a kind of plan concerning Manchuria. It
is rumored that the manchurian affair and developments
until the oresent have followed this plan. Of course
I myself have not seen this plan and am not in a position
to say whether it exists or not. Still I understand
that it is not merely an imaginary document. Of course
the military authorities will deny its existence. But
I ain inclined to believe that it does exist, and that in
this plan is included a program of social reorganization
or revolution in this country- Japan- as a sort of by-
product or corollary of the Manchurian affair. And I
am not unconvinced that this idea is not entertained among
many

that the militry authorities exercise a strict control
over the attitude and doings of their subordinates and work
for the preservation of strict military discipline
and morale."

"As to the cuestion of Mr. Ishizuka concerning the
alleged nchurian program, I fail to understand clearly
what he said, but I may state definitely that there is
nothing in military circles to cause suspicions of the
kind mentioned by the interpellator.

SPECIAL REPORT,
By Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant.
SUBJECT: Speeial Correspondence of
LE TEHPS From the Far Bast
L& TEMPS publiehed on April 23, 1934, an
article from one of its special correspondents
in the Far East, F. Chevallier, dated from Tokye,
and discussing the general results attained so
far by the present Japanese Gevernment.
Whereas the Saito Cabinet appeared to be in
a difficult position last October, it is solidly
establisbed today with a will to remain in spite
of the many obstaeles whidh it has had te surmount,
and it has furthermore been able te mmintain a
strietly Parliamentary attitude.

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
With respect to "Manchukuo,* Chevallier
declares that its consolidation has been con-
tinued methodically, the budget is balanoed
and economic resources are increasing, Japan
having found there "a formula of economio
collaboration whose details are not yet worked
out but which will assure the prosperity of
the new State, not only without complications
for Japanese economics but while supplying the
latter with a valuable contribution in natural
and commercial wealth."
Chevallier goes on to remark:
"The United States proclaims that its attitude
has not changed, but would it insist upon reiter-
ating this if the fact itself were elearly apparent?
The policy observed by Mr. Hirota, Minister fer
Foreign Affairs, is already bearing fruit. The
Japanese Ambassador in Washington is one of the
ablest Japanese career diplomats, and his personal
connections in America have deeply modified the
American point of view in numerous questions where
conflicts were possible."
Chevallier

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.96)
3
Chevallier considers that China itself is
showing more enthusiasm in its responses to Japanese
overtures. The boycott has been totally stopped
for some months, and it was recently reported on
good authority that official conversations were soon
to be opened for an accord concerning former Chinese
loans which have not yet been reimbursed as well as
new Japanese investments in China.
The original of the article quoted is enolosed.
Very respectfully,
anngoudawkon.
Warrington Dawson,
Special Assistant.

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
JUN 71934
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NOTE
May 21, 1934
AY 25 1934
DEPARTMENI OF SIAJE
General Kzunari Ugaki has probably
"developed" more strikingly than any other
Japanese in political life in recent years.
General Ugaki first appeared in politics
in 1924, when he was nominated to the post of
Minister of War by General Baron Tanaka, both
of these officers being members of the Choshu
clan. Before that time General Ugaki had
been Inspector of Military Education, and it
may be assumed that, having the approval of
General Tanaka, he was supposed to have been
a typical Choshu reactionary. After entering
the Cabinet he showed pronounced political
talent, and being associated in the Govern-
ment with such liberal statesmen as Shidehara
and Hamaguchi, his political views took on a
distinctly liberal coloring. When General
Governor General of Korea because of certain
financial scandals, General Ugaki was sent
to Korea. His record in Korea has been so
favorable that the Minseito has persistently
endeavored to secure General Ugaki's consent
to accept the Presidency of the party. Hehas
thus far refused to associate himself with
any political party. It will also be recalled
that General Ugaki has some following in the
Japanese Army, comprising those who are not
in

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2-
in sympathy with the predominant reactionary
element.
We do not know at the moment precisely
what General Ugaki's political views are,
but I should be disposed to believe that,
ahe has consented to accept the premier-
ship, he has "shaded" his views to the ex-
tent of making himself acceptable to the
Militarists. However, he has shown himself
to be a liberal by instinct, and he is
supposed to be a man of unusual character
and energy. His appointment would not
therefore necessarily mean a retrogression
from the attitude and policies of his
predecessor.

Tokyo's Despatch No. 776 of May 5,
1934, reports the appointment of Baron Ikk1
as President of the Privy Council.
The Despatch, which is well worth
reading in full, does not call for
particular comment except for the statement
that the precedent, "that the vice-president
is promoted to the Presidency upon the
resignation of the former incumbent", has
been broken before on one or two occasions,
notably when Baron Hamao was appointed
President of the Privy Council upon the
resignation of Count Kiyoura in 1924.

Appointment of Baron Kitokuro Ikki as

The Honorable
O.N.I. ANDM.I.D
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that on May 3 Baron
Yuzaburo Kuratomi resigned because of ill health from his
post as President of the Privy Council and was immediately
succeeded by Baron Kitokuro Ikki, former Privy Councillor,
M
Cabinet Minister, and Minister of the Imperial Household
FILED
3
There is no reeson to believe that the cause of Baren
19
Kuratomi's resignation was other than the ostensible one of
ill health. He is an old man of 8l with failing eyesight
and

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
and hearing and is said to have expressed his desire
to retire some time ago. The appointment of his suc-
cessor is believed to have been made with the approval, if
not at the behest of the Genro, Prince Saionji, who is
now in Tokyo for the first time in two years. According
to the press, the highest statesmen of the Empire have all
been cognizant of Baron Kuratomi's impending resignation
and, after several conferences, also decided to recommend
Baron Ikki. There is already evidence that the appointment
is meeting with the approval of politicians, the press,
and the nation as a whole.
While the post of President of the Privy Council is
one of vital importance, the choice of Baron Ikki is at-
tracting unusual attention because it was made in violation
of the standing precedent that the Vice-President is promoted
to the Presiaency upon the resignation of the former in-
cumbent. At the present time Baron Hiranuma is Vice-Pre-
sident of the Privy Council. He has been mentioned in all
recent political crises as a possible successor to Premier
Saito, he has the reputation of being identified with fascist
and reactionary organizations, and it is hinted that he
brought a political flavor to the Privy Council not only
incompatible with the super-political traditions of that
body but also embarrassing to the present government. Ac-
cordingly the appointment of Baron Ikki is hailed not only
because of that individual's reputation for erudition, pro-
bity, and ability but also as an astute move which till tend
to weaken the fascist movement and to strengthen the position
of the Saito Cabinet. It is perhaps concrete evidence that
the

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the Genro and the leading statesmen close to the Emperor
are determined to preserve the life of the Saito Cabinet,
and it is also an exemplification of the scrupulous care
with which the Throne is preserved, through the appointment
of disinterested advisers, from all direct association with
politics.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 20, 1934
SKCH
You will be interested to read the
attached despatch No. 814 of June 1, 1934,
from the Embassy at Tokyo, reporting on the
domestic political situation. The Embassy
seems to feel that the discovery of serious
irregularities by responsible officials in
the Finance Department will render "it almost
impossible for the Cabinet to continue in
office", for the reason that the Saito Govern-
ment was established expressly to eliminate
"graft" among public servants. The Embassy
notes the prediction that the Cabinet will
fall about the middle of June.
Count Makino and Prince Saionji are en-
deavoring to keep the present Government in
office lest it be succeeded by a fascist or
military government. The Embassy refers to
the rumor that Prince Konoye, who is supposed
to have reactionary tendencies, was induced
to leave Japan in order that it might not be
possible for the reactionary element to
gather about him and so set up a "fascist"
government.
Of the various persons named as likely
to succeed Viscount Saito as premier, the
despatch mentions the names of Count Kiyoura
and General Ugaki. In a recent telegram, the
Embassy reported that the candidacy of General
Ugaki had apparently collapsed. The other
leading

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
leading candidate, Count Kiyoura, is one
of the last of the old-time bureaucrats,
and as such he is intensely disliked by
the political parties. Although the political
parties have lost standing, they cannot be
completely ignored; and I would be surprised
if he were to be ordered to organize a new
government to succeed that of Viscount Saito.
The key to the situation seems to be the
Minister of Finance, Mr. Takahashi. If he
decides that he can remain in office without
undue loss of "face", it is qe unlikely
that there may be no change of government in
the immediate future.
not
FE:E:DLY

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.94)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, June 1, 1934.
No. 814.
Subject:
Internal Political Situation in Japan.
COPIES SENT TO
O.N.J. AND M. I.D.
REC
M RECD
UN1864
Division of.
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS QOYW
UN 19 1934
894.00/523
Department of State
4Gile
A-C/V
J0L2-1934
ONI
MID
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
515
I have the honor to refer to my telegram No. 96,
of
May 21, 4 p.m., in which I stated that, owing to various
causes, it appeared probable that the Saito Cabinet would
Tr
fall in the near future, and to submit the following b-
FILED
servations and comments on the present political situation
1934
in Japan.
The Saito Cabinet, which, it will be remembered, was
formed after the assassination of Premier Inukai on May 15,
1932, as a super-party government designed to eliminate the
obvious

=== Page 506 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
obvious corruption of the political parties, has
been considerably weakened in recent months by a series
of failures to satisfy the needs of the nation. The
farming oopulation is still crying loudly for relief,
while the Saito Cabinet seems unable to devise ant
practical measures of relief. The failure of the
Government to raise silk prices renders the outlook
very gloomy for millions of silk-worm raisers. While
national finances are in a better position than they
were a year ago, because of an increase in revenue
br ught about tnrough stimulated trade, the budget
recently passed by the Diet was unbalanced and will
require the isruance of large amounts of "red-ink"
bonds, and the C: binet is being censured for its
failure to adjust the national finances. Five mem-
bers of the Cabinet have resigned since its inception,
and each resignation tends to weakeh the C binet.
of
the five resignations, that of Mr. Hatoyama, the
Minister for Education, was the most serious, as the
Premier has been unable to find a successor to Mr.
Hatoyama nd is himself holding the portfolio. As a
result, the Department of Education is in a turmoil
and the nation is demanding reform of the educational
system.
Complaints have bee. heard on all sides, moreover,
that the Cabinet lacks initiative and force, that it
is following a weak-kneed and compromising foreign policy,
that it has not the strength to meet the anticipated
"crisis" in 1935-1936, and that it should step down and
make room for a stronger Cabinet better fitted to serve
the

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the needs of the nation vhen the storm comes. These
complaints originate principally with the Seiyukai,
the majority party, which itself vainly hopes to seize
the governmental reins, and with the reactionaries,
who as usual demand a strong and positive policy.
With the Cabinet thus weakened, the discovery
of corruption in the Finance Department, resulting
in the arrest of several officials, including the Vice
Minister of Fincnce, rendered it almost impossible for
the Cabinet to continue in office. This scandal arose,
it appears,from the transfer of certain securities
held by the B nk of Taiwan (being a part of the property
of the defunct Suzuki Shoten, which failed disastrously
in 1927) to various individuals at less than their
market value. The scandal was kept hidden, although
it was generally known to the nevspapers, which re-
ferred to it as a "serious affair", until ay l8th, when
the press ban on the publication of the news was lifted
and Mr. Kuroda, the Vice Minister of Finance, was formally
arrested. The reactionaries demand that Mr. Takahashi,
the Minister of Finance, should accept responsibility
for the misconauct of his subordinates and demonstrate
his sense of responsibility by resigning. If Mr. Taka-
hashi resigns, it is almost certain that the Cabinet
will fall. It is generally predicted in the newspapers
that the Cabinet's fall will occur about the middle of June.
The

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.96)
-4-
The deviation from the straight and narrow path
of the officials of the Finance Department is especially
serious, as the Saito Cbinet was inducted in 1932 for the
express purpose of abolishing graft in governmental offices,
or, as the euphonistic phrase is in Japan, of "maintaining
official discipline". The Cabinet's failure to enforce
"official discipline" is therefore generally regarded
as the final blow to its prestige and to its continued
existence.
There is no doubt but that Prince Saionji, the only
surviving Genro, nd Count Makino, Lord Privy Seal, are
doing everything in their power to maintain the Saito
Cabinet in office. It appears that they (and presumably
V
the Emperor) desire a reasonably conservative Government
at the present time, when Japan is facing many problems
in its foreign relations and needs to "sit tight" in order
to consolidate its gains on the Asiatic Continent. They
fear a "fascist", or radical reactionary government, as
much as they fear a military government. It is rumored
in Tokyo (and the rumors certainly seem plausible) that
energetic steps vere taken by Prince Saionji and Count
Makino to prevent a "fiscist" Cabinet coming into power
when it seemed inevitable that the Saito Cabinet would
fall. As a first step, they suppressed the news of the
scandal in the Finance Ministry for a month and a half
or two months , until other steps had been taken. They
o
then caused the appointment of Dr. Ikki as President of
kiicbas
the Privy Council, in place of Baron Hiranuma, the Vice
President, who should normally have been raised to the
position of President. Baron Hiranuma is one of the
leaders

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
leaders of the reactionaries and is their candidate for
the Premiership. Passing Dr. Ikki over Baron Hiranuma's
head, as was done, caused him to lose prestige and made
it impossible for him to become Premier in the near future.
The next step, according to the rumors, was to get Prince
Konoe out of Japan, as Prince Konoe has in the past acted
as a stepping-stone between the conservatives and the re-
actionaries, with whom he is suid to be in sympathy. If
he should be in J pan when the Saito C binet falls, cortain
reactionary elements might rally around him and use his
influence to set up a "fascist" Cabinet. Consequently
Prince Konoe w s induced to leave for the United States, and
on the day following his departure, the press ban on the
scandal in the Finence Ministry was lifted nd the present
Cabinet crisis was permitted to develop.
With the "fascist" leaders thus disposed of, only two
men appear as of sufficient influence and prestige to be
candidates fo: the Premiership. One is Count Kiyoura, ex-
Premier and a statesman of kmown ability and integrity. He
is eighty-four yeurs old, however, and it is not thought that
he has the strength to carry the nation over the "dengerous
period"head.
The other is General Ugaki, lat present Governor
General of Korea, several times War Minister, and an able and
highly-respected statesman. His name is the more prominently
mentioned as Premier Saito's successor, and he has the support
of the liberal elements in Japan, as well as of the business
and industrial leaders. He is, however, opposeu by the extreme
militarists

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
militarists, both in the Army and the Navy, because of
his inclination toward disarmament at the time of the
Washington Naval Conference. The spokesman for the Navy
Department stated on May 31 that "the Navy as a whole is
not necessarily opposed to an Ugaki Cabinet", thereby
creating a furore in naval circles, because apparently
a considerable section of the Navy is oonosed to.Ugaki.
(Rear Admiral Sakano, the spokesman for the Navy Depart-
ment resigned on June 1, because of the objections to his
statement) In spite of this opposition, however, it appears
at the moment that General Ugaki is the most likely can-
didate for the Premiership in the event of the downfall
of the Saito C:binet.
The political parties are still out of the picture,
partly because the public was disgusted with their endeavors
to gain political power when they should have been considering
the public welfare during the last session of the Diet, and
partly because the Seiyukai, the majority party and the only
one in a position to form a Cabinet, is so split with internal
dissension that it is powerless. The Seiyukai, however, will
probably use its influence, such as it is, &gainst General
Ugaki, who has long been known to have Minseito affiliations.
It is significant that both Count Kiyoura and General
Ugaki have come to Tokyo, arriving on May 3lst. It is believed,
however, that no visible political strings will be pulled until
after the funeral of Admiral Togo, on June 5th.

July 11, 1934.
Mr. Phillips:
I concur in the view expressed in
Mr. Hamilton's comment: the view that the
despatch overemphasizes the trend toward
liberalism.
There undoubtedly is "a trend toward
liberalism"; but the probability is that
progress in that direction will be slow,
and it is to be hoped that it will be, --
for, if liberalism made rapid gains and
appeared in any way to threaten the position
of control which the military element have
gained and the militaristic attitude which
the people have acquired, there would be
great likelihood that the aggressives and
the "younger men" in the Army and Navy
would precipitate some drastic course of
action in order to combat and toward defeat-
ing the swing toward liberalism.

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.97)

June 5, 1934
SKH
2121.
Tokyo's despatch No. 83l of June 14,
1934, is opportune as well as an interest-
ing report on the internal political
situation in Japan. Some of the information
contained in this desp&toh was used in the
preparation of my memorandum on the appoint-
ment as Premier of Admiral Okada.
You will note that Prince Saionji,
aided by Count Makino, has been working
quietly but very subtly to put Liberal person-
ages in key positions. I recommend that the
despatch be read carefully in full.
The despatch refers to the production
by the Minister of Finance of the theory of
"divided responsibility". This is more than
a theory. Each Cabinet Officer, so far as the
laws and ordinances go, is an officer inde-
pendent of the Prime Minister, for he owes
responsibility in theory only to the Emperor.
Several years ago Dr. Midzuno, then Minister
of Education, refused to put his resignation
in the hands of the Prime Minister and so
accept responsibility for some affair that
took place in his Department. He insisted
upon presenting his resignation directly to
the Emperor.
In stating

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
In stating that the Mitsui and
Mitsubishi are affiliated with the political
parties, the Embassy is taking note of a
story which seems to have acquired as much
hardihood as the old story about Japanese
banks employing Chinese cashiers. The
story arose from the fact that Count Kato
and Baron Shidehara married daughters of
the former head of Mitsubishi. A director
of Mitsui, in commenting on the story,
stated to me that it would be suicidal for
either of these two firms to be tooclosely
allied with either one party or the other,
and that, therefore, Mitsui, and he assumed
Mitsubishi, made the practice of contributing
heavily to both parties.
Generally speaking, Japanese concerns
with important interests outside of Japan
have been "liberal" in their attitude on
international relations. The outstanding
spokesman for this class,of course, was
Mr. Inouye,who was assassinated when
Minister of Finance.

sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatch No. 814
523
dated June 1, 1934, discussing the difficulties which
are besetting the Saito Cabinet, and to my telegram No.
519
121 June 9, 1 p.m., stating that the Ugaki boom had
collapsed and that there were indications that the Saito
Cabinet would not fall in the immediate future.
During the past two weeks, in internal political
acl
circles in Japan, there appears to have been a decided
clips
trend toward liberalism and internationalism, as opposed
to

=== Page 517 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
to fascism and ultra-nationaliam, and toward parliamen-
tarism, as opposed to a dictatorship.
The very astute
move made by Prince Saionji, the sole surviving Genro,
and by Count Makino, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, in
appointing Baron Dr. Ikki to the Presidency of the Privy
Council over the head of Baron Hiranuma, the Vice President
and a reactionary leader, apparently has had much greater
repercussions in political circles than was thought likely
at first. It not only succeeded in removing Baron Hira-
numa from the list of candidates for the Premiership (at
least for the time being) and giving "fascism" a blow from
which it will not recover for some time, but it is also
regarded as distinctly a victory for parliamentarism and
the more liberal elements in Japan. According to reports,
Prince Saionji is said to be in favor of substituting the
President of the Privy Council, the Lord Keeper of the
Privy Seal and the Lord Ghamberlain for the Genro, upon
his death. He has now succeeded in putting three men,
all of whom are advocates of liberalism, internationalism
and parliamentarism, in these three posts.
Baron Dr.
V
Ikki, a man of pronounced liberal views (he was marked for
assassination by the reactionaries in l932) holds the post
N-bou
of President of the Privy Council; Count Makino, Lord
Keeper of the Privy Seal, is well-known as a friend of
liberalism and parliamentarism and an implacable enemy
Kartan
of ultra-militarism and fasoim; and Admiral suzuki,
Lord Ohamberlain, is known as an advocate of disarmament
and cooperation with other nations.) (Prince Konoye, if
the plans of the leaders of the government in Japan material-
ize, will eventually become Genro, but as he is still a
fairly

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
fairly young man and cannot be expected to assume the
position of a Genro for fifteen or twenty years to come,
arrangements are apparently being made for the above-named
statesman to fill his future position in the interim.)
The appointment of Dr. Ikki is also said to be a
victory for the pro-London Naval Treaty group; indeed
the three men, Count Makino, Admiral Suzuki, and Dr. Ikki,
who are believed to uphold the fairness and justice of the
London naval agreement, are in positions of great influence.
It is also reported that Premier Saito, who apparently was
connected with the appointment of Dr. Ikki to his present
post, by that act aligned himself with the group of inter-
nationalists and against the ultra-nationalists. In short,
the appointment of Dr. Ikki to the Presidency of the Privy
Council, in the opinion of observers of the political world,
marks a forward step in the struggle between liberalism and
internationalism on the one side and ultra-nationalism and
militarism on the other.
The growing consciousness in political circles of
the victory won by the forces of liberalism seems to have
had a reaction favorable to the Saito Cabinet. It is
still believed that under certain circumstances the Saito
Cabinet will be compelled to resign, but apparently every
effort is being made to retain the Cabinet in power. If
the charges against the Vice Minister of Finanee turn out
to be justified, Mr. Takahashi, the Finance Minister,
according to statements released recently, will take the
responsibility for the misdeeds of his subordinate and
resign, but Mr. Takahashi has produced the theory of
"divided responsibility". According to this theory, the
responsibility

=== Page 519 === (confidence 0.96)
-4-
responsibility for the misconduct of governmental employees
need be assumed only by the minister concerned and need
not be assumed, as in the past, by the entire cabinet.
On the other hand it is questionable whether Premier Saito
will consent to continue in office without the support of
his old friend and adviser, Mr. Takahashi. The nation,
however, seems to be unable to find a successor to vis-
count Saito, should he be compelled to resign.
Apropos of this situation, it is regarded as signifi-
cant that the charges against the Vice Minister of Finance,
which have led to the present Cabinet crisis, were per-
mitted by the Minister of Justice to be published on the
day following the departure from Japan of Prince Konoye.
The Ugaki boom, which was in full strength two weeks
ago, has died down considerably, largely due to the deter-
mined opposition of certain groups in the Army and Navy,
which would probably make it impossible for General Ugaki
to form a Cabinet should he receive the Imperial command
to do so. Moreover, he has no particular qualifications
which would make it appear that he would be a stronger
or more able Premier than Saito. Hr recently came to
Tokyo from Seoul and interviewed various statesmen, os-
tensibly on matters relating to Chosen, cand left Tokyo
to return to his post on June 12, apparently without having
settled the matter of the Premiership. In addition to
General Ugaki, three other candidates have appeared for
the position of Premier: Count Kiyoura, former Premier;
Kisabaro
S..d
Dr. Suzuki, President of the Seiyukai; and General Araki,
former Minister for War. The FUKUOKA NICHI-NICHI, a
somewhat astute provincial newspeper, sums up the qualiri-
cations of the four candidates as follows:
2

=== Page 520 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
"A certain man who is regarded as a strong
candidate for the post (General Ugaki) is
too ambitious and has political inolina-
tions. The public cannot have more eonfi-
dence in him than in Viscount Saito. An-
other candidate (Dr. Suzuki) is considered
treacherous. His policies would be damaging
to public interests, and the people are dis-
gusted with him.7 A third (Count Kiyoura) is
too old, and there is skepticism of his ability
to rally able men around him. A fourth (General
Araki) is the worst possible. We never dreamed
that the Japanese nation could harbor thoughts of
recommending such a man for the office of Premier.
He is childish in his love of display and is full
of vain-glory. No one will be so foolish as to
entrust this glorious country to this sort of
man, who once seemed to take pride in his non-
sensical "principles" and "philosophy".
A movement has also appeared looking toward the
appointment of a coalition Cabinet, composed of seiyukai
and Minseito statesmen, under the leadership of Baron
Tatsuo Yamamoto, the present Minister of Home Affairs.
08
Baron Yamamoto, however, it is reported has definitely
declined to undertake the task of organizing such a
Cabinet.
Under the circumstances, the opinion is gaining
ground that Viscount Saito should continue in office,
even should Mr. Takahashi resign and refuse to reconsider
his resignation. In such event, it is believed that the
entire Cabinet will resign, but the resignation may be
refused by the Throne, or a new mandate may be given vis-
count Saito to form a Cabinet. In such case, it is ex-
pected that the present Minister for Foreign Affairs and
the present Minister for War will remain in the new Cabinet.
As the Department is aware, the great financial houses
of Japan have long been afriliated with the political
parties - so much so that a party Cabinet would often be
called a "Mitsui Cabinet* or a "Mitsubishi Cabinet".
simee

=== Page 521 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Since the political parties have lost power, however,
there has been little talk of the conneetion of the
financial houses with politics. Rumors of such con-
nections are again appearing. The Mitsui organization,
it seems, has affiliated itself with the reactionary
element under Baron Hiranuma and with the radical Army
element under General Araki. The Mitsubishi organiza-
tion, always conservative*, has on the other hand allied
itself with the more conservative groups, as has the
Sumitomo organization of Osaka. These two great finan-
cial organizations desire the maintenance of the present
government, or if this is impossible, the formation of
an Ugaki government. The two opposing camps have carried
with them the newspapers which they control or wi' which
they are friendly. Thus the OSAKA MAINICHI and TOKYO
NICHI-NICHI, the CHUGAI SHOGYO and the JIJI SHIMPO, alI
of which are connected with the Mitsui interests, are
opposed to General Ugaki and are inclined to favor the
radical Army element, while the Tokyo and Osaka ASAHI,
which is friendly to the Mitsubishi and Sumitomo organ-
izations, is supporting Ugaki for Premier.
According to some political observers, the present

It s believed that the reason given for the collapse

nobe taken too literally. It may be recalled that a few
9
days ago the Minister of Finance issued a statement to
the press in which the opinion was expressed that inter-
national affairs would become less critical, and it indicated
intention to reduce the draft naval and military estimates
for the coming fiscal year by substantial amounts. It
would be safe to assume that, as the Army and Navy would not
have permitted such a statement to pass unchallenged,
the resignation of the Saito Cabinet was due more to
difficulties, actual or anticipated, over the budget for
the next year, rather than to the speculations of a few
subordinate officials. From the list of Japanese Cabinets
hereto attached, it will be noted that the majority of
Cabinets have fallen in the months of June and July. This
F/G
is due to the fact that the draft budgets, which are presest-
C
ed at the beginning of each preceding fiscal year, are referre
to the Cabinet by the Department of Finance during the month
of June.

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of June. If the Cabinet concludes that it is unable to
present to Parliament a draft budget incorporating within
the limits of visible revenue the demands of the various
departments, the Cabinet resigns. There is, therefore,
substantial evidence to support the belief that renewed
conflict with the Army and Navy over fiscal allotments
brought about the resignation of the Saito Cabinet.
The selection of Admiral Okada is distinctly a
surprise. The candidates for succession to Admiral Vis-
count Saito, as reported by the Embassy and by press
correspondents, were:
General Ugaki, Governor-General of Korea;
Baron Hiranuma, vice-president, Privy Council; and
Count Kiyoura, a former premier.
The Embassy reported some days ago that the candidacy
15+9
of General Ugaki, who is the leader of the Liberal Wing
of the Army, had collapsed, due to the opposition of the
militarists. Baron Hiranuma, who has strong "fascist"
tendencies, was, according to the Embassy, put out of the
running by the elder statesman, Prince Saionji, who caused
/516
Baron Hiranuma to lose face by appointing a Liberal to
the presidency of the Privy Council, a post which Baron
Hiranuma would normally have obtained by virtue of having
occupied the office of vice-president. Count Kiyoura, who
is eighty-four years old, is highly unpopular with the
political

=== Page 527 === (confidence 0.98)
4
- 3 -
political parties for the reason that he is by nature and
by training a bureaucrat. His chanoes to succeed to the
premiership were never considered very good.
Various conjectures may be made with regard to the
reasons for the appointment of Admiral Ckada. The most
important issue now before Japan is conceived to be that of
naval limitation. An ordinance issued last year took out
of the hands of the Government the power to determine
Japan's naval strergth and left that power with the Chief
of the Naval Staff, an officer who would correspond to the
Chief of Naval Operations in the American Navy. So far as
naval questions are concerned, the Japanese Government, in
dealing with other interested foreign governments, would
merely be a channel of communication between the Chief of
the Naval Staff and foreign governments; and it may be
assumed that Prince Saionji and his advisors felt that it
would be preferable to have as Frime Minister one who was
once an officer in the Japanese Navy and has close
associations with those who control the Navy.
Although Fleischer's telegram to the NEW YORK HERALD
TRIBUNE described Admiral Okada asa "large Navy" man,
Admiral Okada has always had rather moderate views. His
outlook on international relattons is indicated by the
following incident:
In 1924
Nor.2,1932.

=== Page 528 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
In 1924, the Japanese people were greatly agitated
by reports that the maneuvers of the American Navy which
were about to take place in the Pacific Ocean were designed
to explore the possibility of conducting joint maneuvers
with the Australian naval forces in event of a war between
the United States and Japan. Admiral Okada, who was then
Commander-in-Chief of the Japanese Battle Fleet, issued the
following statement to an American correspondent:
"I am going to take upon myself all responsi-
bility for stating that the imperial navy is feeling
not the least concerned about the Pacific maneuvers
of the American Navy. I am as a soldier little
interested in international politics but I am fully
convinced that the coming Facific maneuvers of the
American Navy is nothing more nor less than a
maneuver; a maneuver of the kind undertaken by the
navy of every other nation though it may be a
little larger in scale and longer in duration. A
maneuver exercised three thousand miles off the
shores of Japan signifies not very much indeed in
our eyes. If I were a statesman I would feel far
more deeply concerned about the attitude of the
press which is ever intent upon making capital of a
business of this kind and eventually creating an air
inconsistent with international friendship and
good understanding."

TOKI, Nov. 2.—"The presence of the
American fleet in the Pacific unneces-
scrily irritates the minds of the Japa-
nese public." Admiral Keisuke Okada.
Minister of the Navy, said today in an
Herald Tribune photo-Acme
exclusive interview with this corre-
Admiral Keisuke Okada
spondent.
The admiral deplored publicity or
propaganda tending to aggravate feel-
ing among the people of Japan or
America.
"There is no reason for Japan and
the United States to go to war." he
said. "War between the two coun-
tries is unthinkable unless it is under-
taken for the sake of war alone. It
would be harmful to both countries
and neither would derive any benefit.
The war talk is an outragè to inter-
tional common sense. and there is no
substantial basis for it."
Admiral Okada said that the London
Naval Treaty of 1930 leaves very much
to be desired from the Japanese stand-
point. and that Japan agreed to the
pact "only because it would cease to be
effective after 1936."
He said President Hoover's proposal
for approximately one-third naval dis-
armament was still open to informal
discussions between rtpresentatives of
the powers, but was not entirely fair
to Japan. He favors the French thesis
of computing naval strength on the
basis of existing strength instead of
reckoning acoording to the theoretical
limits under the treaties.

policy of the Saito administration but with a new

Three. If the United States had been able to
