Admiral Nomura was at one time the
Japanese Naval Attaché in Washington and
also a menber of the Japanese delegation
to the Washington Conference. After his
return from Washington he served in the
American section of the Naval General Staff,
and was then promoted to the rank of Rear
Admiral and put in cormand of the Yangtse
River Fleet. He then served in various
posts in the Navy Department and in the
Naval General Staff. The following reference
to Admiral Norura is contained in the
Embassy's despatch No. 173 of June 11, 1930,
reporting on the removal from important
positions in the Japanese Navy of officers
who had opposed the London Treaty:
"It is not likely that Admiral
Taniguchi will be in office in 1935,
but the chances are extremely favor-
able that he will be succeeded by
Admiral Nomura, who is not only a
genuine admirer of the United States
but is probably the most statesman-
like officer in the Japanese Navy."

Of all the liberal officers who were
advanced to important positions as a result
of the "liberal" victory in 1930, Admiral
Nomura alone was not put out of the way
when the reactionaries gained control of
the Navy in 1931.

while his chief, Admiral Takarabe, was
absent as a delegate at London. It was
Admiral Kobayashi's duty to fight Takarabe's
battles at home with the reactionary Chief
of the Naval Staff, Admiral Kanji Kato.
Admiral Xobayashi was, in 1931 or 1932,
demoted to the command of an unimportant
naval port on the Japan Sea.

July 9, 11 a.m., concerning the composition, policy and
installation of the Cabinet of Admiral Keisuke Okada,
which assumed office on July 8, 1934. The composition
f the Cabinet at the time of installation was as follows:
Premier: Admiral Keisuke Okada, on the retired
list of the Japanese Navy and Navy
Minister in two preceding.Cabinets.
Minister for Foreign Affairs: Mr. Koki Hirota,
a career diplomatic officer and Foreign
Minister in the-preceding Cabinet.
Finanae

=== Page 548 === (confidence 0.99)
Finance Minister: Mr. Sadanobu Fujii, a career
official of the Finance Ministry
and for a short period Vice Minis-
ter of Finance in the preceding
Cabinet.
War Minister:
General Senjuro Hayashi, on the
active list of the Japanese Army
and War Minister in the preceding
Cabinet.
Navy Minister:
Admiral Mineo Osumi, on the active
list of the Japanese Navy and
Navy Minister in the preceding
Cabinet.
Minister for Home Affairs: Mr. Fumio Goto, a non-
party statesman and Minister for
Agriculture in the preceding
Cabinet.
Justice Minister:
Mr. Naoshi Obara, a career offi-
cial of the Justice Ministry and
former Chief Judge of the Tokyo
Court of Appeals.
(p.10
Education Minister: Mr. Gengi Matsuda, a prominent
member of the Minseito and once
Minister for Overseas Affairs.
Minister for Commerce and Industry: Mr. Chuji Machi-
da, a prominent member of the Min-
seito and Minister for Agriculture
in two preceding Cabinets.
Minister for Communications: Mr. Takejiro Tokonami,
formerly a prominent member of
the Seiyukai (now expelled) and
Minister for Home Affairs in two
preceding Cabinets.
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry: Mr. Tatsuno-
suke Yamasaki, formerly of the
Seiyukai (now expelled).
Minister for Railways: Mr. Shinya Uchida, formerly
of the Seiyukai (now expelled).
Minister for Overseas Affairs: Held concurrently
with position of Premier by Ad-
miral Okada.
Confidential Biographic Data Reports on the new members
of the Cabinet are enclosed herewith.
Composition of the Cabinet.
The Cabinet, as finally installed, conqists of three
active

=== Page 549 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
active or retired military men (Admiral Okada, General
Hayashi and Admiral Osumi), three "bureaucrats" (Mr. Hirota,
Mr. Fujii, and Mr. Obara), one non-party politician (Mr. Goto),
three former Seiyukai men (Mr. Tokonami, Mr. Yamasaki and
Mr. Uchida) and two Minseito men (Mr. Matsuda and Mr. Machida).
The Okada Cabinet, therefore, like the preceding Saito Cabinet,
can be considered to be a "national" Cabinet, except for one
peculiar circumstance, namely, that no peer is included in its
composition. As far as can be ascertained by a brief investi-
gation, practically all previous Cabinets have included at
least one member of the peerage. It is possible that the
Overseas Ministry has not been filled for the present with
the expectation of appointing a peer to this post when a suit-
able candidate is found. There has been some newspaper dis-
cussion of the possibility of appointing Prince Konoye (who
has cut short his visit to the United States and is hurrying
home) to the position of Minister for Overseas Affairs, but
both the Prince and the Princess, who is in Japan, have ri-
diculed the idea.
The newspapers generally consider the new Cabinet to be
a safe one, with no radical right or left elements, with the
possible exception of Mr. Fumio Goto who is rumored to be
somewhat in sympathy with the reactionary groups. The TOkYO
ASAHI considers the Cabinet to be bureaucratic, while the
HOCHI considers it to be an extension of the Saito Cabinet,
especially in the realm of diplomacy.
1sk
Appointment of Admiral Okada as Premier.
When it became reasonably certain that the Saito Cabinet
would not be able to survive the scandal in the Finance Minis-
try, there was much speculation in Tokyo regarding the person
ho

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.99)
一 4 -
who would be commanded to form the next Cabinet. The names
of General Ugaki, Count Kiyoura, Baron Hiranuma, Dr. Suzuki
and Baron Yamamoto were mentioned, and at one time it seemed
certain that General Ugaki would be selected to head the
next Cabinet. When it became apparent, however, that influ-
ential elements in both the Army and the Navy were unalterably
opposed to the General, his boom burst. It was then widely
reported that Admiral Viscount Saito would again be commanded
to form a Cabinet, or that the resignation of his Cabinet
would be refused by'the Throne. At the same time, however,
there were vague rumors that Prince Saionji and the states-
men close to the Throne would recommend a "dark horse" as
the next Premier. A few days before his appointment, the
Embassy received an intimation that Admiral Okada was the
"dark horse", but in view of the many rumors which were flying
around the capital, the Embassy hesitated to give credence
to the report.
Various reasons have been advanced regarding the appoint-
ment of Admiral Okada. One (perhaps the most reasonable) is
that the naval disarmament question is the most important
question confronting the nation at the present time, and
therefore a naval man was indicated as the best choice for
Premier. Admiral Okada is not known as a "big navy" advocate,
but when the London Naval Disarmament Treaty was under con-
sideration in Japan, he acted as mediator between the anti-
treaty party, led by Admiral Kanji Kato, and the pro-treaty
party, led by Admiral Takarabe, Baron Wakatsuki and (then)
Premier Hamaguchi, and is therefore, fully informed regarding
both

=== Page 551 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
both the diplomatic and technical sides of the question.
Later (November, 1932) in a newspaper interview, he declared
that the London Naval Treaty left much to be desired; that
Japan only consented to sign it because it would cease to
be effective after 1936; and that he favored naval limitation
on the basis of existing strength rather than on the grounds
of theoretical strength. He is therefore considered to be
thoroughly in favor of preserving and augmenting Japan's
naval strength, without being a "big navy" fanatic.
Admiral Okada is also said to be a compromise Premier.
When it became certain that the resignation of the Saito
Cabinet was inevitable, about the end of June, some sixty
senior officers of the Japanese Navy addressed a petition to
Admiral Prince Fushimi, the Navy Minister, Admiral Osumi, and
Admiral Kato, calling for the abrogation of the Washington
Naval Disarmament Treaty and the establishment of the princi-
ple of naval equality. The fear,aroused by this petition, of
fresh outbreaks of violence on the part of the younger offi-
cers in both the Navy and the Army is said to have influenced
Prince Saionji in the selection of a naval man for Premier, it
being thought that the military would feel that their interests
were in safer hands if a military man were head of the Govern-
ment, than if a civilian were Premier. While, therefore, the
liberal element was calling for the selection of a known
sound, safe statesman, such as General Ugaki or Admiral Vis-
count Saito, and the super-patriots were calling for the se-
lection of an ultra-nationalist, such as General Araki, Ad-
miral Kanji Kato, or Baron Hiranuma, a middle course was taken
and

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
and Admiral Okada was chosen.
Admiral Okada is also said to be something of a genius
at compromising between contending factions. As was mentioned
above, he undertook to mediate between the anti-treaty faction
and the pro-treaty faction at the time when the London Naval
Treaty was presented for ratification in Japan, and succeeeded
in adjusting the differences between the two parties to such
an extent that the treaty was ratified, although apparently
with some tacit understanding that the same ratio would not
be accepted by Japan again. Likewise, when Minister of the
Navy in the Saito Cabinet, he undertook to adjust the dif-
ferences between the Cabinet and the Seiyukai, which at that
time threatened to wreck the Government, as the Seiyukai.
held a clear majority in the Diet. He again succeeded in ad-
justing the differences temporarily, although he resigned as
Minister of the Navy shortly after, his resignation being
popularly attributed to the fact that he was unable to hold
the Saito Government to an agreement said to have been con-
cluded by him. At the present time, when there are distinctly
opposing camps in Japan, including the liberals, the ultra-
nationalists, and the professional politicians, it is felt
that a skilful mediator will be of the greatest value to the
nation. His appointment as Premier has therefore been accepted
for the time being by the most influential factions of the
hation, including the Army and the Navy.
Formation of the Cabinet.
The formation of the Cabinet was attended with considera-
ble difficulty, as, in order to be a really national Cabinet,
such

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
such as was apparently intended, all factions of the nation
should be represented in its final composition. Almost from
the first it was understood that former Minister for Foreign
Affairs Koki Hirota, former War Minister General Hayashi, and
former Navy Minister Admiral Osumi would retain their posts
in the new Cabinet, whileit was soon announced that Vice
Minister of Finance Fujii in the Saito Cabinet would become
Finance Minister in the new Cabinet. This was distinctly
a surprise to the political world, as it had been confidently
expected that if Mr. Takahashi, Finance Minister in the Saito
Cabinet, refused to enter the new Cabinet, Mr. Fukai, Vice-
Governor of the Bank of Japan, would be offered the post.
It is believed, however, that Mr. Fujii, although almost un-
known to the political and financial worlds,will be entirely
acceptable as Finance Minister, as he will carry on Mr. Taka-
hashi's policies.
After the appointment of the so-called "bureaucrats",
however, the formation of the Cabinet became more difficult.
Admiral Okada invited the Seiyukai and the Minseito to name
two candidates each for minor posts in the new Cabinet.
The Minseito accepted readily, as, being the minority
party in the Diet, it could not aspire to the formation of a
Cabinet itself and was glad to accept posts in a national
Cabinet. The Seiyukai, however, considered itself insulted
by the offer of two minor posts in the new Cabinet and re-
fused the olive branch. The faction of the Seiyukai led
by Mr. Tokonami, however, which has long been opposed to
the faction led by the President, Dr. Suzuki, accepted a
later

=== Page 554 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
later offer of three posts in the new Cabinet and named Mr.
Tokonami, Mr. Yamasaki and Mr. Uchida for the posts. At the
same time these three men resigned from the Seiyukai, but
without accepting their resignations, the Seiyukai expelled
them from the party. The defection of the three prominent
members of the Seiyukai, with their followers, has caused
great commotion among the professional politicians and will
undoubtedly cause a new line-up of party politics, but in
the present temper of the Japanese nation, the activities
of the parties do not arouse much interest. While the pro-
fessional politicians, therefore, are displeased with the
new Cabinet, the liberal element in the nation hails it with
great enthusiasm, as indicating a victory for them. It was
a victory, in the sense that a "fascist", or ultra-national-
istic, Government was not placed in power.
Support for the New Cabinet.
The new Cabinet is not supported by the entire nation.
The Minseito (the minority party in the Lower House) has
agreed to work with the Cabinet. The Seiyukai is opposed
to the Cabinet, except for the faction following Mr. Tokonami,
which is computed at some thirty members. The Kokumin Domei
(Mr. Adachi's party) is opposed to the new Cabinet because it
is not a "fascist" government, but reserves its opposition
for the time being. The Kenkyukai, the majority party in
the House of Peers, will cooperate with the Cabinet if its
general policies agree with those of the Kenkyukai. The
Meirinkai (an association of ex-service officers) opposes
the Cabinet because it has affiliated itself with the "cor-
rupt" political parties, but the Meirinkai will reserve its
riew

=== Page 555 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
views for the present. The Nippon Seisanto (the influential
reactionary society of Mitsuru Toyama) is opposed to the
Cabinet because Admiral Okada is supposed to have supported
the London Naval Treaty.
The Okada Cabinet therefore appears to have the support
of the liberal elements, but is opposed by the reactionary
elements and some of the professional politicians. It will
be able, in all probability, to command a majority in the
House of Peers, but there is some doubt regarding its sup-
port in the Lower House. The Seiyukai has in the past few
sessions commanded a clear majority in the Lower House, but
with the defection of Tokonami and his followers, it is
doubtful if the Seiyukai can continue to enjoy its predomi-
nant position. If the Tokonami faction allies itself with
the Minseito, it is possible that the new Cabinet will be
able to command a majority in the Lower House. If not, the
Diet may be dissolved and a general election held.
Policies of the Okada Cabinet.
No general policy of the Okada Cabinet has as yet been
announced, although it was intimated to the newspapermen
that such a statement would be issued after the Cabinet meet-
ing on July 13. The Embassy has received indirectly the
information that ex-Premier Saito stated in a private in-
terview that the Okada Cabinet would follow exactly the
policies of the Saito Cabinet - that there would be no
change at all in either domestic or foreign policies. More-
over, Admiral Saito stated that Finance Minister Fujii would
followexactly the financial policies of his predecessor, Mr.
Takahashi.
The individual Ministers, however, expressed themselves
okada
briefly as to their policies shortly after the Cabinet was
inducted

=== Page 556 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
inducted into office. Thus, Mr? Hirota is reported to have
said (JAPAN ADVERTISER translations):
"I intend to follow the policy of international
co-operation, defined at the Five-Minister Con-
ference under the Saito Cabinet. As regards the
prevailing situation in Europe, I will listen to
explanations from Ambassador Naotake Sato, who will
return shortly from France. The Hull-Hirota mes-
sages reveal the present Japan-American situation
but they are not sufficient. So I will listen to
the opinion of Ambassador Hiroshi Saito of Wash-
ington, and I am prepared to take concrete steps
to promote better relations.
"Japan's relations with China are improving. Our
relations with the Soviet Union are not bad. The
Manchurian question is the most important matter
at present for Japan, and the Government and the
people must give their support.
"The world at present is uneasy economically and
politically. In order to surmount this,the Powers
must cooperate. The Disarmament Conference of next
year will be the best means of realizing the co-op-
eration of the Powers. As Japan is responsible
for the maintenance of peace in the Orient, it is
necessary for this country to maintain the arma-
ment necessary. The world's tendency toward arma-
ment increase is illustrative of the world's un-
certainty. Japan must give the world guidance in
establishing peace."
Mr. Fujii, Minister for Finance, is reported to have said:
"As you are aware, Japan is facing a crisis in-
ternally and externally. The nation must solve
important financial and economic problems. Ef-
forts of the Government alone will not be enough
to surmount the difficulties, and public support
is sincerely hoped for. I will not rapidly change
the financial policy, but will follow the Taka-
hashi plan. Close investigation will be made before
I carry out a policy. I do not think the present
unbalanced Government budget should be left as it
is.
"As regards taxes, I do not necessarily believe
the deficit has to be made good with tax increases
alone, since increased expenditures are a matter
of course in connection with the Manchurian affair
and replenishment of national defense, although a
tax increase will be necessary."
Arhe statements made by other Ministers at that time consisted
of platitudes. and are of no significance, except that Justice
Napshw
Minister"Obara stated that he "attached the greatest impor-
tance to the rectification of official discipline and puri-
fication

=== Page 557 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
fication of elections", indicating that he will continue
his predecessor's policy of uncovering graft whenever and
wherever discovered.
On July 13 the Japanese newspapers stated that Admiral
Osumi, the Navy Minister, had submitted a program to Premier
Okada, calling for the renunciation by Japan of the principle
of naval ratios, for autonomy in national defense, for in-
sistence on Japan's claims to the mandated South Sea islands,
for completion of Japan's Manchurian policy, and for adjust-
ment of relations with the Soviet Union through the solution
of the Chinese-Eastern Railway problem and through the re-
newal of oil-production concessions in Russian Saghalien.
There has been considerable newspaper discussion of the
desirability of renewing the Five-Minister Conferences (the
five ministers consisting of the Premier and the Foreign,
Navy, Army, and Finance ministers), in order that the policies
of the Saito Cabinet may be explained and continued, with such
changes as the changed times would seem to warrant. It is
believed by the political writers for the vernacular news-
papers, however, that the Ministers appointed from the poli-
tical parties, none of whom hold posts entitling them to
participation in the Five-Minister Conferences, would object
seriously to the conferences.
Of Human Interest.
The Japanese newspapers usually endeavor to find items
of human interest in regard to persons in the public eye. In
the case of Admiral Okada, they have stressed his poverty and
his love of alcoholic beverages. It is stated that when he
accepted

=== Page 558 === (confidence 0.97)
- 12 -
accepted the Imperial command to form a Cabinet, he possessed
only Yen 400 in ready cash, and as he was compelled to spend
money at the rate of Yen 200 a day while forming the Cabinet,
he had to borrow Yen loo0 from his relatives. Also that,
when he moved into the Premier's official residence from his
humble and austere private residence, he brought with him
only a taxicab-load of personal effects and no servants ex-
cept a maid who will come each day from his private home
to take care of his clothing. Moreover, the people of his
native province, Fukui, learning that the Admiral had only
a heavy winter frock coat in which to make his official calls
in the summer heat, are reported to have decided to donate
a summer-weight frock coat to the Admiral.
Respectfully yours,
Noseta.Ju
Joseph C. Grew.

MyH:
You might glance through Tokyo's despatch
No. 918 of August 3, 1934, transmitting the
text of the statement of policy issued by
Admiral Okada after the formation of his
Cabinet.
The more important portions of the
statement were cabled over by press
correspondents some weeks ago, while the
remainder is couched in the usual vague
language and seems to have no definite
meaning.
However, there does seem to be some
talk of a special session of the Diet
being called to discuss agricultural relief.
As pointed out in another despatch it may
be possible that the Government may, if the
Seiyukai shows itself to be intransigent,
dissolve the Diet and call for the holding
of a general election. I consider this,
however, an unlikely development.

Sir:
1/
There is transmitted herewith a copy of the state-
3E
ment of policy of the recently formed cabinet of Admira
c33
Okada issued on July 20 and published in THE JAPAN ADVE
TISER of July 21, 1934. This version has been checked
and found to correspond accurately with the text issued
by the Foreign Office both in Japanese and English.
There is little to comment upon in this declaration
of policy which consists of general statements that the
Government will: (1) purify national politics and enforce
official discipline, thereby contributing to national
welfare

=== Page 569 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
welfare; (2) purify the thought of the nation; (3)
endeavor to ensure international peace while leaving
nothing undone "for the fulfillment of the mission that
is Japan's"; (4) exert in the coming disarmament con-
ference "every effort for the assurance of national
safety through fair and justifiable means"; (5) endeavor
to restore the equilibrium between national income and
expenditure; (6) improve the nation's living conditions
with particular attention to those engaged in fishing,
farming and forestry; (7) establish a general indus-
trial policy embracing all branches of economic interests;
(8) pay especial attention to the cultivation of the
"Nippon Spirit" and to the educational system; (9) colla-
borate with the Government of "Manchukuo" on the basic
principle of mutual assistance and mutual benefit; (10)
pay ccreful attention to the improvement of the organs
of administration with a view to insuring promptness
and fuirness in the conduct of daily public business.
This statement of policy was immediately attacked
by members of the House of Peers, the Seiyukai, and by
prominent businessmen, the chief points of criticism
being the fact that it w: s considered to be vague and
that it did not state whether or not a special Diet Ses-
sion would be called to deal with the question of agri-
cultural relief. In addition, members of the House of
Peers pointed out that while the financial policy of
the previous Salto Cabinet had been excellent, they did
not believe that this policy should be followed blindly
by

=== Page 570 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
by the new Cabinet; modification was required "to meet
changing conditions".
Premier Okada countered these criticisms by saying
that he had no intention of calling a special Diet session
until he had a concrete program drawm up for the relief
of conditions among the agricultural population. He
did not, however, comment upon the charge that the state-
ment was vague.
It is noteworthy that no statement concerning the
Government's policy with regard to the forthcoming naval
conference or the preliminary conversations was included;
this subject was touched upon later in an interview which
Premier Okada gave to the newspaper correspondents on
August 1 and will be discussed in a separate despatch.
Respectfully yours,

Copy of statement.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 10, 1934.
Following is a brief review of Tokyo's
despatch No. 908 of July 31; 1934, which
describes the present position of political
parties in Japan.
The assassination of Premier Inukai in
May, 1932, was a clear indication by the
militarists and other reactionaries that
professional politicians would no longer be
permitted to control the national destiny.
Consequently when Admiral Saito formed his
Cabinet he virtually disregarded the political
parties and selected his associates entirely
with a view to their fitness. However, when
Admiral Okada formed his Cabinet last July
he made definite efforts to propitiate the
political parties and arranged for their
representation in his Cabinet.
The Embassy believes that the change
that has taken place indicates a revival of
the influence of the political parties and
a gradual rise of the parties "in the
estimation of the nation". It concludes from
these circumstances that there has been a
corresponding decrease in the influence of
the military and reactionary elements.
The Seigukai has been divided into three
groups: one, which favors cooperation with
the

=== Page 573 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
the present Government, a second, under the
leadership of the president of the party,
which is opposed to cooperation with the
Government and advocates the return of power
to the political parties, and a third faction
which holds pretty much the same opinion but
is hostile to the present leadership in the
party. The Minseito, the minority party,
supports the Government.
In view of covert threats by the
Sei ukai to impeach the Government at the
next session of the Diet, there has been
talk of the Government calling a special
session of the Diet for the ostensible
reason of dealing with urgent economic
problems but in reality to create an
occasion to dissolve the Diet, which would
call for the holding of a general election.
The Government is said to be, however, not
certain whether the present situation
fulfills the requirement of the Constitution
that in the event of "urgent necessity" a
special session of the Diet may be called.
It is suggested that after reading this
review you begin on page 8 of the despatch
and read the section marked "Future of the
Political Parties in Japan".
Comment:

=== Page 574 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
Comment:
The despatch does not explain sufficient-
ly clearly why the Army and Navy should be
concerned over the effect of the vetoing by
the Seiyukai of the military and naval
estimates for the fiscal year beginning
Apr11 1, 1935.
The Diet opens at the end of December
and immediately takes a recess until about
the 25th of January, when important bills
are introduced by the Government. If the
budget is not approved by the Diet the
expenditures of the Government are limited
under the Constitution to the expenditures
authorized in the budget for the preceding
year. The despatch explains that the naval
and military estimates for the coming
fiscal year are greater than the expenditures
authorized for the present year.
Upon the dissolution of the Diet a
certain period of time (I believe 60 days)
must elapse before the general elections
can be held. If, therefore, the Diet
voted down the budget at its regular
session, there would not be sufficient time
to dissolve the Diet, hold the elections,
and convene the new Diet before the end of
the fiscal year. In those circumstances
the

=== Page 575 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 4 -
the only way in which additional funds
could be obtained for the Army and Navy
would be to declare the existence of
"urgent necessity" and convene a special
session. It is obvious that the Government
would like to avoid describing Japan's
international relations as creating "urgent
necessity".
It is my personal opinion that the
Seiyukai would hesitate to invite an act
of dissolution, as I believe there has
been no case on record in the last twenty
years where a Government has lost a general
election.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
: Tokyo, July 31, 1934.
No. 908.
Subject: Present Position of Political Parties in Japan.
Aly
894.00
SosymJE
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
894,00/535
GG
Degartment of State

DEPA
ON1

MID
COPIES SENT TO
The Honorable
O.N.I. ANDM I.D.
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
SEP 1 3 1934
FILED
I have the honor to report that, although the recently.
formed Cabinet is alleged to be a "national", or non-party,
Cabinet, there appears to be a tendency to revert to the
party system of government in Japan, while at the same time
the political parties, which have been distinctly out of
public favor since 1932, appear to be regaining some of their
former influence. This is indicated by the attention which
has been paid, both by the public and by the members of the
Cabinet, to the attitude of the political parties toward
the present political situation.
When

=== Page 577 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
When the Saito Cabinet was formed in May, 1932, almost
no attention was given to the desires or influences of the
political parties. The assassination of Premier Inukai,
the leader of the Seiyukai, had very definitely demonstrated
to the professional politicians that certain factions of the
nation (primarily the military and the reactionaries) would
no longer permit the political parties, in their then posi-
tion of dependence upon the financial houses, to control the
government. Consequently when Admiral Viscount Saito formed
his Cabinet, he made almost no attempt to conciliate the
political parties and selected his ministers of state from
all factions, regardless of their influence or status. When
Admiral Okada formed his Cabinet in July, 1934, however, he
called upon the leaders of the political parties in person
and offered the Seiyukai and the Minseito each two posts in
the new Cabinet. This was later changed to three posts for
the Seiyukai and two for the Minseito, the offers being ac-
cepted by the Minseito but not by the Seiyukai. The three
Seiyukai members who individually accepted the posts in the
Okada Cabinet were summarily expelled from the party. The
fact, however, that a certain number of posts in the Cabinet
were offered to each political party shows that a need was felt
of conciliating the parties.
Moreover, in the selection of Parliamentary Vice Ministers
or Counselors, Admiral Okada endeavored to conciliate the poli-
tical parties, by appointing nine Vice Ministers from the
Seiyukai, nine from the Minseito and six from the House of
Peers. The nine selected from the Seiyukai were promptly ex-
pelled from the party, indicating the irreconcilable attitude
of

=== Page 578 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of that party. The Peers also apparently are not satis-
fied with their representation in the Cabinet (no member
of the Cabinet proper is a peer) and are reported to be
inclined to adopt a cold attitude toward the Cabinet.
The fact, however, that overtures were made to the
two leading political parties indicates the importance which
is being placed on their influence in the nation today, as
compared with their influence two years ago. In addition
to indicating the gradual rise of the political parties in
the estimation of the nation, the fact that efforts were
made to conciliate the parties indicatec a significant decrease
in the influence of the military and reactionary elements,
which were and still are opnosed to the "corrupt" political
parties.
Position of the Seiyukai.
The Seiyukai, which has been striving for some time
past for a return to power, is decidedly opposed to the
Okada Cabinet, but it is doubtful if its influence will
be sufficiently great to cause the overthrow of the Govern-
ment. The party itself is split with dissension, there
being three f ctions more or less opposed to each other.
The principal faction is that of Dr. Kisaburo Suzuki,
the President of the party, who has issued a statement
to the press definitely opposing the Okada government. He
denounced the Okada Cabinet as a "bureaucratic" government,
composed of officials with no contact with the people, and
stated that if the Seiyukai believes in constitutional
government.

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The second faction in importance in the Seiyukai
consists of the Tokonami section. This section, led by
the present Minister for Communications, Mr. Takejiro
Tokonami, and including Mr. Tatsunosuke Yamasaki, Minister
for Agriculture end Forestry, Mr. Shinya Uchida, Minister
for Railways, and some thirty other members of the party,
has long been in conflict with the Suzuki faction and now,
with the definite defection of its three most prominent
members to the side of the Okada Cabinet, may possibly
secede from the party. There has been considerable
discussion of the possibility of a coalition of the Toko-
nami faction of the Seiyrukai with the Minseito, thus
creating a new party which may be able to control a ma-
jority in the Lower House of the Diet. The attitude of
the thirty followers of Mr. Tokonami does not yet appear
to have been definitely fixed, however, and the latest
reports would indicate that there is a possibility of a
reconciliation between the Tokonumi faction nd the rest
of the Beiyukai, by means of which Seiyukai support of
the Okada Cabinet may be assured and the necessity of a
general election for the purpose of obtaining support
for the Government may be avoided.
A third faction in the Seiyukai is led by Mr. Fusa-
nosuke Kuhara, formerly a shipping magnate of Kobe with a
somewhat unsavory reputation. Mr. Kuhara has been endeavoring
to seize control of the Seiyukai, but it is now reported that
he is seeking peace with Dr. Suzuki, in an attempt to avoid
a break-up of the party.
The

=== Page 580 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
A
The older members of the Seiyukai are reported to be
working to prevent a split in the party by a reform from
within. They believe that recuperation of the party can
be attained by a revision of the system of management by
means of which Dr. Suzuki has become a virtual autocrat
within the party, and by restraining the influence of Mr.
Kuhara, which has not been conducive either to peace within
the party or to public confidence outside the party. Other
groups in the party are advocating peace within the party
at any price, as the public is losing confidence in the
political parties because of their constant internal dis-
sensions and because some measure of peace is necessary
for the restoration of party government.
The Position of the Minseito.
The Minseito, the minority party in the Lower House
of the Diet, has not for some time past been in a position
sufficiently strong to aspire to the formation of a ministry
itself, and consequently it has willingly cooperated both
with the Seito and the Okada Governments. The party has
been maintaining its solidarity and, to a greater extent
than the Seiyukai, the respect of the public. At the same
670
time, the Minseito has not nearly so strong a political
6.70
of
1340
machine as has the Seiyukai, and in case a general election
it is doubtful if the Minseito would obtain a working
majority in the Lower House. As mentioned before, however,
it is possible that the Minseito will be strengthened by
the adherence of the Tokonami faction from the Seiyukai.
Parties in the House of Peers.
It is reported that the two most powerful groups, or
parties, in the House of Peers, the Kenkyukai and the Koseikai,
are

=== Page 581 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
are inclined to be cold toward the Okada Cabinet, partly
because Admiral Okada failed to include a member of the peerage
in his Cabinet, and partly because it is considered that the
Admiral bungled the formation of his Cabinet and thereby
aroused the active opposition of the Seiyukai. At any rate,
when the Admiral approached the leaders of the two groups
for the purpose of obtaining their cooperation in the appoint-
ment of Parliamentary Vice Ministers, they are reported to
have declined to give their assistance. Consequently it is
assumed by the newspapers that these two powerful groups in
the Peers, while perhaps not actively opposing the present
Government, will not give it whole-hearted support. Admiral
Okada is said to have depended upon his Minister for Home
Affairs, Mr. Fumio Goto, who is a nominated member of the
House of Peers, to strengthen his position with the Upper
House, but apparently this means of approach has failed to
accomplish the result intended.
Question of Dissolution of the Diet.
When it became obvious that the principal faction in the
Seiyukai, the majority party in the Lower House, would actively
oppose the Okada Cabinet, the question arose as to whether or
not the Government would convene a special session of the Diet,
ostensibly to consider the rice and silk relief problems,but
actually for the purpose of dissolving the Diet and calling
for a general election, in the expectation of obtaining
enough support in the new Diet to carry out its policies.
During the previous Diet session a joint resolution was
introduced

=== Page 582 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
introduced by the Seiyukai and the Minseito, calling upon
the then Government to convene a special session of the Diet
in the autumn of 1934 for the purpose of considering
agrarian relief, especially the relief of rice and silk
growers. The Seiyukai maintains that the present Govern-
ment is bound by this resolution to call a special session,
although the price of rice has risen appreciably in the
meantime and has rendered the problem of agrarian relief
less urgent. The fall in the price of silk cocoons, how-
ever, has placed the sericulturists in a bad position and
therefore there is still a demend for a special session.
The Premier has not yet given out his attitude toward the
guestion, and the Seiyukai threatens to impeach the Govern-
ment at the next regular session of the Diet if the ob-
ligation imposed by the joint resolution is not fulfilled.
The faction of the Seiyukai demanding the special session
is that of Dr. Suzuki, and it is assumed that he wishes
to have the special session called in order to assert the
strength of his party and compel the Government to conform
to the desires of the Seiyukai.
Another- and perhaps a more cogent- reason for calling
a special session in the autumn is connected with the passing
of the budget. The Army and Navy budget estimates for
the coming fiscal year are considerably in excess of those
for the present year and it is certain that every effort
will be made by the Army and Navy to have them passed. If
the Seiyukai, however, insists upon opposing the Govern-
ment, it is more than probable that the Government's budget
will be singled out for attack and that it will not be passed.
In

=== Page 583 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
In such case the budget for the previous year will be
continued, which will not please the Army and Navy and
may cause further acts of violence. Consequently the
Cabinet may find it necessary to convene the Diet in
the autumn, dissolve it at the first opportunity,
and call a general election in the hope of breaking
the Seiyukai's majority and obtaining the support
necessary to pass the Government measures. In such
case a special session would have to be called in Sep-
tember, in order to hold the general election in October
and have the new members of the Diet inducted into
office in time for the regular session in December.
The Cabinet, however, appears to be in doubt. as to
the advisability of calling a special session (probably
because of fear that & favorable majority will not be
returned in case a general election becomes necessary)
and is said to be debating the question of whether or
not, under the Japanese constitution, a special session
can be calleá for the purpose of dissolution or of
seeking a vote of confidence.
Future of the Political Parties in Japan.
The Okada Government is said to have been intended
by the Genro, Prince Saionji, to pave the way to a return
to normal politics and normal constitutional government
in Japan. The Okada Cabinet is variously termed "national",
"bureaucratic" or "quasi-party" by the different news-
papers, according to their political complexion, but
in fact it seems to be a super-party Cabinet with much
more

=== Page 584 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
more contact with the political world than the Saito
Cabinet had, and consequently indicates an intention
on the part of the Genro (who is n ardent advocate of
constitutional government) and other senior statesmen
to lead the nation gredually back to normal political
conditions.
At the same time, the HOCHI in its issue of July 19,
1934, asserts that, disgusted with the corrupt connection
between the political perties and the financial interests,
the senior statesmen reconinended the organization of a
"national" Cabinet, nd that the bureaucrats are taking
advantage of the on ortunity offered them to endeavor
to crush the political parties. It is also asserted
in the editorial referred to that the leaders of the
nation vere opposed to the present monopolistic system
of capitalism in Janen and wanted to replace it with a
system of managed economy, and consequently recommended
a "national" Cabinet in tead of a party Cabinet, the
parties being affiliated with the mononolistic financial
houses.
There has been some speculation on the part of the
newspapers as to whether or not Admiral Okada deliberately
moved to spiit the Seiyukai when he asked Mr. Tokonami,
Mr. Uchida and Mr. Yamasaki
to join his Cabinet. It is
claimed by some that it was a bungling move, as it pro-
voked the active opposition of the major section of the
party, but others regard it as a very astute move, cal-
culated to split the Seiyukai and thus destroy its hold
on the Lower House of the Diet.
Another

=== Page 585 === (confidence 0.96)
-10-
Another report, quoted in an editorial in the FUKUOKA
NICHI NICHI of July 19, 1934, is to the effect that Mr.
Fumio Goto, Minister for Home Affairs in the present Cabinet,
is highly dissatisfied with the political parties in Japan
and is planning to organize a young men's Cabinet with
Prince Konoye as Premier. It is said that Prince Konoye
and Mr. Goto are closely allied, and that Mr. Goto is in
favor of a purely bureaucratic form of government. It
is also reported that both of them are, to some extent,
at least, in sympathy with the so-called "fascist" element
in Japan.
As will be noted from the above, the political situation
in Japan can haraly be called static; it is rather in a
state of flux, and what will emerge later cannot be pre-
dicted at present. The fact remains, however, that there
is decidedly less tendency on the part of the nation's
leaders to ignore the political parties than there was two
years ago.
Respectfully yours
Nth
Joseph C. Grew.

JAPAN.

894.00
Japan is a constitutional monarchy. Under the Constitu-
tion all legislative, judicial.and administrative powers are
vested in the Emperor: laws are enacted, with the concurrence
894.00/53
of Parliament, by the Emperor; justice is dealt out in his
name; and the country is administered by him through his min-
isters. The Cabinet of Ministers is non-parliamentary, being
responsible only to the Emperor. Under the Constitution, the
Emperor "has the supreme command of the Army and Navy" and
"determines the organization and peace standing of the Army
and Navy". These provisions operate to give to the military
and naval establishments, as in the case of the former German
36
Empire, a marked measure of freedom from control by the Cabinet.
Domestic Political and Economic Conditions.
Although Japan enjoyed during the War a degree of pros-
perity such as it had never experienccd before, the depression
which began in 192o was perhaps felt more severely in Japan
than in any other important industrial country. Owing to the
very heavy material losses resulting from the earthquake of 1923
and to a severe financial crisis thut took place in 1927, the
country had not returned to normally prosperous conditions when
in 1929 the present general world-wide economic depression set
in. There had been a period of almost ten years during which
the prices of raw silk and of rice, upon which the Japanese
agricultural element is primarily dependent for livelihood,
remained near or below the cost of production. Conditions had
developed similar to those which later developed in the Unite
FILED
States: farms were mortgaged and mortgages were foreclosed, 
crops were hypothecated, and the farmers as a class were reduced
to the most straitened circumstances. The Government had mada
but the measures employed were largely ineffectual. The urban
S
population had been the principal beneficiary from the favorable
foreign trade conditions which developed in i928 and 1929, but
those conditions could not survive in the face of the new world-
wide depression which began in 1929.
In short, no substantial progress had been made toward
solving certain basic problems which arose from an increasing
population rapidly approaching the point of saturation under the
conditions then existing. At a timo when there were marked.
evidences of widespread social unrest and discontent, atten-
tion was drawn by certain sensational events to the existence
on a wide scale of corrupt practices by politicians. There
occurred a general revulsion against political parties which
resulted

=== Page 587 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
resulted in a corresponding loss of popular confidence in
parliamentary government.
The temper of the people was such that th y. were prepared
to look favorably upon any program calculated to break the
impassc in which they conceived themsclves to be. Following
the action taken by the Japanese Army in Manchuria in September,
1931, which was represented to the Japanese people as "positive"
action calculated to lead inter alia to a solution of Japan's
economic and social problems -- problems which the government
of politicians had been unable to solve --, the party system
fell into disrepute. During the past two years authority has
been in the hands of a Government representative of, or favored
by, the various elements which have a voice in national affairs.
The political parties still exist but during recent months the
weakness inherent in them has asserted itself more and more
conspicuously through internal dissent, resignations, and the
ostablishment of new political groups. Since l93l, the military
element -- not the political party element -- has been the
dominant factor in the government.
The phenomenal increase in Japan's foreign trade during
the recent years of goneral world depression has been popularly
attributed to low wages, depreciated currency and "dumping".
Low wages and the depreciation in the exchange value of Japan-
ese currency undoubtedly have been and are now important fac-
tors, but another factor the importance of which is being in-
creasingly recognized is a plan, inștituted after several years
of study, to coordinate and make more effective Japan's agen-
cies of production and distribution. Thero has been established
a governmental bureau whose principal functions are to bring
about elimination of unnecessary costs of production, to regu-
late and to reduce competition among producers, and to conduct
research and investigations into new methods of production.
The bureau also has authority to supervise the activities of
exporters with a view to eliminating competition and to pre-
venting in this connection practices injurious to Japanese com-
mercial interests.
Substantial results have been achieved both in the field
of production and in the field of distribution. For example,
in the rayon industry manufacturing costs were reduccd in the
two years 1930 to 1932 by 60 percent, while in the iron and
steel industry the index of efficiency bused on a standard of
100 in 1925 was raised in 1933 to 208. Manufacturers and ex-
porters of cach principal commodity have been required to form
a guild, which guild has authority under law to apportion for-
eign markets among its members, to inspect articles intended
for export and when advisable to refuse a license for exporta-
tion. Illustrating the coordination and centralized direction
which are featured in the plan, the purchasing of foreign com-
modities has been transferred from one country to another in or-
der to meet complaints of those countries which purchased more
from Japan than they were able to sell to Japan. For example,
in the case of Argentina, which formerly had an unfavorable
balance of trade with Japan, the Japanese increased their pur-
chases (largely of wool and other primary commodities hitherto
purchased elsewhere) during the first four months of the
present year to a point where Argentina exported to Japan
approximately six times as much as it did in the same period in
1932, with the result that Argentina sold approximately twice
as

=== Page 588 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
as much to Japan as Japan sold to Argentina.
The latest information available indicates that the rate
of incrcase which occurred in Japan's total exports during
1932 and 1933 is still being maintained. Its exports for
the first four months of the present year are approximately
80 percent greater than its exports for the corresponding
period in 1932.
According to official reports, practically all the im-
portant industrial and commercial firms in Japan were at the
end of 1933 in sounder financial condition than in 1930, their
total profits had been materially increased, their debts had
been reduced or been refunded at lower rates, and their re-
serves were larger. Notwithstanding the depreciation of the
currency in terms of foreign exchange, labor costs have ad-
vanced very slightly, and although commodity prices have moved
upward the advance has been relatively smailer than the degree
to which the currency has been depreciated. There has been
some inflation in note issue but such inflation is probably
warranted by improved business conditions; and there appears
to be no lack of confidence in the currency. Total bank de-
posits have increased during the past three years by about a
billion. yen, and there is no evidence that there has been dur-
ing that period any decrease in the capital resources of the
country.
On the other hand the situation wiih respect to Govern-
ment finances is less favorable, due to the issuance of bonds
and treasury notes to mcet the heavy deficits of the national
budget. The actual increase in national bonded indebtedness
during the past three years has been about three billion yen.
So long as the foreign tradc of Japan can be maintained at
approximately the present levels, there would seem to be no
reasonable basis for any suggestion that a collapse of the
financial structure of the country is impending. However,
measures have been taken or are under contemplation in various
countries to restrict imports from Japan, and if such measures
should collectively result in a material curtailment of Japan's
markets in foreign countrics, it is not unlikely that the ex-
pansion in Japanese industry recently effected in anticipa-
tion of a growing demand for Japanese products may give rise
to serious problems.
Commercial Relations with India.
The boycott instituted in 1931 by the Chinese against
Japanese goods forced the Japanese textile industry to develop
foreign markets elsewhere which would absorb the production
of Japanese milis which had hitherto found their most important
outlet in China. Exports of Japanese cotton textiles to India
during 1932 were substantially increascd, largely at the ex-
pense of competing British manufacturers. The Indian duty
on cotton textiles from other than British countries was
finally increased to 75 porcent ad valorem which afforded
British textiles a substantial preference. donsiderable re-
sentment was felt in Japan against both the British and the
Indian Governments over what was considered to be an act of
discrimination against Japanese goods, and the Japanese cotton
spinners

=== Page 589 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
spinners instituted in the carly part of 1933 a boycott against
the purchase of Indian cotton. Japan normally purchases from
15 to 20 percent of India's cotton crop, and the Japanese boy-
cott, which was effectively maintained, was sharply felt by the
Indian cotton growers. Tho British cotton spinners, on the
other hand, are not important purchascrs of Indian cotton -
for the reason that the British mills are not equipped to use
the slort staple Indian cotton. Yielding to pressure from the
Indian cotton growers, the Indian Government with the approval
of the British Government agreed to enter into a conference
with the Japanese Government with a view to finding a mutually
satisfactory settlement of this problem.
A conference was held this year and, after several months
of deliberation, a convention was concluded. The convention
provides that the Indian tariff on Japanese cotton textiles
shall be reduced from 75 porcent ad valorem to 50 percent
ad valorem, and it permits the entry unnually into India
of 325,000,000 square yards of textiles from Japan (approxi-
mately the amount imported in 1931), provided that Japan pur-
chases annually 1,Oo0,Ooo bales of Indian cotton. The conven-
tion further provides that if the Japanese purchase 1,500,000
bales or more of Indian cotton the maximum quota for imports
of Japanese textiles into India shall be increased to
400,000,000 square yards. This convention has not as yet, ac-
cording to the most recent information available to the Depart-
ment, been ratified, but the two Governments are proceeding in
accordance with the teims of the convention.
Commerciel Relations wi ch the Netherland East Indies.
The Netherland Easi Indics have for some years been an
important market for Japanese goods but following Japan's
serious difficulties with China which began in 193l particular
efforts were made to develop oxports to that area. In 1933,
Japan's oxports to the Netherland East Indies exceeded in
value its exports to any other country except the United
States, Manchuria and India. The Netherland East Indies Gov-
ernment took successive measures to rustrict imports from
Japan, but the Japanese, who have for many years enjoyed a
favorable balance in their trade with the Netherland East
Indies, took no retaliatory measures. The Japanese Government,
however, entered vigorous protests, and, after protracted
conversations, arrangements were made for the holding of a
conference between the Japanese Government and the Government
of the Netherland East Indies to explore the possibilities
of regulating their commercial relations by treaty. A
Japanese trade delegation arrived at Batavia on June 3, 1934,
and has since been in negotiation with representatives of the
Netherland East Indies.The likelihood of the conference
yielding a great measure of success is regarded with pessimism
both in Japanese and in Netherland quarters. It is understood
that the Netherland East Indiea Govcrnment has indicated readi-
ness to grant certain concessions to thc Japanese Government on
condition that Japan increase its purchases of Java sugar. The
efforts of the Japanese Government to dovelop the sugar industry
in the Japanese colony of Taiwan would appear to be an obstacle
to the acceptance by the Japanese of that condition.
Relations

=== Page 590 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Relations with Soviet Union.
The relations between Japan and the Soviet Union which
have been severely tried by the almost complete elimination
by Japan of Russian influence from North Manchuria, are almost.
entirely political or politico-economic in character, the
volume of trade between the two countries being inconsiderable.
The issue which has attracted the widest attention is that
which involves the Chinese Eastern Railway, a railway line
which was built by Russia across North Manchuria as a shorter
alternative route to the Amur section of the Trans-Siberian
Railway. For over a year negotiations have been held between
the Soviet Government on the one hand and the Japanese Gover-
ment and the regime in Manchuria on the other hand looking
toward the sale to "Mancnukuo" of the sovict interest in the
railway. It is understood that concessions have been made
on both sides and that the difiorence between the price asked
by the Soviet Governmont and the price offered by the prospec-
tive purchasers has been narrowed down, but there has been
achieved thus far no agrcement. In fact, the negotiations
have reached, or appcar to be reaching, a state of deadlock.
By agreemont with the Soviet Union, Japar se nationals
have the privilege of fishing in Soviet territ :ial waters.
Every year, however, disputes arise in regard o the areas
in which the Japanese are permitted to fish and the amount
of the fees to be paid to the Soviet Union.
The fact that the boundary between Manchuria and Siberia
has not been accurately defined has been another fruitful
source of trouble between the Soviet Union and Japan. The
Soviet Government and the rogime in Manchuria have undertaken
by conference to define the boundary.
At the end of 1931, the Soviet Government proposed to
the Japanese Governmont the conclusion of a treaty of non-
aggression. It has been reported that the Japanese Government
stated in its reply to that proposal that the various questions
outstanding between the two countries snould be adjusted prior
to the conclusion of a treaty of non-aggression; and that on the
other hand the Soviet Government has reiterated that the con-
clusion of a treaty of non-aggression would facilitate the ad-
justment of those issues.
There is thus in the relations between Japan and the Soviet
Union the basis for constant disputes of a sporadic character.
In addition thcre are in the relations between the countries cer-
tain fundamental opposing forces, among which may be mentioned
(a) the conflict of interests and aspirations in and with respect
to Continental Eastern Asia; (b) Japan's repugnance to communism;
(c) the concern felt in gertain quarters in Japan over the
presence of Soviet air forces at Vladivostok, which is only
some six hundred miles from the large industrial cities of
Japan.
Relations with China.
The political relations of Japan with virtually all the
other Great Powers may bo regarded as revolving around its
relations

=== Page 591 === (confidence 0.99)
-6
relations with China. Japan's trade with China, including
Manchuria, is second only to its trade with the United States.
Japan has invested something over a billion dollars in China,
and there are more Japanese nationals residing in China than
in any other foreign country. The absence of a strong central
government in China, and the civil and military disorders which
have prevailed in that country over an extended period of time,
on the one hand, and the presence in a nearby country of a
well-disciplined, vigorous and militant people, as are the
Japanese, on the other hand, are factors which have operated
toward creating and maintuining conditions of unrest in the
relations between these two countries, and, incident thereto,
in the relations between Japan and the other Powers that have
interests in China.
NOTE: Certain phases of the relatins between China and
Japan are discussed in a separate number of Information Series
which is devoted to the subject of China.
Relations with thc United States.
The interests of the United States in Japan are primarily
commercial and secondarily philanthropic. The United States is
the largest consumer of Japan's exports, while the value of the
exports of the United States to Japan is exceeded only by the
value of its exports to (a) Great Britain and to (b) Canada.
The United States ordinarily purchases about 95 percent of
Japan's total exports of raw silk, while Japan is the largest
foreign purchaser of American raw cotton. American investments
in Japan, consisting of American holdings of Japanese government,
municipal and industrial bonds, and investments of American cap-
ital in American-managed industrial enterprises in Japan, and
invostments by missionary bodies in churches, schools and other
institutions, are estimated to total about $400,000,000.
The majority of Americans resident in Japan are engaged
in missionary and educational work. American missionaries
founded several of the principal private Japanese universities
and colleges, and they took a position of leadership in the
establishing of hsopitals and asylums and in other forms of
eleemosynary work. Most of the principal American missions
are preparing, as the Japanese churches show a capacity to
administer and maintain themselves, to curtail the scope of
their work with a view to eventual withdrawal from Japan.
An important problem in the relations between the
United States and Japan is that of naval disarmament. Al-
though no authoritative disclosure of Japan's naval policy
has thus far been made, it would seem to be clear from state-
ments made by prominent Japanese naval officers that the
Japanese Navy, supported by the nationalistic element of the
country, favors abandoning the ratio principle in regulating
naval armament and substituting therefor an allotment to the
powers parties to the naval treaties of total tonnages which
each would be free, within certain limits, to distribute in
accordance with its needs among the various categories of
vessels.
Since the passage of the Unitcd States Immigration
Act of 1924 there has existed in Japan resentment against
the

=== Page 592 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
the United States because of the provisions of that Act
which operate to prevent the entry into the United States
of Japanese as immigrants. The question of legislation
to change our immigration laws is, of course, a matter for
the consideration primarily of the lugislative branch of the
Government. The primary concern of the Department of State
in reference to the question of amending the existing law so
as to remove the provisions which wound Japanese pride re-
lates to the effect which the introduction in Congress of a
proposal for a change would have and the effect which the
adoption or the rejection of such a proposal would have,
in the field of our international relations. There is sub-
stantial evidence that thcre are, both on the Pacific Coast
and in other sections of the country, not only opinions
favorable but opinions unfavorable to proposals for altering
at this time the provisions of the immigration law.
Summary.
In tho relations between the United States and Japan,
there are outstanding very few problems which are of concern
exclusively to the tvo countries.
There are, however, several important and fundamental
problems affecting on the one hand Japan and on the other
hand several other nations, including the United States,
which are multilateral in character. Among such problems
are those arising from differences of attitude and view in
regard to Japan's action in Manchuria and to Japan's policy.
vis-a-vis China as disclosed in part by official acts and
in part by statements of highly placed Japanese authorities.
Other multilateral problems are those which arc created by.
Japan's commercial oxpansion and the competition which flows
therefmom. Another problem of international concern wherein
Japan is involved along with other powers is that of general
disarmament.
Because Japan and the United States both are powers
on the Pacific Ocean, becausc they both are outstandingly
important powers in that area, and because they differ in
view and attitude with regard to a number of fundamental
problems of international contacts, the relations between
the two countries are of first rank importance and concern
to the American people and tho American Governnent.

Tokyo's despatch No. 1068 of
November 30, 1934:
The two major Japanese political
parties, the Seiyukai and the Minseito, are
endeavoring, with s ome success, to regain
some of their lost prestige and influence.
They have announced a "coalition", which on
examination seems rather to be a temporary
truce in the attempt to carry public favor.
There seems to be no indication that
the two parties have come to a fundamental
agreement on policies. The Seiyukai is
onnosed to the present Okada Government,
while the Minseito has supported the
government since its incention. However,
their rapproachment at this time may assist
in restoring normal constitutional government
by enabling the parties to take concerted
action.

Sir:
In recent months Japanese leaders, the press, and
the public have manifested a tendency to pay more atten-
tion to the political parties, which have been in public
disfavor since 1932 but appear to be retrieving some of
their former influence.* This tendency was especially
noticeable at the time of the formation of the Okada
Cabinet in July and has been increasing in the past five
535
months.
F
*Embassy's despatch No. 908 July 31, 1934.

=== Page 595 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
months. Now that a special session of the Diet has been
convened (on November 28) the two principal political
parties, the Seiyukai and the Minseito, will have an op-
portunity to enunciate their views to the public and ac-
tively to exert greater efforts toard making their in-
fluence felt in the political life of the country.
In fact, two days before the convening of the extra-
oroinary session of the Diet, the Seiyukai and Minseito
issued a joint statement announcing a "coalition", of
which the following translation appeared in the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of November 28:
"The responsibility of the political
parties, which are based upon the popu-
lar will and exist to carry out parlia-
ment ry governnent, is especially heavy
at this time, when the country faces dif-
ficulties both at home and abroad. Under
the circumstances, they should act in ac-
corance with great principles, forgetting
their historical antagonisms and personal
feelings and ending their scramble for poli-

should work together whole-heartedly for the
salvation of the nation.
"With firm belief in this and for the sole
purpose of carrying it out, the coalition
of the Seiyukai and the Minseito has been
effected. It shall be the common aim of
the two parties to work together to promote
the best interests of the nation by aiding
in the formulation of fitting national policies
and by striving to relieve the suffering of
the people and to solve other difficulties con-
fronting the country."
It will be observed that the Seiyukai-Minseito announce-
ment is general and vague in character and does not indi-

operate on any concrete issues. The so-called coalition
would appear to have more the aspect of a temporary truce
during

=== Page 596 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
during the special session of the Diet than that of
a definite coalition of interests. From the wording
of the above-quoted statement it would appear that one
of the implicit purposes behind the coalition is a de-
sire on the part of the two parties to curry favor with
the public and to increase the separate political prestige
of each party.
There has been no public indication that the Seiyu-
kai and the Minseito have come to a fundamental agreement
on policies. What: ver attitude the Seiyukai may reveal
during the present session of the Diet, it is known that
this party is strongly against the Okada Government. On
the other hand, the Minseito has supported the Government
since its inception and there is no reason to believe
that it will lessen this support simoly because of the
coalition, since the Minseito has everything to gain and
prectically nothing to lose in cooperating with the Govern-
ment. This party has a minority in the Lower House of the
Diet an cannot reasonably hope for the formation of a
Ministry of its leaders. The divergency in the views of
the two principal parties was set in relief by certain
statements that Dr. Suzuki, the leader of the Seiyukai,
and Mr. Machida, chairman of the boara of Minseito managers
and Minister of Commerce and Industry in the present Cabinet,
made in speeches delivered on November 19 to parliamentary
members of the parties. Dr. Suzuki vigorously assailed
the Government, stating that his party was not going to
tolerate "makeshift measures with which the Government is
trying

=== Page 597 === (confidence 0.99)

trying to patch matters"; that the Government should
establish a permanent policy for the relief of the rural
population and the evelopment of industries; that it was
the duty of the Seiyukai party to examine on behalf of
the nation the question as to whether the Government had
carried out any of its basic policies as announced at the
time of its organization. Mr. Machida, who became chair-
man of the board of managers of the Minseito after Baron
Wakatsuki's recent resignation from the Presidency of the
party, stated that the attitude of the Minseito had not
changed from what it was while Baron Wakatsuki was Presi-

of the Government, in sharp contrast to Dr. Suzuki's program
of opposition. The mere fact that Minseito leaders pressed
Mr. Machida, a Cabinet member, to accept the presidency
of the party and finally persuaded him to become chairman
of the board of managers, showed that there had been no
material change in the Minseito policy of upholding the
Government.
The remainder of Mr. Machida's speech contain-
ed no direct criticism of the Government.
While it is unlikely that the Seiyukai and the Minseito
will effectively coordinate their policies with regard to
national political issues, their rapprochement at this
time is a factor that might bring back the functioning of
normal constitutional Government through a more direct
and more effectual intervention by the parties in politi-
cal affairs. The joint announcement of the Seiyukai and
Minseito regarding the recently arrived at agreement
stated

"We part with Mr. Takahashi in accordance
with the basic doctrine of the Seiyukai."
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

was prepared by Mr. Neville (Counselor of

I would suggest that your reading begin
with page 4. Mr. Neville emphasizes the
strength of nationalist sentiment in Japan.
He says that the fashion of nationalism is
one to which the Japanese "take kindly". The
Japanese "are persuaded that these are emer-
gency times"; "the country is on an emergency
footing . . ."; he suggests that it is unlikely
that this tenseness will continue indefinitely;
but, "either the present tension would ease,
or there would be a violent alteration in
the course of things -- a war, which seems
improbable at the moment . . . ." on this
point, I would comment that, while "improbable
at the moment", it is by no means impossible
that the momentum of the nationalist tide which
is running ever higher may carry Japan un-
expectedly and suddenly over the breakwater
of sound reasoning which stands in the way
of new manifestations of superpatriotism --
the surmounting or breaking down of which
would carry Japan into war)
